callable occurrence, or the current own/dynamic C value of that binding yields a
callable occurrence at runtime. In the latter case the expected call-site
signature is known during lowering and the selected occurrence's method record
must validate exactly before the typed call.

5.3. Expected Field Positions and Anonymous Bodies

Parentheses create ordinary bounded Structures. A bounded Structure is not executable merely because it is parenthesized.

A receiver signature assigns an expected role to each field. If a field position
is defined as a statement/body role, the receiver consumes the supplied
Structure as its own statement list or control tail. This is receiver-defined
translation/interpretation of Structure data; it does not create a separate
anonymous-function syntax.

If a field position expects a named function, subroutine or receiver reference,
the supplied name must resolve through compiler state or an explicit structural
path to a callable Lmx occurrence. A bounded
Structure supplied in argument position remains Structure data unless the active
receiver explicitly consumes it under its own rule.

If a field position expects a primitive/scalar value and the source supplies a bounded Structure that is not already a direct primitive/scalar value, the receiver consumes that Structure as a value producer for the expected type. An operator-consumed Structure is the usual compact case of this rule.

If the expected type is boolean, the value producer must produce a boolean value. If the expected type is numeric or another primitive/scalar type, the value producer must produce that type or be convertible according to the active profile.

Example:

for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++
    print: i


The `for` receiver consumes:

    field0 -> initialization Structure
    field1 -> boolean-producing condition expression
    field2 -> iteration-step statement Structure
    body   -> vertical statement Structure consumed by `for`

Compact body example:

    for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++ print(i)

Here `print(i)` is the last argument Structure of `for`. The `for` receiver
signature names that position as its statement tail and lowers it as the loop
body. The compact body example uses only compact Frames and contains no inline
short colon-form.

5.4. Example: Profile-Level Branch Receiver

For a computational profile-level branch receiver, the receiver may define fields:

    condition
    first
    second

and consume them as:

    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    first     -> statement Structure
    second    -> statement Structure

During translation, the receiver lowers these fields into target control flow. During interpretation, the receiver evaluates the condition and then interprets only the selected body. This example illustrates receiver-signature field assignment; the concrete L2/L3 `if` control receiver is described in chapter 19 and may use a separate sibling `else` receiver.

5.5. Example: `fn`

For an `fn` definition receiver, fields may be consumed as:

    name -> function name
    args -> argument Structure
    ret  -> return Structure
    body -> function statement Structure

The receiver creates a callable Lmx child occurrence and causes its real C
method body to be emitted under a globally unique symbol. The `fn` Frame itself
is not a function call.

5.6. Same Shape, Different Receivers

The same surface shape can have different semantics under different receivers.

Example:

    (a, b)

As a function argument Structure, it may mean two positional arguments.

As a return layout Structure, it may mean two return fields.

As a model fragment, it may mean a structural model.

As query input, it may mean a tuple of terms.

The receiver defines the interpretation.


6. Operator Tokens and Expression Consumption

Mathematics is a core runtime domain because expressions, numeric models,
arrays, queries, reductions, constraints, validation, scheduling metrics,
build rules and diagnostics all rely on typed computation.

The core parser builds Structures first. Operator tokens and compact
operator-looking spelling chunks stay in the same structural pipeline: P0
tokenization produces ordinary value/name fields and operator-token fields,
then receiver/operator profiles consume those fields according to context.

6.1. One Structural Parser and Operator Tokens

Lingvamyxa has one core structural parsing system. It builds ordinary receiver
Frames, bounded Structures, value fields and operator-token fields.

Operator spellings such as the following are not valid bare source identifiers
in the portable profile:

    =
    !=
    <
    >
    <=
    >=
    +
    -
    *
    /

When such a spelling is whitespace-bounded, the parser can classify it
immediately as an operator token. Because it is not an ordinary source
identifier, it does not require `:` and it is not parsed as a head/frame form.

Example source:

    i = 1

Core structural form:

    (i, =, 1)

Here `i` and `1` are value fields. The token `=` is an operator field. It is
equality/comparison for expression/operator consumers, not assignment and not
native named-field syntax.

Example inside a receiver frame:

    print: 1 + 2

Core structural form:

    print(1, +, 2)

Because there is no colon after `1`, `1` is a value field, not a receiver/head.
The `+` operator may consume the neighboring values supplied by the
Structure.

6.2. Compact Spellings and P0 Token Boundaries

The core parser always accepts whitespace-bounded operator spellings. With explicit whitespace:

    print: 1 + 2

normalizes directly as:

    print(1, +, 2)

A compact spelling such as:

    print: 1+2

contains the compact non-quoted chunk `1+2`. Because `+` is not valid inside a
portable bare identifier, P0 inserts token boundaries around the operator
spelling and produces the same structural form:

    print(1, +, 2)

The same rule applies to compact sequences such as `a+b*c==d` and `arr[i+1]`:
they become ordinary field sequences containing value/name fields and
operator-token fields.

Exact quoted identifiers are never split this way. For example:

    `1+2`
    `a+b`

remain exact identifier/name values.

6.3. Expression Structures Are Data Before Consumption

An expression-like Structure is data until a receiver consumes it as an
expression.

For example, the `if` receiver consumes its `condition` field as an expression. A
configuration receiver might instead store the same Structure as data.

When consumed in a primitive/scalar field position, an expression-like Structure
is a value producer for that expected type. A comparison or logical operator
structure may satisfy a boolean field. A numeric operator structure may satisfy a
numeric field, subject to the active profile's conversion rules.

The important semantic rule is that operator-token consumption produces ordinary
Structures or typed semantic Structures; it is not an ordinary runtime action.

The bootstrap expression profile keeps expression handling inside ordinary P0
tokenization. P0 produces ordinary token fields and registered
receiver/operator consumption works over those fields.

For the ANSI C projection, arithmetic, comparison and boolean token sequences
keep ANSI C precedence and associativity. L2/L3 profiles that lower to ANSI C
use the same projection rule; they may add semantic checks around an expression,
but they do not need to reorder primitive C-like expressions.

Backends that do not target ANSI C may define their own lowering for the same
token Structures. Such a backend-specific rule belongs to that backend profile,
not to the source syntax.

6.4. Registered Operator Forms

An operator profile may recognize token sequences as registered operator forms
before ordinary receiver consumption. This document does not define a final
operator set for increment, decrement or similar forms, but it defines the
expected source-semantic shape for such operator methods.

For example, if a profile registers prefix decrement:

    --i

P0 tokenization produces the operator-token field `--` and the source target
name `i`. The operator profile may consume that sequence as:

    decBefore("i")

For a bare `i` the operator method resolves the current activation value: own
declared/local value first, then inherited dynamic value, then the immediate
`node\[0]i` fallback. It may lower the operation as a C read-update-write
sequence. Updating an own node-backed working local marks it dirty; updating an
inherited/fallback copy changes only this activation. A direct graph mutation
must spell `node\i`, `reference\i` or an Array index explicitly. The ordinary
head/update form is:

    i: newValue

If `i: newValue` resolves as an activation-local assignment, no graph child is
directly replaced. An explicit structural receiver may separately define append
or child replacement and may apply `implements(new old Consumer)` with Consumer
the remaining known source. A backend may reuse physical storage only as an
unobservable optimization that preserves real ordered child occurrences. There
is no reified scope stack whose older binding is restored on return. If `i` resolves to a callable
occurrence that defines read/update consumption, the sequence may lower to
generated temporary code that invokes that occurrence through its exact
`{addr,sig}` record with `callable->node`, declared actuals and dynamic values.
An addressable field/index target mutates the selected graph/storage location
and does not append a runtime name binding.

The exact operator methods, temporary names, evaluation order, numeric rules and
diagnostics belong to the operator-consumption specification. The core point is
that operator methods do not introduce a separate assignment syntax; they
compose with the same structural resolution and head/update form as ordinary
source.

6.5. Basic Array Operations

There are two distinct arrays in the implementation: a typed service array of
descriptors and the actual language arrays described by its entries. The
service array's address range is known. An Array reference addresses an entry
in that range, so the runtime identifies its type by the reference address.

Each Array descriptor has exactly two values: length and a pointer to actual
element storage, schematically `{len, data}`. It has no Lmx `node`, short name,
child list, type tag or Structure schema. An Lmx field can hold the descriptor
reference in its own `data`; the field's header is not the Array descriptor.
Arguments and returns copy the descriptor reference under 21.4.

For every used Array type T the implementation provides a typed service pool,
schematically `all_array_of_T`. This is the same address-classification
algorithm as the primitive service arrays and `all_methods_array {addr,sig}`.
Names of these pools are illustrative substrate identifiers, not a runtime
source-name lookup mechanism. Pool storage and actual application-array
backing must not be confused. Mutable descriptors and backing belong to the
owning Message's one arena; globally available range classification does not
share those mutable cells between Messages.

A language Array's owning backing is a rectangular contiguous block. In
Russian: "ровный (прямоугольный) многомерный массив с единым непрерывным блоком
памяти". One-dimensional and multidimensional C-style arrays have the same
physical principle: one block of element cells. Rank and dimensions determine
ordinary C-style index calculation and do not expand the two-value descriptor.
For shape `[d0, d1, ..., dN-1]`, there are `d0 * d1 * ... * dN-1` cells
in row-major order, with the last index varying fastest. A direct C image
preserves dimensions, for example `T a[D0][D1]`, and the corresponding
pointer-to-array/decay rules. The service array of descriptors has its own,
unrelated size.

This backing invariant predates Revision 2. Text that reopened contiguous
versus another representation was an editorial regression. Nested `[]`
denotes logical dimensions, not independently allocated row objects.
Primitive elements do not acquire Lmx headers or containment parents.
Reference-valued elements form one contiguous block of references to separate
referents. A rank-one array of Array references is different from a rectangular
rank-N array of elements.

Descriptor terminology is domain-specific. The prohibited descriptor is an
implicit Structure schema prefix or per-source-name C layout used to interpret
Structure fields. The required Array descriptor is the two-value length/data
entry in a typed service array. The required method descriptor is `{addr,sig}`.
These records do not restore a Structure schema or a runtime Namespace.
Optional program descriptions remain ordinary explicit Structures.

Remaining implementation work includes exact C encoding/alignment of the two
values, allocation/registration of service-array ranges and optional view/
resize contracts. It does not reopen the two-value descriptor, address-derived
type, or contiguous rectangular backing. A live descriptor reference must stay
valid: expanding/reorganizing a service pool requires stable entry addresses
or a complete reference-rewrite mechanism. This is separate from reallocating
the language array's element backing and updating its descriptor data pointer.

A view may share backing when its selected operation contract defines indexing
and lifetime. It does not imply separate row allocations or extra fields in
every descriptor. Existing optional view operations are logical requirements;
their adapters must respect the selected two-value reference representation.
Copying an Array reference does not clone elements. Indexed mutation acts on
the referenced storage, while assigning another reference to a callee-local
variable changes only that variable. Resize follows the same distinction.

The constructor receiver is `[]`. Runtime construction creates descriptor/
backing and returns the Array reference. An Lmx field that stores it is a
separate occurrence. Translation alone does not construct a static Array.

Examples:

[]: values
. 1
. 2
. 3
end: values

[]: matrix
. []: row
.. 1
.. 2
.. 3
. []: row
.. 4
.. 5
.. 6
end: matrix

The matrix has rank 2 and shape `[2, 3]`, with one contiguous backing. This
shape describes its elements, not the service descriptor pool. The historical
hosted `T **` allocation of separate rows is not a conforming layout for
this rectangle.

6.5.1. Indexing

Indexing selects an element or a subview.

For a rank-N array, a full index has N integer coordinates.

Example:

matrix[0, 2]

A partial index may produce a view rather than a copy.

Example:

matrix[0]

may produce the first row as a rank-1 array view.

Ordinary array indexing is checked at both source levels. L2 may separately
allow unchecked indexing of a raw typed address; that is not ordinary array-domain
indexing. L3 cannot use the raw-address operation.

6.5.2. Views and Copies

Array operations may produce either a view or a copy.

A view may share backing in the same Message arena; its operation/adapter must
retain the indexing and lifetime contract without adding fields to every Array
descriptor. A copy uses distinct backing in that arena. Neither creates a new
arena or a raw mutable cross-Message pointer.

Examples of view-producing operations:

slice
reshape, when compatible with storage layout
transpose
broadcast
partial indexing

Examples of copy-producing operations:

explicit clone/copy
materialize view
result of elementwise arithmetic unless assigned into an existing output buffer

The exact representation, alias relation and retention/reachability policy of a
storage-sharing view are unresolved with the Array ABI. Its mutable backing and
metadata nevertheless remain in the one owning Message arena. A selected design
must make sharing, retention and copying explicit and compatible with Lmx
identity and the end-turn collection rules.
Ordinary `return` must not hide an allocation or copy merely to repair an
otherwise invalid lifetime.

6.5.3. Shape

shape(array) returns the array dimensions as a rank-1 integer array or shape
Structure.

rank(array) returns the number of dimensions.

length(array) returns the size of the first dimension for rank greater than zero.

size(array) returns the total number of elements:

size = d0 * d1 * ... * dN-1

6.5.4. Reshape

reshape changes logical shape metadata without changing element values.

reshape is valid only if the total element count remains unchanged, unless the
operation is explicitly defined as a copy/fill/truncate operation.

Example:

reshape(values, shape = [3, 1])

A reshape may return a view if the underlying storage layout permits it.
Otherwise it may materialize a copy.

6.5.5. Transpose and Permute

transpose(array) swaps the two last dimensions for rank >= 2, or follows the
profile-defined matrix convention.

permute(array, axes) returns a view with reordered axes when possible.

Example:

permute(tensor, axes = [1, 0, 2])

These operations normally modify shape and stride metadata only. They do not
copy data unless materialization is requested or required by the target backend.

6.5.6. Elementwise Operations

Arithmetic and comparison operators over arrays are elementwise by default.

Examples:

array + array
array - array
array * array
array / array
array == array
array < array

For numeric arrays, the element operation is selected from registered payload
domains and any explicit contract/profile data required by that operation.

Example:

+(array<i64>, array<i64>)
  -> elementwise i64 addition

+(array<decimal>, array<decimal>)
  -> elementwise decimal addition using the decimal backend

Elementwise operations return an array whose shape is derived from the operand
shapes by the broadcasting rules.

6.5.7. Broadcasting

Broadcasting is a shape-compatibility rule for elementwise operations.

It does not require copying data.

Two shapes are broadcast-compatible if, after aligning dimensions from the
right, every corresponding dimension pair satisfies one of the following:

1. the dimensions are equal;
2. one dimension is 1;
3. one side has no dimension there and is treated as dimension 1.

The result dimension is the maximum of the two dimensions.

Examples:

[3] + []
  -> result shape [3]

[2, 3] + [3]
  -> [2, 3] + [1, 3]
  -> result shape [2, 3]

[2, 3] + [2, 1]
  -> result shape [2, 3]

A broadcasted dimension may be represented as stride = 0 in an array view.
This means that the same element is read repeatedly along that dimension.

Example:

[1, 2, 3] + 4

is semantically equivalent to:

[1 + 4, 2 + 4, 3 + 4]

but the scalar 4 is not copied into [4, 4, 4]. Its Lmx leaf is read directly
under a virtual rank-0 broadcast rule; an implementation may use
backend-private stride-0 iteration metadata internally.

6.5.8. Reduction

A reduction collapses one or more dimensions using an associative or explicitly
ordered operation.

Examples:

reduce(+, values)
reduce(+, matrix, axis = 0)
reduce(max, matrix, axis = 1)

If no axis is provided, the reduction applies to all elements and returns an
Lmx scalar leaf by default. An explicit operation/profile may instead request a
logical rank-0 array result; its descriptor and storage follow 6.5.

If an axis is provided, only that axis is collapsed.

The operation must define:

identity value, if required;
element operation;
result type;
ordering guarantees.

For floating/real/decimal values, deterministic profiles should define stable
reduction order to avoid profile-dependent rounding differences.

6.5.9. Scan

scan is a prefix reduction.

Example:

scan(+, [1, 2, 3, 4])
  -> [1, 3, 6, 10]

scan may be inclusive or exclusive according to the explicitly selected
operation/profile.

6.5.10. Map

map applies a function/procedure to every element.

Example:

map(sin, values)

For pure functions, map may be vectorized, parallelized or lowered to a backend
array operation.

For impure procedures, ordering and side effects must be explicit. The default
array map profile should require pure functions.

6.5.11. Filter

filter selects elements according to a boolean predicate.

Example:

filter(values, predicate = isPositive)

The result is normally a newly materialized logical array in the current
Message arena, because the selected elements are not guaranteed to form a
regular view. Its descriptor and contiguous result backing follow 6.5.

6.5.12. Concatenation

concat joins arrays along a selected axis.

Example:

concat(a, b, axis = 0)

All dimensions except the concatenation axis must match.

concat normally materializes a new logical array result in the current Message
arena. The operation selects its copying/sharing strategy within the descriptor
and rectangular-storage contract of 6.5.

6.5.13. Matrix and Tensor Operations

Elementwise multiplication and matrix multiplication are distinct operations.

The * operator remains elementwise for arrays.

Matrix multiplication, tensor contraction and dot products should be represented
by explicit receivers/operators such as:

matmul(a, b)
dot(a, b)
contract(a, b, axes = ...)
outer(a, b)

A profile may define a symbolic operator for matrix multiplication, but it must
not overload elementwise * ambiguously.

6.5.14. C99 Lowering

The service pool for each used Array type contains two-value descriptors
`{len, data}`. A language Array reference addresses a descriptor entry, and
the entry's data pointer addresses the actual element backing. Neither record
nor element cells are Lmx children. An Lmx field storing the descriptor address
retains its own ordinary Structure containment independently.

The owning backing is contiguous C-style rectangular storage. Its
multidimensional index/declarator information belongs to the array type and
indexing context, just as in C; it does not describe the service pool and does
not require extra descriptor fields. A direct C image can use
`T a[D0][D1]` and pointer-to-array/decay rules. Separately allocated row
pointers do not implement this rectangle.

Elementwise operations may lower to loops over the broadcast result shape.
Computed strides, including a zero stride for an input broadcast dimension,
may be temporary loop/indexing data; they are not extra members of every Array
descriptor. The result descriptor refers to the selected result backing.
Specialized loops, SIMD, BLAS or other explicit numeric backends must preserve
these reference, shape and mutation semantics.

6.5.15. Minimal Bootstrap Profile

The current hosted bootstrap may initially support only the following migration
subset of operations. That coverage limit is not permission to replace the
language invariant of one contiguous rectangular backing block:

rank-1 arrays;
rank-2 arrays;
contiguous owned storage;
checked indexing;
shape;
copy;
elementwise + - * /;
scalar-array broadcasting;
array-array elementwise operations with equal shape;
reduce over all elements.

Full broadcasting, slicing views, transpose, permute, scan, filter and tensor
contraction may be added later without changing these logical operations. Their
addition does not reopen contiguous-versus-noncontiguous storage and does not
select the remaining machine encoding or view-operation ABI.

6.6. Numerical Types and Built-In Mathematics

A high-level structural runtime without built-in mathematics is incomplete.

The default numeric model has two broad implementation families:

    native C99-backed numeric types;
    library-backed mathematical types.

Native C99-backed numeric domains are Lmx leaves whose payload addresses select
the numeric storage family. Their ordinary lowering may use C99 arithmetic and
C99 storage behind the Lmx boundary; this does not replace the persistent graph
value/occurrence with a bare C scalar. Declared/dynamic primitive inputs and an
unqualified used-own-field snapshot are nevertheless typed C activation values
under sections 7.4 and 21, with dirty-only publication where applicable.

Library-backed mathematical values are likewise Lmx leaves or Structures whose
operations are selected through compiler/profile resolution, an actual callable
Lmx occurrence or an explicit graph path, and lowered to selected backend
helpers. They do not require a runtime Namespace or attached type descriptor.

Default bootstrap map:

    char       -> C char
    schar      -> C signed char
    uchar      -> C unsigned char

    short      -> C short
    ushort     -> C unsigned short
    int        -> C int
    uint       -> C unsigned int
    long       -> C long
    ulong      -> C unsigned long
    llong      -> C long long
    ullong     -> C unsigned long long

    i/u        -> implementation-profile C integer family

    i8         -> C99 fixed-width or profile-defined 8-bit signed integer
    u8         -> C99 fixed-width or profile-defined 8-bit unsigned integer
    i16        -> C99 fixed-width or profile-defined 16-bit signed integer
    u16        -> C99 fixed-width or profile-defined 16-bit unsigned integer
    i32        -> C99 fixed-width or profile-defined 32-bit signed integer
    u32        -> C99 fixed-width or profile-defined 32-bit unsigned integer
    i64        -> C99 fixed-width or profile-defined 64-bit signed integer
    u64        -> C99 fixed-width or profile-defined 64-bit unsigned integer

    isize      -> profile-defined pointer-sized signed integer
    usize      -> profile-defined pointer-sized unsigned integer

    bool       -> C int / C99 _Bool / stdbool bool according to profile

    f32        -> C float
    f64        -> C double
    flong      -> C long double

    bigint     -> GMP mpz_t
    real       -> MPFR mpfr_t
    decimal    -> decNumber

The fixed-width aliases are valid only when the selected C99/runtime profile can
provide the corresponding width. If a target cannot provide an exact-width
storage type, the profile must reject that alias or define an explicit emulation
backend.

Fixed-width C spellings are projection aliases, not the semantic foundation of
LMX integers. An explicitly referenced numeric-description Structure may
describe value family, range, representation, ABI boundary and serialization
contract. A selected projection profile then chooses whether the payload lowers
through `int`, `size_t`, `uint32_t`, a library-backed value, a checked wrapper
or another admitted storage spelling. Such a description is ordinary data and
is never attached to every numeric node.

Numeric descriptions should keep semantic range, representation, ABI projection
and serialization contracts distinct. A backend profile may store projection
or validation mappings such as `printf.format` in an explicit Table, but those
rows are configuration data and do not declare numeric names. A type may be
semantically compatible even when the selected backend cannot lower it; that is
a projection failure, not a parser or Registry fact.

Contextual literals are explicitly installed compiler/profile operations or
ordinary description Structures, not parser keywords and not facts activated
by an arbitrary Table. An operation may use an explicit mapping Table internally
as described in section 19.18.2.

Formatted-output mappings may likewise be stored in an explicitly supplied
Table. An explicitly selected validator operation may consume
`printf.format` for recommended class-to-spelling projection and a separate
`printf.argument.class` relation for spelling-to-expected-argument-class
validation. The Tables do not bind the validator. For example, `%zu` may map to
`size_t` and `%lu` may map to `ulong`; passing a known `size_t` value to a `%lu`
slot is then a validation failure in the selected C99 projection profile, not a
style warning.

C99 undefined-behavior policy is not a global promise that no generated program
can ever enter a bad state. It is ordinary profile validation vocabulary. A
profile may declare rows such as `c99.ub.policy[c99.format-mismatch] =
diagnostic`, `c99.ub.policy[c99.null-dereference] = diagnostic` or
`c99.ub.policy[c99.signed-overflow] = profile-defined`. An explicitly declared
validator may reference a supplied `profile.validator` Table. The Table itself
binds nothing. The C99 projection profile must not use
undefined behavior as a semantic mechanism; known dangerous projection patterns
are rejected, guarded, checked or explicitly delegated to a profile rule.

The `i/u` family denotes implementation-profile integer types over C integer
storage. A concrete profile may define names such as `i`, `u`, `i32`, `u64`,
`isize` and `usize` as separate type constructors or as aliases over a smaller
primitive set.

The special mathematical types are not primitive C values:

    bigint:
        exact integer arithmetic backed by GMP `mpz_t`;

    real:
        arbitrary-precision or profile-defined high-precision real arithmetic
        backed by MPFR `mpfr_t`;

    decimal:
        decimal arithmetic backed by decNumber and a decimal context.

`bigint`, `real` and `decimal` are ordinary source names resolved to constructors,
operations or description Structures when the selected profile explicitly
installs them. Their physical
representation and linked libraries belong to the selected L1/runtime profile.

6.6.1. Mathematics Uses Structural Operation Resolution

Operator-token syntax does not directly call C symbols.

Operator-token fields and compact operator spellings produce ordinary
Structures. A receiver that expects an expression later consumes that expression
Structure. During that consumption, operators and functions resolve through
compiler/profile state, an actual callable Lmx occurrence or an explicit graph
path. No call pushes or merges a runtime Namespace.

Canonical pipeline:

    source structure
        -> operator-consumption Structure
        -> numeric operator/function resolution
        -> typed numeric semantic Structure
        -> interpretation or lowering

Example:

    sqrt(x) + y

Conceptual resolution:

    resolve the `sqrt` callable occurrence/operation
    resolve the `+` callable occurrence/operation
    inspect explicit operand contracts only when the selected operation requires them
    lower to L1/backend calls if the profile allows it

The observable Lingvamyxa semantics are defined by the selected Lmx operations
and explicit data contracts, not by accidental C symbol spelling. Invoking a
generated Lingvamyxa method calls the typed entry from its
`all_methods_array {addr,sig}` record. The first service value is always
`callable->node`; declared actuals and the hidden dynamic values named by `sig`
follow in deterministic typed order. It passes no descriptor, closure
environment, arena, MessageThread or reified Namespace parameter. Backend helper
functions called inside that method may use their native C signatures. Every
outbound source call is a dirty-only checkpoint. A runtime/foreign operation is
the same boundary when it can expose or control graph state outside the current
activation, publish it, call back into LMX or otherwise re-enter user code. A
private, non-escaping helper used only for this activation's own traversal,
classification or explicit graph operation is not such a boundary merely
because the backend outlined it as C.

6.6.2. Native C99 Numeric Operations

Native C99-backed numeric types may lower to direct C99 operations when the
active profile defines the overflow, conversion, comparison and evaluation
rules.

Examples:

    i32 + i32        -> i32 or promoted profile-defined integer result
    u64 * u64        -> u64 or checked/profile-defined result
    f64 / f64        -> f64
    bool && bool     -> bool

The operator/numeric specification must define or reference the active profile's
rules for:

    integer overflow;
    signed/unsigned mixing;
    integer promotion;
    floating conversion;
    comparison;
    boolean conversion;
    rounding;
    division by zero;
    NaN and infinity handling for floating values.

A deterministic profile should avoid silently inheriting target-dependent C
behavior when that behavior would make Lingvamyxa results platform-dependent.

6.6.3. Library-Backed Numeric Operations

Library-backed numeric operations are normal registered operations.

Example source:

    a + b

If `a` and `b` are `bigint`, the `+` operator resolves to the accepted
`bigint` addition operation and may lower to GMP.

If `a` and `b` are `real`, the `+` operator resolves to the accepted `real`
addition operation and may lower to MPFR using the selected real context.

If `a` and `b` are `decimal`, the `+` operator resolves to the accepted
`decimal` addition operation and may lower to decNumber using the selected
decimal context.

Conceptual examples:

    +(bigint, bigint)  -> bigint
    +(real, real)      -> real
    +(decimal, decimal)-> decimal

The operator spelling is not tied to one backend function. Compiler/profile
resolution or an explicitly reached callable occurrence selects an admitted
implementation according to explicitly available operand contracts, precision
context, rounding policy, target profile and optimization profile.

6.6.4. Destination-First Backend Procedures

Many mathematical C libraries expose procedures rather than value-returning
functions.

Example backend style:

    result is stored in the first argument;
    operands are read from following arguments;
    rounding/context/status may be additional arguments;
    the C return value may be `void`, `int` status or diagnostic metadata.

Such a C function can still implement a Lingvamyxa value-producing mathematical
operation.

Example conceptual wrapper:

    C backend:
        void mpz_add(mpz_t rop, const mpz_t op1, const mpz_t op2)

    Lingvamyxa operation:
        +(bigint, bigint) -> bigint

Lowering concept:

    allocate or reuse result payload storage under the selected runtime ABI;
    initialize backend value if required;
    call backend destination-first function;
    return the Lingvamyxa numeric value.

Therefore C `void` does not mean "not mathematical". It may mean "the value is
written into a destination argument". Lingvamyxa exposes the value operation,
not necessarily the raw C procedure shape.

6.6.5. Return Models

Every high-level external wrapper operation must expose an explicit return
contract as ordinary Structure data or profile metadata.

Return models include:

    ret: T
        value-producing function;

    ret: Unit
        effect-only function corresponding to C `void`;

    ret: Status
        status-only function;

    ret: Status T
        status/value pair or profile-defined checked result;

    ret: destinationArg(N) T
        destination-first backend procedure that produces a Lingvamyxa value;

    ret: pointer / handle / borrowed / owned
        unsafe or explicitly managed low-level result.

Only value-producing functions may appear in value-required expression
positions.

Valid:

    x = sqrt(y)

Invalid in a pure value-required expression/operator context:

    x = Lingvamyxa\GMP\raw\mpz_clear(y)

because raw clear is an effect/release operation, not a pure value.

A `Unit` function may be callable as a statement-level receiver when its
explicit operation contract allows that level and effect profile.

6.6.6. L2 Universal L1 Adapter and Checked Structural Wrappers

L1 / ANSI C symbols are not automatically high-level Lingvamyxa functions.

The level boundary is:

    L2
        may expose a universal adapter to the selected L1 / ANSI C profile.
        Raw C functions, raw pointers, raw storage, C headers and platform
        calls belong here. This adapter is governed by the L2/L1 build profile,
        not by hidden high-level wrapper metadata.

    L3
        may not call raw L1 / ANSI C symbols directly. They may call only
        Lingvamyxa receivers, functions, methods, wrappers, handles or
        capabilities whose explicit Structures/operations define the needed
        contract.

The L2 universal ANSI C adapter does not read attached wrapper metadata
instructions in order to assign semantic meaning to raw L2 code. Its inputs are the
L2 source and the L2/L1 build profile:

    target compiler;
    target ABI;
    calling convention;
    include paths;
    headers;
    defines;
    compiler flags;
    linker inputs;
    platform profile.

The build profile itself may be described by a direct L2 build/configuration
Structure. In that role the receiver supplies mechanical toolchain inputs that
will be passed to the compiler/linker; it does not assign high-level meaning to
C functions.

This is intentionally close to an ordinary C compilation environment. The same
L2 source should not acquire a different raw C meaning because unrelated Table
or Registry data changed.

For raw C-visible names, L2 uses an explicit C-surface spelling:

    c.name

`c.name` is a reserved L2 spelling form. It is not Structure field access, not
`/` source-path access, not `\` typed field-follow, and not high-level graph
lookup. It denotes the C spelling `name` as seen by the active L2/L1
translation unit after the build profile's headers, defines and toolchain
inputs have been applied. This is the canonical and permanent L2 C-surface
escape; it is not a Lingvamyxa runtime Namespace and not a temporary bootstrap shortcut.

Exact registered receiver heads take precedence over this generic copying rule
in receiver/head position. Thus `c.struct:`, `c.union:`, `c.enum:` and
`c.array:` are consumed by their registered L2 receiver semantics rather than
being copied as calls or updates of C identifiers named `struct`, `union`,
`enum` or `array`. A `c.*` spelling for which no exact receiver applies remains
the generic C-surface escape described here. This precedence is contextual and
does not make `c` a runtime Namespace.

A dotted continuation after the explicit C door remains in the C surface:

    c.tree.branch

lowers to the ANSI C value-field spelling:

---ANSI C---
tree.branch
---ANSI C---

This is intentionally separate from ordinary Lingvamyxa field-follow. For an
ordinary Lmx referent, `x\field` enumerates current children and selects the
ShortNameId/occurrence described by sections 13 and 21; it does not lower to a
source-name-specific C member. Only an explicitly typed raw-L2 machine/foreign
referent may define `x\field` as a direct `x->field` projection. `c.x.field` is
always the explicit direct C value-structure/member spelling. In head/update
position, `c.x.field: value` lowers to `x.field = value`.
Dotted field spelling without the explicit `c.` door is not Lingvamyxa field
access. Ordinary L2 Structures use `\` for typed field-follow; any dotted
value/member path belongs to the explicit C surface and must be spelled through
`c.`.

For convenience, the L2 parser treats a compact token that starts with `c.` as
one C-surface token up to the next Lingvamyxa field boundary, such as horizontal
whitespace, a physical line break, comma/semicolon, `(`, `)`, `:`, a line
comment start, or the end of the current source item. The L2 lowering then drops the `c.`
prefix and copies the remaining ANSI C spelling directly into the generated C
code. For example:

L2:
    c.boxes[1].value: 7
    return: c.boxes[1].value != 7

may lower as:

---ANSI C---
boxes[1].value = 7;
return boxes[1].value != 7;
---ANSI C---

This is an intentional low-level L2 C-surface escape. It does not introduce
Lingvamyxa dot syntax or Lingvamyxa array/member semantics outside the explicit
`c.` door; the copied spelling is checked by the selected ANSI C toolchain.

The `c.` prefix may be used directly in C call or value position. Primitive
L2/C-profile type receivers such as `int`, `char`, `size_t`, `u32`, `i32` and
`FILE` are ordinary bare L2 type spellings and do not use `c.`:

L2:
    fn: main () int
        c.printf: "Hello C\n"
        c.printf: "%s\n" "message"
        size_t: n c.strlen("abc")
        FILE: file
        c.puts: "Hello C"
        return: 0
    end: main
end: L2

Here `c.printf`, `c.strlen`, `size_t`, `FILE` and `c.puts` do not need
attached Lingvamyxa descriptors for raw L2 use. They become valid only if the L2/L1
build profile makes the corresponding C spelling visible or otherwise accepted
in that C grammar position, for example through `#include <stdio.h>`, `#include
<stddef.h>` or another selected header. The C compiler, preprocessor and linker
validate whether each spelling is actually a function, object, typedef, enum
constant, object-like macro or function-like macro in that position. Bare
primitive type receivers are checked by the L2 profile before ANSI C lowering.

The same source may also call ordinary Lingvamyxa names such as `println` when
compiler/profile resolution or an explicit structural path selects the
corresponding callable occurrence, for example from a prelude/core `sub`
declaration. Such names are language names, not
C-visible identifiers.

A bare unresolved L2 head or atom such as `printf` is not automatically treated
as a raw C fallback. Direct C-visible access is explicit through `c.printf`.
Bare primitive type receivers such as `size_t` or `int` are ordinary L2
receivers. A bare Lingvamyxa name is not an implicit C fallback; after
resolution it is an ordinary Lingvamyxa `fn`, `sub`, label, variable or
registered receiver, and that resolved operation may choose to lower to C internally.

The `c.` spelling covers raw C-visible names, C preprocessor-like spellings,
object-like macros, function-like macros and explicit C value-field paths in
the selected L2 profile. It does not cover primitive type receivers, C tag
names as tags, labels or declarator syntax. Those are provided by the minimal L2
structural bridge, by selected profile-specific L2 forms, or by an explicit raw
L1 text escape.

L2 storage/address/index forms are Lingvamyxa structural spellings; the C base
type words used inside them are ordinary bare primitive/type receivers:

    int: x
    char: ch
    @: int p
    @@: char pp
    []: int values 16
    \p
    p\field

Thus L2 may have many C-visible identifiers through headers, but the structural
spelling of pointers, arrays, raw loads and field-follow remains Lingvamyxa
syntax.

Explicit ordinary Structures may describe high-level wrappers, constants,
numeric backends, type mappings, ownership rules, borrow rules, effect rules and
operator implementations. Optional authorization profiles may add capability
requirements. They are consumed only through an explicit reference/operation;
they are not attached to values or searched through a runtime Namespace.

The high-level structural-wrapper family does not declare every C function for L2. A
selected L2 universal ANSI C adapter may discover or bind raw symbols from
headers, toolchain metadata, build metadata or explicit L2 adapter syntax such
as `c.name`. Those raw symbols remain L2-only unless a high-level Lingvamyxa
wrapper is explicitly declared and resolved to a callable Lmx occurrence.

Only the last-resort `C:` receiver admits raw C text. Ordinary implementation
uses structural L1. The escape boundary, distinct from C-name lookup, is:

C:
===
printf("Hello C\n");
===

Raw text follows the string/block rules. Source-level decrease may close the
`C` frame without an explicit `end: C`.

No special mechanism for making C headers visible to mathematics is required.
Mathematics and ordinary L3 code see high-level Lingvamyxa operations and
explicit contract Structures rather than raw foreign-substrate symbols.

Canonical rule:

    L2 source + L2/L1 build profile define raw L1 toolchain visibility;
    L2 build receivers provide compiler/linker configuration data;
    explicit ordinary Structures may define high-level contracts;
    ordinary program code may inspect or validate those Structures;
    declarations/imports update compiler resolution and runtime graph builders;
    expression/operator resolution selects real callable occurrences/operations.

A system message may propose a candidate description Structure at runtime. It
affects a graph only when an explicitly resolved ordinary operation links or
copies it. The
language does not prescribe an authorized source list or mandatory validation
policy; a program may call its own validation code before binding. A high-level
description does not by itself rewrite raw L2 translator machinery.

6.6.7. L2 Raw Adapter and Checked Wrapper Example

A raw L1 function does not need a checked semantic wrapper declaration merely
to be used by an L2 universal adapter. L2 is the low-level adapter boundary. Headers,
include paths and link inputs are direct L2 build-profile data. They are passed
mechanically to the toolchain rather than used as high-level receiver/contract
policy.

Using MPFR from L2 may therefore be as direct as ordinary C-profile use, if the
L2/L1 build profile provides `mpfr.h` and the MPFR link inputs.

This does not make `mpfr_sqrt` callable from ordinary expression/operator consumption
or L3 source.

A high-level value-producing wrapper may be registered separately.

Example conceptual wrapper Structure on a direct `.lm2` module surface:

externFn: Lingvamyxa\MPFR\sqrt
    sourceLevel: L1
    sourceName: mpfr_sqrt
    header: `mpfr.h`
    pkg: `PkgConfig::mpfr`
    args:
        real: x
    ret:
        real
    backendShape:
        destinationArg: 0
        operandArg: 1
        roundingArg: activeRealContext\rounding
        statusReturn: int
    effects:
        pureWrapper
    context:
        activeRealContext
    implementation:
        Lingvamyxa\MPFR\raw\mpfr_sqrt
    callableFrom: L2 L3
---

This ordinary wrapper Structure identifies the high-level callable occurrence
that L3 code calls and records explicit result, ownership, context and effect
contracts. It is not attached to the callable/value and is not a second object
header. Its generated Lingvamyxa entry uses the exact `{addr,sig}` contract: the
first argument is `callable->node`, followed by the declared actual `x` and any
ordered dynamic values required by the wrapper. The native multi-argument
`mpfr_sqrt` call occurs only inside that entry as a backend-helper call and does
not define the source-method ABI.

Expression use:

    y = sqrt(x)

Conceptual lowering:

    allocate result payload under the selected runtime ABI;
    initialize MPFR storage according to activeRealContext;
    call mpfr_sqrt(result\storage, x\storage, activeRealContext\rounding);
    record status/exactness according to profile;
    return result.

6.6.8. Structural Calls Outside Pure Expressions

High-level external wrappers may also be called through ordinary structural path
receiver/call syntax when their explicit operation contracts allow it.

Example high-level structural-path call:

    Lingvamyxa\MPFR\sqrt: x

Example raw/runtime-only call through the L2 adapter:

    Lingvamyxa\MPFR\raw\mpfr_sqrt: rop op rnd

Functions that return `Unit` or perform effects should normally be exposed as
structural calls or explicit receivers, not as pure expression
functions.

Examples:

    Lingvamyxa\GMP\init: x
    Lingvamyxa\GMP\clear: x
    Lingvamyxa\Decimal\setPrecision: ctx 34
    Lingvamyxa\Real\setRounding: ctx nearest

High-level numeric code should not normally call backend initialization and
clear functions directly. The selected payload-domain runtime should initialize,
retain and clear backend storage according to the explicit lifetime contract.

6.6.9. Pure Expression Profile

The default expression/operator profile is pure.

A pure expression may call only functions registered as pure value functions or
pure wrappers.

Allowed examples:

    sqrt(x)
    sin(x)
    cos(x)
    pow(x, y)
    abs(x)
    gcd(a, b)
    roundDecimal(x)
    a + b
    a * b

There is no blacklist of forbidden C names in this profile. The rule is simpler:
raw L1 / ANSI C calls are not visible to L3 expression profiles at all. They
belong to the L2 universal adapter boundary.

Effectful operations may be visible in both L2 and L3 as high-level Lingvamyxa
receivers or wrappers. Declared effects determine whether they are available in
a pure-expression profile; optional capability checks belong to an
authorization profile, not to the L3 level.

Required rule:

    raw L1 / ANSI C symbol
        -> L2 adapter only;

    no high-level wrapper operation
        -> no high-level L3 operation;

    raw pointer-bearing interface
        -> L2 only; a separate high-level handle/receiver may be L2/L3;

    effectful operation
        -> not callable from pure expression profile.

6.6.10. Pointer and Memory Rule

An L1 function with raw pointer parameters or raw pointer return values is not a
high-level value-producing expression function and is not directly visible at L3.

At L2, the selected universal ANSI C adapter may call it according to the L2
memory profile.

At L3, it must be wrapped by a Lingvamyxa Structure/operation that hides the raw
representation and defines at least:

    ownership;
    borrow duration;
    null policy;
    bounds;
    element type;
    mutation effects;
    failure behavior;
    high-level result and effects.

Threading, visibility and capability fields may be required by selected
runtime, provider or authorization profiles, but are not intrinsic L3 gates.

Without such a wrapper, the function belongs to L2 adapter semantics only, not
to ordinary L3 expression or structural-call syntax.

6.6.11. Contexts for `real` and `decimal`

`real` and `decimal` operations use explicit or profile-selected numeric
contexts.

A `real` context may define:

    precision;
    rounding mode;
    status flags;
    trap policy;
    exactness handling.

A `decimal` context may define:

    precision;
    exponent limits;
    rounding mode;
    status flags;
    trap policy;
    quantization policy.

The selected context must be reachable as ordinary Lmx data from the containing
Structure, through an explicit graph link/path, or be fixed compiler/profile
configuration. It must not be a hidden lexical environment, TLS semantic slot
or extra generated source-method argument.

Examples:

    withRealContext: precision 256 rounding nearest
        y = sqrt(x)
    ---

    withDecimalContext: precision 34 rounding halfEven
        total = price * quantity
    ---

The exact context receiver names are profile-defined. The semantic rule is that
context-dependent numeric operations must not silently depend on hidden
platform state when the deterministic profile requires reproducible results.

6.6.12. Arrays and Numeric Backends

Array operations over library-backed numeric types use the same explicitly
selected scalar operations. Iteration uses the typed descriptor and element
layout of 6.5.

Examples:

    +(array<bigint>, array<bigint>)
        -> elementwise GMP-backed integer addition

    +(array<real>, array<real>)
        -> elementwise MPFR-backed real addition using activeRealContext

    +(array<decimal>, array<decimal>)
        -> elementwise decNumber-backed decimal addition using activeDecimalContext

Broadcasting, reduction, map and tensor operations do not introduce separate
numeric semantics. They repeatedly apply or lower the accepted scalar operation
over the derived array shape.

For floating, real and decimal values, deterministic profiles should define
stable reduction order or another explicit reproducibility rule.

6.6.13. Minimal Built-In Mathematics Profile

The minimal built-in mathematics profile should include:

    native integer arithmetic;
    native floating arithmetic;
    boolean operations;
    comparisons;
    bigint construction and arithmetic;
    real construction and arithmetic;
    decimal construction and arithmetic;
    numeric conversions with explicit loss/rounding policy;
    selected pure functions such as sqrt, abs, min, max and pow;
    array elementwise arithmetic over supported numeric types;
    reduction over supported numeric arrays.

Additional functions may be introduced through explicit callable declarations
or imports and optional explicit policy validation.

The language core does not require every C library function to be visible.

The source-visible mathematical model is:

    typed numeric values;
    typed operators;
    callable Lmx occurrences and explicit operation contracts;
    explicit contexts;
    compiler-resolved or explicitly traversed external wrappers;
    high-level expression profiles;
    explicit low-level machine operations confined to L2.

7. `fn`, `sub` and Structural Closure

7.1. `fn` and `sub` Are Receivers

`fn` and `sub` are definition receivers. Each consumes the parsed definition
Structure and causes two connected artifacts to be emitted:

    a callable Lmx occurrence in the runtime Structure tree;
    one real, globally reachable C method body.

This rule applies recursively at every nesting depth. The module walker must
not emit only top-level executable definitions. A `fn` or `sub` nested in a
named Structure or another executable body is not metadata and is not a C
member declaration. Its body must be walked and emitted as an ordinary C
function at translation-unit level with a unique program-wide symbol.

Every generated source method has a typed signature whose first service input
is:

    Lmx *node

`node` is the containing Structure occurrence of the selected callable and is
always supplied as `callable->node`. The source word `node` is reserved: caller
code cannot shadow it, replace it with a caller binding or pass another value in
its position. A generated signature may additionally contain the declared call
ABI and hidden dynamic inputs required by unqualified free names:

    result-convention full_source_path_method(
        Lmx *node,
        declared-argument ABI...,
        hidden dynamic values...
    );

These extra values are the native C99 realization of source dynamic visibility,
not a reified Namespace, descriptor, closure environment, parser context or
Message Thread. The exact result convention remains a separate ABI decision.
Given selected occurrence `callable`, invocation obtains `{ addr, sig }` from
the physical class of `callable->data`, uses `sig` to construct the exact typed
argument list and cast `addr`, and supplies `callable->node` in the reserved
first position.

Parser and translator helpers may of course receive compiler contexts while
the program is being translated. Those helpers are not generated source
methods and do not define the runtime method ABI.

7.2. Function Definition Form

Example source:

fm: myFunc (int(i), int(j, 1), p1, p2) (int(r1), int(r2))
    print: i
    print: j
    p1()
    p2()
return: r1 r2

Strict conceptual form:

    fm(
      name(myFunc),
      args(
        int(i),
        int(j, 1),
        p1,
        p2
      ),
      ret(
        int(r1),
        int(r2)
      ),
      body(
        print(i),
        print(j),
        p1,
        p2,
        return(r1, r2)
      )
    )

Explicit nested-source form:

fm:
    name: myFunc
    args:
        int: i
        int: j 1
        p1
        p2
    ret:
        int: r1
        int: r2
    body:
        print: i
        print: j
        p1()
        p2()
        return: r1 r2
end: myFunc

The L1 receiver function registered as `fn` or `fm` receives the whole frame argument Structure as its first argument and consumes that Structure by receiver-defined argument positions. The explicit nested forms `name(...)`, `args(...)`, `ret(...)` and `body(...)` are ordinary Structures inside that first argument Structure. They are not `=` named fields.

The `fn`/`fm` frame's logical argument Structure is not the same thing as the formal parameter Structure of the function being defined. In the compact short definition profile, `fn` consumes its fields as:

    name -> field 0
    args -> field 1, required bounded formal argument Structure
    ret  -> field 2, required single-value return type
    body -> fields 3..end as the statement Structure consumed by fn/sub

The `fn` receiver returns one source-level value. The difference between `fn`
and `fm` is not primitive versus non-primitive. At a language call boundary a
Primitive has value semantics, while a Structure or Array has reference-value
semantics. Returning a reference does not clone its referent. The exact C result
channel is unresolved; this does not change the single source value or permit
implicit mutation of the caller's result binding.

The `fm` receiver uses the same field order, but field 2 is a return-description
Structure. That Structure explicitly declares the returned fields. `fm` is the
multiple-return form: the source result is one Lmx return Structure, and
consumers may treat it as one value or unpack/map it as a sequence of returned
values where their receiver signature admits it. The result may be an existing
Structure for which `implements(returnedValue declaredReturn returnConsumers)`
holds or one created before the return operation under the eventual result and
lifetime ABI. The exact C result/error channel remains unresolved; it must be
recorded in `sig` and used consistently by the typed call. It is distinct from
the reserved structural `node` argument and the hidden dynamic inputs. An
optimizer may eliminate only unobservable traffic while preserving Lmx
identity and the no-allocation-at-return rule of section 8.8.

Function definition is not function call syntax. The first compact `fn`/`sub`
field must be the declared name as its own field. A spelling with no separator
before the bounded argument Structure is parsed as a Frame/call-like Structure
and is syntactically correct P0 source, but it is a receiver-level semantic
error for `fn`:

    fn: test(body) int     # syntactically valid, semantically invalid for fn
                           # field 0 is Frame test(...), not name atom test
    fn: test (body) int    # valid shape: field 0 is name atom test, field 1 is args Structure

The `fn` receiver must not try to extract the declared function name from the
inner Frame `test(body)`. That Frame is ordinary source data produced by compact
call syntax. If the active receiver is `fn`, the declared function name is the
separate field consumed as the `name` argument.

Calls intentionally use the no-space callable form:

    test()
    test

Likewise, ordinary head/update consumption remains separate from call syntax:

    test: <int: 5>

Before lowering or interpreting a function body, `fn`/`sub` consumes the formal
argument Structure and validates the admitted actual values against it. The
selected `sig` defines their typed C calling convention. Primitive actuals are
copied by semantic value; Structure and Array actuals copy the reference value.
Rebinding a formal therefore never rebinds the caller's variable, while an
explicit field-follow or index operation mutates the referent. This creates no
runtime Namespace and no implicit argument copy-back. A direct call consumes
its actual argument Structure against the formal source shape.
Positional fields are assigned first in order. Once a named argument field is
encountered, all following actual fields in that call must be named
fields; the receiver/translating profile maps them by formal parameter name and
reports unknown, duplicate, missing or out-of-order arguments.

A named actual argument may carry a Structure body as its value:

    f( arg: 1 2 3 )

Here `arg` is a named actual field. The body `1 2 3` is not an anonymous wrapper
around the whole call argument list; it is the Structure value supplied to the
formal parameter named `arg`.

P0 does not know that a nested compact Frame is a function call. For example:

    print(1, 2, get(1,2))

is parsed as the `print` Frame with three fields; the third field is the nested
Structure/Frame `get(1,2)`. During L2 lowering, `get(1,2)` becomes a generated
call only if compiler resolution identifies a call-capable current activation
binding, explicit graph traversal selects an `fn`/`sub` callable occurrence or
registered receiver, or the current own/dynamic C value for `get` yields such an
occurrence at runtime. The dynamically selected record must have the exact
signature expected at this Consumer call site. If `get` is an ordinary data
occurrence, label or named Structure, the same source field is not a function
call. If it is unknown, the translator reports an unknown Lingvamyxa name unless
the source explicitly uses the permanent ANSI C door, such as `c.get(1,2)`.

Structures are never executed merely because they exist. A translator or
interpreter may lower a named Structure/Frame as a call only when that field is
in an executable/consumed position and compiler resolution has selected a
call-capable activation binding, or structural traversal/current activation has
yielded an `fn`, `sub` or registered receiver/operator occurrence with the exact
expected signature. Anonymous Structures and named Structures whose heads
resolve to non-callable variables, labels, records, arrays, tables or ordinary
data remain data until a receiver explicitly consumes them.

P0 does not know these receiver-specific meanings. It only builds the field
sequence. After receiver normalization, a valid `name` argument is privileged
visible metadata of the normalized Structure. Editors, highlighters and
translators may use that metadata to show the introduced name and to validate
`end: <target>` against the receiver-exposed close target. A receiver that
consumes no valid `name` argument still uses the general named-close default,
its own head spelling, unless it explicitly defines an anonymous/nameless close
form.

The formal argument field must be an argument-list Structure even when there is only one formal argument. Therefore:

    fn: factorial (int(n)) int

is valid, while:

    fn: factorial int(n) int

is invalid in the compact `fn` definition profile. The second form gives `fn` the declaration Frame `int(n)` directly where the `fn` receiver expects the bounded formal argument Structure that contains it. The empty formal argument list is written as `()`.

The `fn` return field is a single-value contract/name field, not a
return-description Structure. Write the explicit single-value contract admitted by the active
profile. Use `fm` when the source return itself is a Structure with explicitly
declared returned fields:

    fm: `split harvest` (PlantBed(bed))
        (
            f32: harvest
            f32: redApples
            f32: greenApples
        )
        return: bed\area 0 0
    end: `split harvest`

Example with no required formal parameters:

fn: words () iterator(Text)
    yield: "Hello"
    yield: "World"
end: words

Generic structural reading:

    fn(
      words,
      (),
      iterator(Text),
      yield("Hello"),
      yield("World")
    )

Receiver-level interpretation:

    name -> words
    args -> empty formal parameter Structure
    ret  -> iterator(Text)
    body -> yield("Hello"), yield("World")

The physical newline after `iterator(Text)` does not close the `fn` frame. The following `yield:` lines are at the implicit argument level of the `fn` frame, so they become following fields in the same field sequence. They are body fields only because the `fn` receiver consumes fields 3..end as executable body.

If an explicit anonymous Structure is wanted as one body field after the name,
formal-argument Structure and return-contract fields, write a delimiter:

fn: words () iterator(Text)
    ---
        yield: "Hello"
        yield: "World"
end: words

Generic structural reading:

    fn(
      words,
      (),
      iterator(Text),
      Structure(
        yield("Hello"),
        yield("World")
      )
    )

The names `int`, `boolean` and similar names are primitive/type-constructor
receivers when installed by compiler/profile resolution. In declaration context, a
type/constructor receiver consumes the following raw identifier or exact
identifier as a child name. If that name is already present at the selected
structural location, the form is a redeclaration and is admitted only by the
rule in section 7.3.1.

Compact definition form with vertical children inside the bounded argument list:

fn: i(
    args: int(v, j)
    ret: int
    body:
        j: v / 2
        return: v
)

This parses as a colon line Frame named `fn` whose first argument is a compact Frame named `i` with vertical child Structures. Parentheses do not disable source-level composition.

The `fn` receiver may define this as a compact definition form. In that form, the compact Frame name `i` becomes the function name, and the compact Frame children become definition child Structures.

Conceptual receiver-level interpretation:

    fn(
      name(i),
      args(
        int(v, j)
      ),
      ret(
        int
      ),
      body(
        j(v / 2),
        return(v)
      )
    )

The `args` child receives one compact declaration Frame:

    int(v, j)

In declaration context, this means an `int` child occurrence named `v` whose
default/initial value is `j`.

The same declaration may also be written with short forms inside bounded Structures:

    fn: i(args: int(v, j); ret: int; body: (j: v / 2; return: v))
    fn: i(args: (int: v j); ret: int; body: (j: v / 2; return: v))

In the first spelling, `args: int(v, j)` uses a compact declaration Frame as the `args` tail. In the second spelling, `args: (int: v j)` gives `args` a bounded Structure containing the short-form Frame `int(v, j)`. Inside `body: (j: v / 2; return: v)`, the semicolon terminates `j: v / 2`; `return: v` is the next short-form item in the same bounded Structure.

The form:

    args: int v j

is valid ordinary short-tail syntax and normalizes as:

    args(int, v, j)

It does not secretly create `args(int(v, j))`; that interpretation requires a compact Frame such as `args: int(v, j)` or a receiver-defined semantic equivalence.

Here `int(v, j)` remains the one `v` declaration with `j` as its
receiver-defined default/initial field; it does not declare a second formal
argument. `j: v / 2` therefore assigns the current activation value selected for
`j`. In this example `j` is inherited dynamically or initialized from
`node\[0]j`, so the assignment is private and has no write-back. Even if the
caller owns its own `j`, this assignment inside `i` does not dirty the caller's
slot. Only an assignment executed in an activation that selected `j` as its own
graph-backed field marks that activation's own slot dirty. The form never
appends a graph occurrence without an explicit structural receiver/path.
The final result is returned with `return: v`.

7.2.1. Bodyless `fn` Form (Revision-2 Status)

The syntax of a bodyless form such as

    fn: close () int

remains accepted by the parser. Revision 2 has not yet fixed whether this form
is an external method declaration, a structural callable contract, or another
ordinary data Structure. It must not be implemented by silently generating a
callable zero-returning body such as `lm_lmx_fn_File_close(void) { return 0; }`.
If a call reaches a declaration with no linked implementation, the translator
or runtime must issue the selected unresolved-method diagnostic. It must never
pretend that the source supplied an executable body.

When a bodyless declaration is explicitly linked to an implementation, its
callable Lmx occurrence identifies an immutable `all_methods_array {addr,sig}`
record like every other callable. It does not introduce a descriptor type or
runtime namespace binding. The exact external linking syntax and result ABI are
open Revision-2 items; the input signature is explicit metadata, not guessed
from a raw address.

Revision-1 implementations treated this form as a descriptor declaration and
combined it with lexical capture/closure-environment projection. That design is
retired and is retained only in repository history. It supplies no normative
algorithm for Revision 2; the unresolved items are exactly those listed above.

7.2.2. Returned Callable and Bound Structure State

In Revision 2 a returned callable is an Lmx occurrence (or an explicit graph
reference to one), and any state deliberately retained with it is represented
by ordinary Structure nodes and links. No semantic C closure struct or captured
runtime Namespace object is created. The C activation and its dynamic argument
copies are not captured automatically. A later invocation derives `node` from
the callable occurrence and dynamic values from its then-current caller.

Within one Message, returning, passing or rebinding a callable copies only its
ordinary Lmx reference value and retains/roots that exact callable occurrence;
it does not copy its containing subtree. Only an explicit graph-copy/merge
operation may create another occurrence. Crossing a Message boundary instead
uses the explicit copy/serialize/adopt rules of the Message transport. The exact
physical result/root carrier remains an unresolved ABI detail, but it may not
change this reference semantics or the occurrence's real `node` relationship.
If the program wants lexical/captured state, it must construct and link that
state explicitly; the compiler must not silently convert the current dynamic C
activation into a closure environment.

Because functions may return Lmx references and an occurrence may be callable,
a function may return a callable value. When ordinary Structure nodes linked
with that callable record already-bound values, evidence or other explicit
state, it may be viewed as a residual executable produced by partial evaluation.
The state is graph data reachable by the selected explicit links; it is not a
captured lexical environment.

For example, but only when the source explicitly constructs or links the
state, `makeAdder(10)` may build an ordinary residual Structure containing an
`x = 10` node and a callable occurrence. Such a result may be admitted by
`implements(residual AddX callAddLikeConsumer)` and represent the remaining
computation `y -> 10 + y`. Merely passing `10`, calling `makeAdder`, or returning
an existing callable does not materialize/capture `x` automatically. Partial
evaluation here means that explicit graph state records the already consumed,
resolved or bound part of the expression; it is never an implicit snapshot of
the caller's C activation.

Not every returned callable is partial evaluation. If a function simply returns
an already-existing callable with no specialization, no captured values and no
additional evidence, it is just a callable reference. The partial-evaluation
reading applies when the returned callable preserves some already-computed
graph state, bound argument, selected implementation link, validator evidence
or other specialized context.

Keep this distinct from the two other deferred/result mechanisms:

    lazy / yield
        delays production of sequence values. A consumer asks for one step at a
        time through an explicit state Structure, `each`, `awaitNext` or an
        equivalent receiver.

    returned callable / residual executable
        returns a callable occurrence/reference with linked Structure state that
        contains bound or resolved information. Calling it later consumes the
        remaining arguments/computation through ordinary graph operations.

    message-boundary reply wrapping
        wraps any produced result, including a callable/residual Structure, as a
        reply ProposedMessage only when the message boundary supplies a
        `replyTo` address or a local future/continuation that resolves to one.
        A correlation identifier relates records but does not supply an address.

7.2.3. Callable Argument Consumption and Partial Structural Binding

A callable passed as a source argument is an ordinary Lmx reference and is not
invoked merely by being passed. If the receiving body later invokes that
occurrence, invocation reads its `{ addr, sig }` record, passes
`callable->node`, supplies the declared actuals and the current caller values
for the hidden dynamic names in `sig`, and performs the explicitly typed C
call. Passing the callable does not capture the dynamic values of the passing
activation.

An explicit partial-application operation, if admitted by a profile, may build a
new ordinary Structure containing a callable link and bound argument
occurrences. The exact call-context subtree, retention/copy policy and syntax for
that operation are unresolved. The implementation must not solve the open item
with an implicit closure environment or captured runtime Namespace. An explicit
residual Structure may contain the callable and explicitly stored values.
`implements` remains the consumer-relative predicate from section 2.1; at a
callable leaf actually invoked by Consumer it also admits the method signature,
but it neither performs the invocation nor creates the residual callable.

Revision-1 `callable.projection`, `callable.capture`, closure-struct and partial
binding tables are non-normative migration history and are not part of the new
translator core.

7.3. Subroutine Definition Form

Example source:

sub: select_branch (boolean(condition)) on_true on_false
return

Strict conceptual form:

    sub(
      name(select_branch),
      args(
        boolean(condition),
        on_true,
        on_false
      ),
      body(...)
    )

Explicit nested-source form:

sub:
    name: select_branch
    args:
        boolean: condition
        on_true
        on_false
    body:
        return
end: select_branch

The receiver `sub` consumes its child Structures by receiver-defined semantic roles. The argument `condition` is a primitive/evaluated argument through the registered constructor `boolean`. The bare names `on_true` and `on_false` are structural arguments. No `Struct on_true` annotation is required.

7.3.1. Own Names, Dynamic Visibility and Exact Callable Replacement

Revision 2 has dynamic call visibility but no reified runtime Namespace object.
A name explicitly declared by the current method is its own local/formal and
takes precedence. An unqualified free name is a compiler-generated dynamic
input. At a call site the immediate caller supplies its current localized value
for that name; if the caller received the same name dynamically and has not
declared its own, it forwards its current copy. This gives transitive dynamic
visibility through actual calls without a runtime Name-to-Binding map.

When no caller activation supplies the name, the selected callable's immediate
containing node supplies the fallback:

    dynamic name x absent -> copy node\[0]x

The fallback is a value initialization, not an implicit structural write. A
Primitive is copied by semantic value. A Structure or Array copies its reference
value. Assigning the resulting C local affects only the current activation and
may be passed farther down to another callee, but it is discarded on return
unless the program explicitly stores or passes it. An explicit `node\x`,
`reference\x` or `array[index]` operation bypasses the dynamic copy and mutates
the selected graph/storage location.

For a valid invocation, every required free name is therefore supplied by one
of those two channels. When no dynamic caller value exists, absence of the
immediate `node\[0]name` fallback means that the callable occurrence is
incompatible with this invocation/Consumer and must be rejected by translation
or validation. It is not a third global lookup rule and does not create an
empty value implicitly.

The compiler keeps two analyses distinct:

    DynRequired(M)
        bare free names read/written by M, plus transitive names required by
        exact statically known callees or by the expected `sig` at a
        runtime-selected callable site, except names satisfied by M's own
        binding;

    OwnUsed(M)
        own graph-backed fields selected through unqualified binding for M's
        own work or needed to supply an outgoing callee dynamic value.

`DynRequired(M)` produces hidden C inputs and is never graph-backed merely
because it is forwarded. `OwnUsed(M)` produces the used-only C working locals
and dirty checkpoints of section 7.4. Explicit path operations belong to
neither cache merely because they mention the same name. Direct and mutually
recursive call graphs require a finite fixed-point propagation of
`DynRequired`; the selected method's `sig` records those names in deterministic
C-parameter order.

The compiler may compare source shapes with `implements(New Old Consumer)`
before emitting a statically checkable replacement. Dynamic operations may
perform the same comparison on live Structures. A used callable leaf requires
the exact canonical signature selected for the Consumer call site. Neither case creates an ordered
Name-to-Binding overlay. The default child occurrence is `[0]`, not the newest
declaration.

Revision-1 runtime namespace stacks, descriptor capture and environment objects
are non-normative migration history and must not be reproduced in the new
translator. Ordinary C arguments and automatic values are the runtime carrier
of dynamic visibility. A structural operation may compare a pending Lmx
subtree and the currently selected subtree with
`implements(New Old Consumer)` before mutating or appending graph occurrences.
Failure leaves the graph unchanged. Success does not change occurrence order:
an appended same-name child is a later occurrence, while ordinary structural
lookup remains the first `[0]` child.

An unqualified field-follow selects occurrence zero, the first occurrence of
the exact field name in the Structure's logical ordered field sequence:

    value\field

When the left value is written as an exact quoted identifier, its closing
backtick requires the ordinary token boundary before the field-follow operator:

    `exact value` \field
    `exact value` \ `exact field`

Both spellings are the same typed field-follow operation; the whitespace does
not turn `\` into a raw-load prefix.

An explicit occurrence-qualified field-follow is written:

    value\[occurrence]field

`occurrence` is zero-based among fields with the same exact name, counted in
logical source/ordered order from the first occurrence to the last. It is not
the absolute position among all fields and is not backend layout metadata such
as a `field.index` relation. The selector is an expression and is evaluated
once after the left value is evaluated once. Its result must be a non-negative
integer Primitive.

This does not reinterpret `value\field[index]`. That form first selects the
default `[0]field` occurrence and then indexes the value returned by the
field-follow. Consequently:

    value\field[index]       index the value of the default `[0]field`
    value\[index]field       select same-name field occurrence `index`
    value\[i]field[j]        select occurrence `i`, then index its value by `j`

An occurrence-qualified field-follow may be used everywhere an ordinary typed
field-follow may be used, including as an addressable update target. It selects
an existing field slot; it does not append a field or publish a namespace
binding.

This rule applies equally to explicit declarations, nested `fn`/`sub`
definitions, imports and named construction results. It is ordered Structure
traversal, not a shadowing policy.

Message selectors resolve through compiler/profile state or an explicitly
referenced message/operation Structure. Such a Structure may reference a
dispatch Table, but the selected implementation is a real callable Lmx
occurrence.

In expression position, an `fn` name can be lowered as a call only after it
resolves to an actual callable occurrence. The canonical way to return a value
is `return: value`, including terminal return at the source level of the current
function. Appending another same-name occurrence does not replace `[0]`.

Example:

fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)
    if: n <= 1
        return: 1
return: n * factorial(n - 1)

Inside the body, `return: 1` exits with value `1` when the condition holds. The expression `factorial(n - 1)` is a recursive function call because it appears in expression position. The final `return:` line is terminal return and closes the function.
