7.3.2. Function/Subroutine Occurrences and Addressable Updates

In Revision 2 a function/subroutine value is a callable Lmx occurrence. It may be
selected through an explicit Structure path or held in an own, declared or
dynamically inherited activation value. A call follows the selected occurrence's
method record and passes its containing `node`, declared actuals and
compiler-generated dynamic values to the globally linked C method according to
the exact expected `sig`. An addressable update changes the explicitly selected
Structure occurrence. Rebinding a declared or dynamically imported activation
input changes only that C value. Rebinding an own graph-backed C working local
changes that local immediately, marks its backing occurrence dirty, and affects
the graph only at the next checkpoint.
There is no separate function namespace and no reified combined runtime
namespace. Active dynamic values are ordinary C activation values; callable
identity and persistent source-visible state remain graph data.

Executable definitions remain ordinary Structure children before resolution.
A Frame is lowered as a call only when compiler state identifies a call-capable
activation binding, or actual graph traversal/current activation yields a
callable `fn`/`sub` occurrence or registered receiver whose record matches the
expected exact signature.

The line form:

    i: 5

has one surface shape. After name resolution, it may mean a plain-name rebind of
`i`, or a call/receiver consumption of the value currently bound as `i` with
argument `5`. The language does not use `=` assignment to distinguish these
cases.

When a plain-name head/update form resolves as a rebind rather than
call/receiver consumption, it updates the current activation value under
sections 7.4 and 21.3:

    i: otherCallable

An own graph-backed working local becomes dirty and is published only at its
next checkpoint. A declared formal/result or inherited/fallback dynamic value
changes only in this activation. This plain form neither immediately updates
`node\[0]i` nor appends a child occurrence.

Structural replacement or append requires an explicit addressable path or a
structural receiver. That operation applies `implements(new old Consumer)` to
the selected pending/current subtrees, leaves the graph unchanged on failure,
and preserves the existing first occurrence as `[0]` when it appends. The value
may be callable or non-callable. If Consumer invokes a used callable path, the
comparison includes the exact `all_methods_array.sig` selected at that call
site. This is part of the used-tree comparison, not a nominal type attached to
every value. Source semantics does not require a separate `=` spelling for the
activation-local rebind.

An update whose head is an addressable field/index location mutates that selected
graph/storage location according to its receiver. It does not append a hidden
name binding.

For a function/subroutine callable occurrence or registered receiver with no
declared arguments, value-like consumption may be receiver/profile-defined. For
example, an operator method or receiver may lower a read of `i` to a
zero-declared-argument source call and an update to an explicit update Frame,
depending on the resolved occurrence and active profile. The generated C method
still receives reserved `callable->node` and any hidden dynamic values required
by its signature.

Call admission has one coherent Consumer-relative rule. Structural paths,
primitive leaves and used callable signatures are checked by
`implements(varA varB Consumer)`. The selected call receiver additionally maps
the concrete actual values into the already admitted declared formal ABI. It
does not invent a second nominal callable type system.

7.3.3. Nested `fn` and `sub` Definitions

Nested `fn` and `sub` definitions are allowed inside named Structures and
executable bodies. Each nested executable is a real Lmx child occurrence and
has a real globally reachable C implementation. The translator must recursively
walk executable definitions at every depth; a module pass restricted to
top-level definitions is non-conforming.

Example:

fn: outer (int(x)) (int)
    fn: addX (int(y)) (int)
    return: x + y
return: addX(10)

The nested `fn` `addX` is reached as a child/path of `outer`. Inside generated
code, the reserved `node` argument is derived from the selected `addX`
occurrence. Bare `x` is a hidden dynamic argument: the current `outer`
activation passes its C-local value. Explicit `node\x` instead reads or writes
the immediate containing Structure field and cannot be overridden by the
caller. No lexical environment object is captured or copied. Recursion follows
the callable occurrence in the Structure graph while each invocation has its
own ordinary C stack activation.

When an executable occurrence is copied by runtime `merge`, the copy keeps the
same global C method address but receives a rewritten `node` parent. Therefore
the one method body can operate on the copied containing Structure without a
new C compilation. Returning, storing or passing a nested callable passes an
Lmx reference under the normal graph lifetime rules. It does not require a
`c.closure-struct` descriptor.

The precise result channel and lifetime rule for an escaping callable remain
Revision-2 ABI items. An escaping callable does not retain the current C
activation or its dynamic inputs implicitly. A solution may retain or copy
ordinary Lmx nodes only when the source explicitly constructs such state; it
must not create a semantic namespace/capture object.

Repeated exact names create ordered child occurrences. The following source is
checked by the structural rules of section 2.1 and addressed through occurrence
indexes; it does not replace a runtime namespace binding:

fn: outer () (int)
    int: helper
    fn: helper (int(x)) (int)
    return: x
return: 0

The old hosted behaviour that emitted a stub for a nested body, omitted the
body entirely, or represented the nested declaration only as a descriptor is a
translator defect and is explicitly prohibited.

7.4. Body Names, C Working Locals and Checkpoints

A body has three distinct binding categories:

| Binding | C working value | Automatic publication |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Declared argument | Primitive value or copied reference | None |
| Imported dynamic name | Caller value, otherwise immediate `node\[0]name` | None |
| Used own field | Local loaded from its exact backing Lmx occurrence | Dirty writes only |

Declaring an argument does not make it an own field. Explicit `node\field`,
`reference\field` and `array[index]` operations access the referent on each
execution, not a cached own local.

Before calls and exits, publish only own locals actually written since their
last successful checkpoint; clear dirty after a successful store. Never reload
the caller after a call. Recursion has separate C frames but may publish to the
same containing Structure.

Section 21 is the full contract: name selection (21.3), used-field/retained-
occurrence rules (21.5), checkpoints and private-helper boundaries (21.6),
recursion and the normative ontology statement (21.7), exact operand evaluation
order (21.8), and cleanup/suspension (21.10). Unresolved backing-mutation and
store-failure policies are listed once in 21.13.

7.5. `end:` as General Closing Receiver

`end` is written with the ordinary colon Frame surface form, but it is not an ordinary executable body receiver. It is analogous to a closing parenthesis for vertical Structures. It has no runtime-return meaning by itself and no body-level runtime action.

The source-level closing form is:

    end: <target>

The bare form `end` and the old-looking form `end <target>` are not valid Lingvamyxa source syntax.

`end:` validates `<target>` against the admitted close targets of a visible open
frame/entity/label block. An opening frame admits at least its receiver/head key
as a close target unless the receiver explicitly defines an anonymous/nameless
close policy. A receiver may also expose a more specific visible name. The
privileged source spelling for such a visible name is the ordinary argument name
`name`: when a receiver consumes a valid `name` argument, that value is the
canonical close target. The receiver/head key remains a valid alternative close
target even when such a canonical name exists. Multi-word target names may be
written as exact quoted identifiers or as string close targets, depending on
what the receiver accepted into its close-name table.

The `<target>` operand of `end:` is outside ordinary runtime name/path expression
rules. It is a literal close target consumed by the structural validation layer,
not a variable, function call, computed value or user-level receiver argument.
Therefore `end: someName` never means "evaluate `someName` and close whatever it
returns"; it means "close the currently visible target whose admitted source
close target is exactly `someName`". In this sense `end:` has broader
structural authority than
ordinary function receivers: it may cut an admitted open tail, while ordinary
calls can only consume and produce runtime values inside already-open structure.

Unlike `return: value`, which may be a valid runtime item inside an executable
body, `end: <target>` is valid only as a structural close from an admitted close
position. If P0 records `end: <target>` as an ordinary nested Frame because it is
written inside a body position rather than at a close position, the close
validation layer must reject it unless an explicit profile defines another
non-runtime meaning for that receiver.

For `fn`, the canonical explicit close target is the function name consumed by
the `fn` receiver. Therefore `fn: myFunc ...` canonically closes as `end:
myFunc`, and an expanded form with `name: myFunc` also canonically closes as
`end: myFunc`. The receiver/head alternative is also admitted, so the same
function may close as `end: fn` when that is clearer in context.

The closing line has its own source level. It may close several currently open implicit vertical levels at once if the named target is visible at or above that level. This allows a long tail of nested blocks to be cut with one named close. A parser must reject `end: <target>` if the target is ambiguous, not visible or not valid for closing from that position.

`end:` is not the default close mechanism for all named frames. The default structural close is the ordinary source-level cut. `end:` is the explicit named form of that close. It is useful when the source wants to identify the close target directly, close across a long nested tail, or improve diagnostics and readability in long blocks.

Examples:

if: condition
    bodyCall()
end: if
else:
    fallbackCall()
end: else

or with ordinary source-level cuts:

if: condition
    bodyCall()
else:
    fallbackCall()

Here `if` and `else` have no separate `name` argument, so the core receivers
expose their head spellings as close targets.

fn: myFunc (int(i)) (int(r))
    return: i
end: myFunc

Here `fn` exposes the function name `myFunc` as the canonical close target.
`end: fn` would also be a valid close of this frame by receiver/head key.

fn:
    name: myFunc
    args:
        int: i
    ---
    ret:
        int: r
    ---
    body:
        return: i
end: myFunc

Here `fn` exposes the explicit `name: myFunc` field as the canonical close
target. `end: fn` remains valid as the receiver/head close target.

fn:
    args:
        int: i
    ---
    name: myFunc
    ret:
        int: r
    ---
    body:
        return: i
end: myFunc

This is also valid because close-target validation uses the normalized valid
`name` argument, not the physical position of the `name:` field.

The same rule applies to any receiver that consumes a valid `name` argument as
its visible name. In this example the receiver/head is `hello`, while the
canonical visible close target is `world`:

hello: name: world
. "!"
. "!"
end: world

When the same close target is visible at several levels, the source level of
the closing line participates in target selection. A parent-level close selects
the matching target opened at that parent level and cuts the open deeper tail
along the way:

hello: name: world  # world #1, opened at level 0
. "1"
. hello: name: world  # world #2, opened at level 1
. . "2"
. . "!"
end: world  # closes world #1 and also closes its still-open tail

To close the inner `world` explicitly, place the close at the inner opening
level:

hello: name: world  # world #1
. "1"
. hello: name: world  # world #2
. . "2"
. . "!"
. end: world  # closes world #2
end: world    # closes world #1

All core examples above close by one of the receiver-admitted targets. For
receivers with a valid `name` argument, that argument value is the canonical
close target, and the receiver/head spelling remains an admitted alternative.
For receivers without a valid `name` argument, the ordinary admitted target is
the receiver/head spelling. A receiver that wants no named close target must
define that explicitly.

The argument name `name` is privileged for readable structural closing, not for
ordinary execution semantics. Tools may highlight it specially, the normalized
Structure exposes its value as the introduced visible close name, and
`end: <target>` validation uses it as the canonical named close target for that
opened Structure. The receiver/head key remains admitted as a structural close
target unless the receiver explicitly disables that policy. If the `name`
argument value fails the close-name table check, the receiver behaves for named
close as though no such argument was supplied and therefore admits only the
receiver/head target.

A translator may implement vertical closure by internal closing handlers. Those handlers are implementation mechanisms. Source semantics only requires that the correct Structure is closed and that invalid closing tokens are rejected according to the active closing policy.


7.6. Label Blocks as Local Nullary Executables

Labels are not a separate source grammar category. A label spelling such as:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
end: readLoop

is an ordinary colon Frame with an empty inline tail and a vertical body. When
the active executable/control profile consumes this shape as a label block, it
creates a label/control Lmx child occurrence in the containing Structure.
Because this receiver has no separate `name` argument, its visible close target
is its own head spelling. Therefore the explicit close form is:

Label:
    step0
    step1
end: Label

A label block can also be closed by the ordinary source-level cut when the
surrounding structure makes the target clear. `end: Label` is the explicit
checked form for long or visually separated label bodies.

A label block is a local executable/control occurrence with no declared source
arguments and no normal return value. Explicit names/paths in its body are graph
data; bare free names are hidden dynamic inputs with the same caller-chain and
immediate-node fallback as another method. Nullary means zero declared
arguments, not zero dynamic values. Whether it can be consumed is checked by
that receiver and its exact `sig` at the call site. There is neither a separate
label Namespace nor a combined reified runtime Namespace for labels and other
values.

In executable statement position, a bare identifier or exact identifier may
resolve through compiler state or structural traversal to a zero-argument
label-block occurrence. In that context, the bare or exact name is the canonical
call/consumption syntax for the label block:

readLoop

This is not a special parser form and it does not require `readLoop()`. The
parser builds an identifier/value line. The current executable consumer resolves
`readLoop` to the actual local nullary executable occurrence and invokes its
typed method record. Its declared argument list is empty, but the call still
supplies the containing `callable->node` and every hidden dynamic value listed
by `sig`.

The same label occurrence may also carry a control-target role when the active
profile materializes it as a loop/block label. Control-transfer receivers such
as `break: readLoop`, `continue: readLoop`, `redo: readLoop`, `retry: readLoop`
or failure-control receivers use that control-target role. A plain executable
statement `readLoop` uses the callable/nullary-executable role.

Example post-condition loop:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

Here `readLoop:` opens a labelled executable block. The `until:` receiver closes
that block and consumes it as the body of a post-condition loop. The label name
`readLoop` identifies its Structure occurrence; the `until` lowering may
implement the loop by direct structured control flow, by a generated label and
branch, by an inline nullary block, or by another representation preserving the
same source semantics.

Calling or consuming a label block does not create a function Namespace, return
binding or closure object. A later profile may define escaping label values,
addressable label callables or call-frame labels, but the structural core only
requires a local nullary executable occurrence and structural/compiler
resolution.


8. Runtime Return and Terminal Return

8.1. `return` Receiver

Runtime exit from an `fn`, `sub`, iterator-like executable body or method-like executable body is represented by the `return` receiver/operator.

`return` is not the same as the general structural close receiver `end:`. `end:` closes source structure by visible target name. `return` exits executable control flow. In a terminal trailer position inside an open executable definition, an admitted `return` form may also serve as the structural close of that executable definition.

For `fn`, the ordinary explicit structural close uses the function name exposed
by the `fn` receiver, for example `end: square`. A value return, written as
`return: value`, may also close the function when it appears in a valid terminal
trailer position. The same spelling inside the body is still an ordinary runtime
return and does not structurally close the definition.

8.2. Return Forms

The core return forms are:

    return
    return: value

Inside an executable body, `return` exits the current executable body.

For an `fn`, the core profile requires a value-bearing return form. `return:
value` evaluates or consumes one logical value admitted by
`implements(value declaredReturn returnConsumers)` by
section 2.1, then exits the current executable body. `value` denotes one Lmx
reference admitted by that same predicate, whether its payload domain is a
scalar leaf, Structure branch, callable or array representation. Whitespace-separated short-tail fields admitted by
an `fm` receiver populate its already existing return Structure before the
control exit; they do not create an aggregate value or allocate a Structure in
the `return` operation.

For a `sub`, `return` without tail exits without producing a normal return value.

For a `sub`, `return: value` is invalid unless a later profile defines a status/result convention for subroutines.

For a label/procedure-like executable with no return value, a bare `return` may be valid when the profile admits it.

A bare `return` is not a valid `fn` return in the core profile. Another explicit
`return` receiver may define a different convention, but that is not the default
source semantics of this revision.

`return` is a receiver. It consumes the Structure supplied by its tail. It is not assignment syntax and does not introduce `=` as a native assignment or named-field operator.

Examples:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
return: x * x

Conceptual executable body:

    return(x * x)

Example with early runtime return and terminal return:

fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)
    if: n <= 1
        return: 1
return: n * factorial(n - 1)

The indented `return: 1` line is an ordinary runtime return inside the `if` body. The final `return: n * factorial(n - 1)` is terminal return for the surrounding `fn` definition.

8.3. Terminal Return as Executable Structural Close

A terminal return is a `return` source item written in a structural trailer position for an open executable definition. The common compact position is the source level of the executable definition itself, where a structural close of that executable definition is expected.

If an executable definition is opened at source level N, then its ordinary
vertical body items are normally written at level N + 1. A `return` line at level
N is not a sibling root item. It is parsed as terminal return for the currently
open executable definition when that definition's resolved receiver admits the
return form.

Here "at the source level of the executable definition" means the level of the opening source item, such as `fn: name` or `sub: name`, not the ordinary body level. A `return` or `return: value` item written at level N + 1 or deeper is a runtime return inside the executable body. It exits the executable at runtime, but it is not the structural trailer of the executable definition.

Terminal return is equivalent to appending the corresponding runtime `return` as
the final body item and then closing the executable definition by its admitted
executable close target. For an `fn`, this is equivalent to appending `return:
value` to the body and closing the function with its receiver-exposed name, such
as `end: square`.

If the terminal `return` item is written at a smaller source level than the executable definition itself, it may still act as the terminal trailer for the visible executable tail when the normal source-level cut reaches that tail and all intervening frames admit such closure. In that case, the `return` role belongs to the executable tail. Enclosing non-executable frames are closed by the source-level cut, not because `return` is a general close receiver for them.

Example:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
return: x * x

is source-semantically equivalent to:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
    return: x * x
end: square

The first form is the canonical compact form for a short function. The second form is an explicit expanded explanation of the same source semantics.

Example with an enclosing level receiver:

L3:
    sub: helloMain
        print: "Hello"
    return
end: L3

means:

L3:
    sub: helloMain
        print: "Hello"
        return
    end: helloMain
end: L3

The source-level `return` at the level of `sub: helloMain` closes the `sub: helloMain` definition. The following `end: L3` closes only the enclosing level receiver.

Example where the same terminal return item is written at a lower source level:

L3:
    sub: helloMain
        print: "Hello"
return

means, when the active level profile admits this cut:

L3:
    sub: helloMain
        print: "Hello"
        return
    end: helloMain
end: L3

Terminal return may close the open executable tail in the same checked way as the executable's admitted `end:` target. For an `fn`, that target is the function name exposed by the `fn` receiver, such as `end: square`. It is not a general close form for arbitrary Structures. It is valid only for executable definitions or executable-like receivers that explicitly admit terminal return.

A terminal `return` may also cut through still-open nested body frames above its target level in the same structural-trailer sense as `end: <name>` or an allowed nameless delimiter, provided all intervening frames admit such closure.

Invalid examples:

return: 1

at root level with no open executable is invalid.

config: app
return: 1

is invalid unless `config` is an executable-like receiver that explicitly admits terminal return. `return` does not replace `end:` for ordinary data, model, configuration or registry Structures.

8.4. Runtime Return vs Terminal Return

The source level separates the two roles.

Runtime return inside a body:

fn: abs (int(x)) (int)
    if: x < 0
        return: -x
return: x

Here the indented `return: -x` line is runtime control inside the `if` body. It does not structurally close the `fn` source item. The final `return: x` is terminal return at the function level and closes the function.

Terminal value return may use values prepared in named return variables:

fm: `estimate harvest` (PlantBed(bed)) (f32(harvest), f32(redApples), f32(greenApples))
    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
return: harvest redApples greenApples

This is equivalent to appending a body-level `return: harvest redApples greenApples` and closing the function.

8.5. Named Return Variables

Multiple return values may be represented by declared return variables inside an executable body when the `fm` return-description Structure gives them names.

Example:

fm: `estimate harvest` (PlantBed(bed))
    (
        f32: harvest
        f32: redApples
        f32: greenApples
    )
    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
return: harvest redApples greenApples

The named return variables denote fields of the already existing return
Structure. The terminal `return:` finishes their updates, forwards that
Structure reference and closes the function definition. It does not allocate or
copy the return Structure.

An `fm` return-description Structure with several fields defines one Lmx return
Structure. Updating its declared fields and returning that existing reference
are source-semantically equivalent when the produced
Structure is admitted by `implements(returnedStructure declaredReturn returnConsumers)`.

Example positional structural return:

fm: `split harvest` (PlantBed(bed))
    (
        f32: harvest
        f32: redApples
        f32: greenApples
    )
return: bed\area 0 0

Here the `return:` tail supplies three field values consumed by the return
signature. The values are installed in the already existing return Structure;
the `return` control operation then forwards its reference.

8.6. Function Name Rebind

Revision 2 has no reified lexical Namespace object, but a C activation may have
an explicitly receiver-defined result working slot. The function definition's
callable Lmx occurrence remains a distinct persistent child in its containing
Structure and is never overwritten by that slot.

The historical example

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
    square: x * x
return: square

is an admitted function-result shorthand when the `fn` receiver declares an
activation-local result working slot named `square`. `square: x * x` assigns
that C value and `return: square` returns it. It does not update/append a
structural child, mutate the callable occurrence or create a runtime Namespace.
If a function profile does not declare that shorthand slot, the spelling is
rejected rather than reinterpreted as graph mutation. Canonical
`return: value` remains valid; the exact C result channel is open.

8.7. Implicit Return at Function End

Implicit return at function end is not a core source form in this revision.

In the core profile, an explicit structural close such as `end: f` does not append an implicit runtime return for a value-producing function.

The preferred style is:

fn: f (int(x)) (int)
return: x + 1

rather than relying on a value left in a body child occurrence at structural close.

8.8. Return Source Semantics

A `fn` has one logical result under its declared contract: a primitive value,
a Structure/callable reference, or an Array-descriptor reference. An `fm`
returns one explicit Lmx result Structure whose children provide its
multiple-return surface. The consumer may keep it or unpack those fields.

`return: expression` returns the admitted value after
`implements(expression declaredReturn returnConsumers)`. For a reference it
preserves the exact referent identity; return itself does not clone, allocate
or promote a tree or Array. Any explicit construction belongs to evaluation of
the expression. Primitive results follow their value contract.

A bare `return` exits a `sub` or no-value executable. In trailer position,
terminal return also closes the definition. Failure values without `throws`
must satisfy the return contract; declared failures pass explicit payloads to
`catch` under 19.11.

The physical result/status channel, including placement of an `fm` result
Structure, is an open ABI item recorded in `sig`. It must retain returned
references and cannot expose a per-name C aggregate or hidden Namespace.

Every return has this order:

    evaluate result once and retain/root it
        -> dirty-only checkpoint
        -> applicable finally cleanups, reverse registration order
        -> checkpoint own fields changed by cleanup
        -> transfer result and destroy C activation

Without cleanup the second checkpoint is normally empty. Dynamic inputs are
never copied back and the caller is never reloaded. Return is not end-turn
collection (19.29.14).

9. Type and Model Forms

9.0. Default C99 Bootstrap Type and Dependency Map

The default ANSI C / C99 bootstrap profile may use the following implementation mapping for primitive and service-level source types. This is an architecture-binding / dependency map for descriptions authored as in section 2.2.2, not a language list of primitive kinds and not a separate source grammar.

    i/u      -> C integer          -> no vcpkg
    bool     -> C int / bool       -> no vcpkg
    bigint   -> GMP mpz_t          -> vendored `third_party/gmp.zip`
    real     -> MPFR mpfr_t        -> vendored `third_party/mpfr.zip`
    decimal  -> decNumber         -> vendored `third_party/decNumber.zip`
    SQL      -> SQLite            -> vendored `third_party/sqlite.zip`
    Regex    -> PCRE2 8/16/32     -> vendored `third_party/pcre2.zip`

The default project bootstrap keeps third-party source archives under
`third_party/*.zip`. A full local build expands them into `build/third_party/`
and emits static libraries into `build/libs/`. `vcpkg` may be used as a
download/cache helper for upstream archives, but the default build does not
depend on live package-manager libraries. The bundled PCRE2 profile builds
8-bit, 16-bit and 32-bit code-unit libraries so raw length-based payloads can be
interpreted by the receiver profile instead of being forced through a single
null-terminated UTF-8-only representation.

The `i/u` family denotes implementation-profile integer payload domains over C
integer storage. `bigint`, `real`, `decimal`, `SQL` and `Regex` are ordinary
source names resolved to constructors/operations or explicit description
Structures when the active profile installs them. Their physical representation
and linked libraries belong to the selected L1/runtime profile; no runtime
Namespace or attached type record is implied.

9.1. Type and Model Information

Type and model information, when required, is represented by ordinary explicit
description Structures. A compiler may resolve a declaration/import name to one
of those Structures or to a constructor operation, but the runtime does not
attach the description to every Lmx node or store it in a Name-to-value
Namespace. An ordinary Table may contain description data; placing a row in a
Table does not alter source-name resolution or runtime graph reachability.

The structural core does not require a separate `field` receiver. In
declaration context, a compiler/profile-resolved primitive or model constructor
consumes a raw identifier token and creates the requested Lmx child occurrence
at runtime. A primitive constructor selects a registered payload address domain;
an explicit description Structure from section 2.2.2 may be supplied to the
operation but is not attached to the result. A Structure or Array constructor
materializes an Lmx-reachable value only when its runtime construction site is
reached. Neither operation creates a class, per-name layout or runtime name
table.

Example:

    u32: id
    Text: crop
    f64: harvest 0.0

Conceptual meaning:

    create child occurrence `id` through the `u32` constructor
    create child occurrence `crop` through the `Text` constructor
    create child occurrence `harvest` through the `f64` constructor with value `0.0`

The token after the type constructor is not evaluated as an existing value. It
is the raw name proposed by the declaration. If the exact name is already a
child at the selected structural location, the form is a redeclaration governed by
`implements(NewType OldType Consumer)` as specified in section 7.3.1, with Consumer the
remaining known source that still sees the name.

9.1.1. Nested Description and Constructor Forms

Type/element and mutability descriptions plus Structure/Array constructors are
ordinary compiler/profile-resolved receiver heads. They do
not require a separate declaration syntax. A colon only builds the right-nested
form; it is not a named semantic node.

For example:

    const: char: (): s "hello" "world!"
    const: char: []: s "hello" "world!"
    const: String: (): s "hello" "world!"
    const: String: []: s "hello" "world!"

These are parsed as nested forms:

    const(char(()(s, "hello", "world!")))
    const(char([](s, "hello", "world!")))
    const(String(()(s, "hello", "world!")))
    const(String([](s, "hello", "world!")))

The heads `const`, `char`, `String`, `()` and `[]` are ordinary source names
resolved by compiler/profile state. In this profile:

    const       qualifies the introduced storage/value as immutable
    char        selects the character primitive/element payload operation
    String      selects the Lingvamyxa immutable string value/reference model
    ()          creates a named Lmx Structure occurrence at runtime
    []          creates an Array descriptor/backing; a named Lmx field stores its reference

Therefore the examples mean:

    const: char: (): s "hello" "world!"
        `s` is an immutable Structure reference whose fields contain character
        or text values according to the explicitly selected operation/profile.

    const: char: []: s "hello" "world!"
        `s` is an immutable array-domain Lmx reference whose elements are
        character/text values. Its exact storage and lifetime follow the
        unresolved Array/lifetime ABI.

    const: String: (): s "hello" "world!"
        `s` is a reference to an immutable Lmx Structure whose fields
        are references to immutable Lingvamyxa String values.

    const: String: []: s "hello" "world!"
        `s` is an immutable logical array whose elements denote immutable
        Lingvamyxa String values; physical element representation is unresolved.

`char` and `String` are intentionally different operations/contracts. `char`
selects a primitive/element payload domain. `String` has higher-level immutable text behavior
and must not be treated as merely a spelling of `char *`.

---ANSI C---
A Structure field's `Lmx.data` may refer to String storage or to an Array
descriptor. Array descriptors live in typed service arrays and contain length
and an element-storage pointer (6.5); String encoding remains profile-defined.
The field's Lmx header is not the layout of either referent. A generated
per-source-name `typedef struct`, descriptor prefix or direct named-member layout
is prohibited; a proven direct access may only optimize generic traversal
without changing observable graph semantics. C automatic/static duration is not
observable source semantics.
---ANSI C---

9.2. Type Constructors

A form such as:

    int: i

or:

    PlantBed: bed

is meaningful only if compiler/profile resolution selects `int` or `PlantBed`
as a primitive constructor or Structure-builder operation. An ordinary
description Structure by itself is not automatically a constructor. Merely
exposing names for `implements(varA varB Consumer)`, or
supplying backend layout information, does not by itself make a source name a
constructor. `PlantBed: bed` executes the selected runtime builder and creates
the Lmx occurrence `bed`; it does not instantiate a statically materialized
`PlantBed` object, attach a descriptor or modify `PlantBed`.

A receiver such as `fn`, `sub`, `autoModel` or `args` may consume these Structures as typed declarations.

10. Conditional Structures: L3 Core Extract

10.1. L3 `if`

L3 `if` is a computational control-flow statement receiver. In the current
core form it consumes:

    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    body      -> statement Structure consumed by `if`

`else` is a separate sibling statement receiver. It may immediately follow an
`if` at the same statement level and consumes:

    body      -> statement Structure consumed by `else`

At interpretation time, the `if` condition is evaluated once. If the condition
is true, the `if` body is interpreted and the following sibling `else` body is
skipped. If the condition is false, the `if` body is skipped and the following
sibling `else` body, when present, is interpreted.

At translation time, the two receivers lower to adjacent target control-flow
forms such as `if (...) { ... } else { ... }`. If another statement separates
`if` and `else`, the pair is no longer adjacent and the target/profile
validator may reject the resulting control flow.

10.2. Conditional Forms

Short positional conditional form:

if: mode = debug
    build: debugTarget

else:
    build: releaseTarget

Normal receiver sequence:

    if(
      <expression Structure for `mode = debug`>,
      (build(debugTarget))
    )
    else(
      (build(releaseTarget))
    )

A profile may also define an independent branch-like receiver with explicit
named branch Structures:

branch: mode = debug
    first:
        build: debugTarget
    second:
        build: releaseTarget
end: branch

This builds:

    branch(
      <expression Structure for `mode = debug`>,
      first(build(debugTarget)),
      second(build(releaseTarget))
    )

The named vertical form is an ordinary receiver-signature pattern. It is not
required for the current core `if`/`else` statement pair.

11. L2 Addresses

11.0. L2 Addresses, References and Callable References

This section separates ordinary Structure/primitive/Array values from explicit typed-address
and machine-storage operations available only at L2. Raw L2 addresses may point
outside registered high-level payload domains; they are not a second header or
an implicit representation of an ordinary Structure.

11.1. L3 Passing Model

L3 exposes no raw machine-address syntax. Structure/callable values use Lmx
references, Arrays use direct descriptor references (6.5), and primitive
arguments/results follow their value contracts. Bare names may denote current
C activation values (21); explicit paths access graph fields.

An L3 handle is an admitted primitive or explicit wrapper Structure with a
checked operation contract. It does not expose its raw backing merely because
the implementation stores a pointer.

11.2. L2 Address Values and Storage/Reference Declarations

At L2, ordinary Structure/primitive/Array values coexist with explicit
typed-address values and raw machine storage. A compiler/profile-resolved builder such as `PlantBed` creates
an ordinary Lmx Structure only when its runtime construction site is executed.
Ordinary `[]:` creates an Array descriptor in its typed service pool and the
actual element backing (6.5). Neither operation attaches a Structure-schema
prefix, instantiates a per-source-name C struct or updates a runtime Namespace. Explicit address slots and
low-level machine-storage forms separately expose raw storage.

Bare L2 value reading is intentionally narrow. A primitive/scalar Lmx leaf or a
typed-address primitive may be read directly by naming its occurrence. Raw
machine aggregates, when a profile exposes them, are accessed through typed
addresses/low-level operations and are not ordinary Lmx Structures.

Examples:

PlantBed: branch
PlantBed: tree
PlantBed: root
int: count 5
[]: leafs count

int: x 5
@: int p
@@: int pp
@@@: int ppp

p: @x
pp: @p
ppp: @pp

Meanings:

    branch  Lmx `PlantBed` Structure occurrence created at runtime
    tree    Lmx `PlantBed` Structure occurrence created at runtime
    root    Lmx `PlantBed` Structure occurrence created at runtime
    count   Lmx primitive leaf initialized to `5`
    leafs   named Lmx field holding a descriptor reference to `count` Array elements
    x       Lmx primitive leaf initialized to `5`
    p       address-depth-1 slot whose base type is `int`
    pp      address-depth-2 slot whose base type is `int`
    ppp     address-depth-3 slot whose base type is `int`
    p: @x   stores the address of storage location `x` into `p`
    pp: @p  stores the address of storage location `p` into `pp`
    ppp: @pp stores the address of storage location `pp` into `ppp`

Structure builders such as `PlantBed`, primitive constructors such as `int`,
and Array constructors such as `[]` are compiler/profile-resolved operations.
They consume a raw result name and create that result occurrence at the runtime
construction site; they do not register an attached descriptor, emit a per-name
layout or generate Table rows.

An L2 address value is not necessarily a source-level integer. It may carry, or be statically associated with, layout/type metadata. Receivers and L1 lowering may use that metadata to treat an address as a typed reference rather than as a printable numeric address.

The core L2 syntax does not use a separate `*` or `^` marker for address types.
Address-ness is introduced by address-slot declarations, the reserved
address-depth operator family `@ⁿ`, address-producing expressions and explicit
raw field/index operations. Ordinary Arrays do not introduce raw addresses.

Minimal L2 field-follow is address-oriented. If the binding is a typed address,
`value\field` is the direct Lingvamyxa field-follow form. A direct ANSI C
value-structure operation is written through the explicit C door:
`c.value.field`, which lowers to `value.field`. This keeps C value-member access
visible as C-surface syntax while leaving ordinary Lingvamyxa values as Lmx
references.

11.2.1. Reserved Address-Depth Operator Family `@ⁿ`

The token sequences:

    @
    @@
    @@@

form the reserved L2 address-depth operator family `@ⁿ`. They are reserved operator heads, not ordinary user-defined identifiers. Ordinary source cannot bind procedures, functions, receivers or variables named `@`, `@@`, `@@@` and so on, unless a privileged core/prelude profile explicitly defines the reserved operator metadata.

In head position before `:`, `@ⁿ:` declares an address/reference slot of depth n:

    @: BaseType name
    @@: BaseType name
    @@@: BaseType name

Examples:

    @: int p
    @@: int pp
    @@@: int ppp

Meanings:

    p    address-depth-1 slot whose base type is `int`
    pp   address-depth-2 slot whose base type is `int`
    ppp  address-depth-3 slot whose base type is `int`

Whitespace separates the base type and the binding name under the ordinary short-tail rule. Therefore:

    @@@: int ppp

has two fields, `int` and `ppp`, while:

    @@@: int; ppp

terminates the `@@@:` short form after `int`. The following `ppp` is not the binding-name field of that declaration; it is the next source item at the current level.

The prefix expression form `@x` remains the depth-1 address-of operation. Higher address depth is expressed by declaring deeper address slots and storing ordinary depth-1 addresses of those slots:

    int: x 5
    @: int p
    @@: int pp
    @@@: int ppp

    p: @x
    pp: @p
    ppp: @pp

This keeps prefix address-of simple while preserving arbitrary address depth in declarations.

The implementation of `@ⁿ` may be selected by reserved compiler/profile
operator entries. The source semantics only require that `@ⁿ` is parsed as a
reserved operator family and that its declaration form produces typed address
slots compatible with the active L2 memory profile. No runtime descriptor or
Namespace is required.

Address-depth is an L2 machine-profile spelling of typed address *slots*, not a
property of Lmx source values.

A Primitive binding (`int: x`) is a cell. `@: int p` is an L2 slot that stores
the address of a cell. `@@:` / `@@@:` nest those slots. Prefix `@x` is
address-of the *binding* `x`. If `x` already holds an address, `@x` is
address-of-address.

An Lmx child occurrence (`User: u`) *is* the source reference. It is not a C
pointer category that "starts at depth 1". `@: User slot` is an L2 address of a
machine slot that stores that Lmx reference. The same holds for an array-domain
Lmx occurrence. Explicit `@ⁿ` adds layers on those machine slots only.

Do not collapse `FILE` and `User`. `FILE` as authored in section 2.2.2.4 is a
primitive opaque cell (a word whose description merged `Cell` with opaque
spelling). `User` is an ordinary Lmx Structure reference. A C spelling `FILE *` for the
primitive cell is projection of that cell, not proof that `FILE` is a
Structure. A C spelling `Lmx *` is the universal Structure reference, not a
requirement or permission to give each Structure name a C `typedef`.

An imported opaque L1 name may be stored and passed without exposing fields.
A profile/import that explicitly declares layout metadata may make typed
field-follow available for that imported layout. That is import of ABI, not
a third value category.

Ordinary Array construction creates a `{len, data}` descriptor in the service
array for the used type and a contiguous element backing. It produces the
descriptor reference; a containing Lmx field is a separate occurrence. The
hosted `ArrayReference` record is not selected by this section.

Primitive/scalar descriptions are ordinary explicit Structures. Compiler/profile
resolution may select a constructor and may supply such a Structure for range,
contextual-literal, projection or serialization information. An explicit Table
may organize the same information, but Table membership does not declare the
primitive, alter the value ontology or attach metadata to its Lmx leaf.

Examples in the hosted ANSI C profile (one L1 spelling, not source kinds):

    char          -> primitive cell, C `char`
    @: char       -> L2 address slot of a `char` cell, C `char *`
    []: char      -> typed service-pool descriptor {len, data}
                     # data points to contiguous char elements

    FILE          -> primitive opaque cell; C may spell the cell `FILE *`
    @: FILE       -> L2 address of the binding that stores that cell

    User          -> ordinary `Lmx *` Structure reference
                     # C must not emit a per-name `typedef struct User`
    @: User       -> L2 address of the binding that stores that reference
    []: User      -> typed service-pool descriptor {len, data}
                     # data points to contiguous User-reference cells

The Array arrows distinguish the typed service descriptor from the actual
application backing. A `FILE *` spelling of the primitive `FILE` payload belongs
to raw/profile storage; it is not the Structure header used for `User`.

The hosted C99 profile distinguishes three operations without changing the
universal value ontology:

1. Ordinary Structure field-follow enumerates real Lmx children, compares their
   ShortNameId values in the sole reverse index and selects `[0]` or an explicit
   occurrence. It uses no attached description and no named C member layout.

2. An imported primitive opaque payload such as `FILE` may be stored and passed
   without exposing Structure children. A C spelling such as `FILE *` belongs
   to its registered/raw payload domain. It becomes structurally traversable
   only through an explicit wrapper Structure or a distinct raw L2 ABI operation.

3. An explicit L2 machine path may lower to a selected L1 pointer-member
   spelling such as `a->b`. That is foreign/machine-layout access, not the
   definition of ordinary `user\age` traversal.

When the translator needs to know that a field is callable or what an operation
returns, it uses compiler resolution state and explicitly supplied contract
Structures. Runtime traversal itself uses the graph. Compiler caches are not
source values, runtime Namespaces, attached descriptors or alternative Lmx
headers. Returning or passing a resolved occurrence preserves the same Lmx
reference.

L2 `@ⁿ` depth applies to typed machine-address slots only (section 11.2), never
to an Lmx Structure, callable or array-domain occurrence as a source value. An
imported ABI description may guide an explicit raw L2 projection but cannot
replace generic Structure traversal or create a per-source-name layout.

Any hosted fallback that still uses `class.reference-base`, `class.kind`,
`class.semantic`, `class.c.projection` or a structural-field cache is
non-normative migration machinery. It must not classify ordinary Lmx values,
attach a descriptor or create another source-semantic lookup environment.

11.3. Address-of, Raw Load and Callable References

The prefix form:

    @x

returns the address of the storage location `x`.

If `x` is already an address binding, `@x` is the address of the binding that stores that address. It is address-of-address, not the address of the target object. This is the expression-side companion of the declaration-side `@ⁿ:` family: `@@: int pp` declares an address-depth-2 slot, while `pp: @p` fills that slot with the address of the depth-1 slot `p`.

The prefix form:

    \a

performs a raw L2 load at address expression `a`. It reads the primitive cell, word or value defined by the active L2 memory profile. It does not perform typed structural rendering by itself.

The field-follow form:

    a\field

when `a` is an ordinary Lmx reference, enumerates the ordered children of `a`
and compares each child's ShortNameId through the reverse name index. It does
not require known C layout/type metadata. When `a` is an explicitly declared
raw L2 `c.struct`/foreign typed address, the selected raw profile may instead
use its declared C layout. These are distinct operations even when they share a
surface spelling.

When more than one child has the same exact short name, `a\field` is exactly
`a\[0]field`, the first match in forward child order. `a\[n]field` selects the
`n`th exact-name match in that same forward order. Both forms designate the
selected Lmx occurrence and may participate in a read, field/index chain or
structural update.

C-profile mnemonic table:

    @: char c        # C declarator role: char *c
    const: @: T p    # C declarator role: const T *p
    @c               # C expression role: &c

    lmxParent\child  # generic Lmx child traversal by ShortNameId
    rawParent\child  # rawParent->child only for explicit c.struct/foreign type
    c.parent.child   # explicit C expression role: parent.child
    \rawParent\child # parsed as \(rawParent\child); raw role: *(rawParent->child)
    (\rawParent)\child # grouping: raw-load first, then profile-defined follow
    \slot: value     # C update role: *(slot) = value
    \\slot: value    # C update role: **(slot) = value

In expression position, field-follow and index chains are assembled before
prefix `@` and prefix `\`; parentheses override this order.

The last spelling is not the default parse. It is the explicit grouped form for a profile that needs the meaning "first raw-load `parent`, then field-follow `child` from the loaded typed address/reference".

In update/statement head position, the same leading prefix `\` may be used on
the left-hand target before `:`. The leading backslash run applies to the whole
following field-follow/index target and writes through the addressed cell:

    \slot: value
        Store `value` into the cell addressed by `slot`; C-profile role `*(slot) = value`.

    \\slot: value
        Store `value` through two address levels; C-profile role `**(slot) = value`.

This is still receiver/update semantics, not C assignment syntax. The colon
continues to introduce the Structure consumed by the update target. The active
L2 profile is responsible for type/address-depth validation.

Examples:

print: @count
print: leafs[0]
print: leafs[3]
print: \p
tree\branch: branch
root\branch: tree
print: tree\branch
print: @tree\branch
print: root\branch\branch

Meanings under the declarations in section 11.2:

    print: @count
        Print the address of the primitive storage location `count`.
        To print the primitive value `5`, write `print: count`.

    print: leafs[0]
        Perform ordinary checked array-domain indexing and print the first
        element. The Array descriptor reference is not converted to a raw backing address.

    print: leafs[3]
        Perform checked array-domain indexing and print element `3`.

    print: \p
        Raw-load the primitive cell addressed by the explicit typed-address
        primitive `p`. This operation does not apply to an ordinary Lmx array
        occurrence.

    tree\branch: branch
        Store the `PlantBed` Structure reference `branch` in field `branch` of
        the Structure referenced by `tree`.

    root\branch: tree
        Store the `PlantBed` Structure reference `tree` in field `branch` of
        the Structure referenced by `root`.

    print: tree\branch
        Read the field `branch` through Structure reference `tree`.
        If that field contains a `PlantBed` Structure reference, `print` may
        render the whole subtree reached by that occurrence.

    print: @tree\branch
        Print the address of the field slot `branch` inside the storage addressed by `tree`.
        This is parsed as `print: @(tree\branch)`.

    print: root\branch\branch
        Follow `root\branch` to its selected Lmx child/reference, then follow
        its `branch` field. If the final result is a `PlantBed` Structure,
        `print` may render that whole nested tree/graph.

    \p: 9
        Store `9` into the primitive cell addressed by the explicit typed
        address `p`.

    \\pp: 10
        Store `10` into the primitive cell reached by two address levels from `pp`.

Field-follow and index chains bind tighter than prefix `@` and prefix `\`.

Therefore:

    @tree\branch

is parsed as:

    @(tree\branch)

and means the address of the field slot `branch` inside the storage addressed by `tree`.

Prefix raw-load is valid only when its operand is an explicit typed-address
primitive. It does not reinterpret an Lmx Structure reference or Array descriptor
reference as a raw backing address. A profile that exposes an Array's backing address must do so through
a separate explicit L2 adapter; only the adapter result may be consumed by
prefix `\` or raw address arithmetic.

At L1 lowering time, a receiver such as `print` receives an Lmx occurrence and
the selected operation classifies its payload address domain. For example,
`leafs` may be printed as a whole array/string/buffer, and `tree\branch` may
yield another Lmx Structure reference for structural
rendering. The prefix raw-load form `\expr` instead consumes an explicit typed
address and produces the primitive cell/value loaded from it.

Function/subroutine address forms are strictly L2. A statically resolved L2
callable occurrence may be addressable through `@name` if the active L2 profile
exposes explicit callable-address metadata.

At L3, calls are receiver Frames resolved through compiler/profile state or
explicit graph traversal to actual callable occurrences, not raw callable
addresses. Table entries are never automatically callable occurrences or L2
addresses.

The exact L2 memory profile must define primitive cell size, address arithmetic,
alignment, raw storage duration, allocation failure, callable-address
representation and which explicit storage locations/occurrences are addressable through `@`. These choices
apply to explicit L2 machine storage such as `c.struct:`, raw indexed storage
and raw addresses. Ordinary Structure occurrences retain the Lmx header; primitives and Array
references retain their registered service-domain interpretations and owning
Message lifetime rules even when working values are projected to C.

variant 1:
Treat `@ⁿ`, prefix `@`, prefix `\`, field-follow `\field` and the postfix
index suffix `target[...]` as built-in L2 parser forms. The ordinary `[]:`
Array constructor is a compiler/profile-resolved L2/L3 receiver and is not part of
this L2-only list.

variant 2:
Treat them as bootstrap-registered L2 operators with parser support only for tokenization and precedence.

variant 3:
Allow both forms: parser-level recognition for core operators and receiver-level extension for specialized memory models.


12. Arrays, Text and Indexing

12.1. Logical Model

A language Array is described by a `{len, data}` entry in one of the N typed
service arrays. The descriptor address identifies the type by range membership;
its data pointer addresses the language array's element backing (6.5). An Lmx
field may store the descriptor reference but does not become the Array header.
The service array and the language array are distinct.

The owning element backing is rectangular, contiguous and C-compatible.
Dimensions govern indexing as for C arrays; they do not add members to the
service descriptor. There are no per-element containment `node` links.
Primitive cells and reference cells use their element type's interpretation.
Text similarly requires an explicit payload domain/profile.

12.2. L2 Lowering

A language Array reference denotes the two-value descriptor in its typed
service pool:

    Array reference -> descriptor {len, data} -> actual element backing

A raw element-storage pointer is a different value, obtained through an
explicit low-level adapter when admitted. The historical by-value `LmSlice`
carrier is not selected by the spelling of two descriptor fields: the current
reference identifies an entry whose type is known from its address range.
Lmx containment applies only to the containing Structure occurrence.

Machine encoding and optional view/resize operations must preserve the selected
descriptor/reference model, the contiguous backing and Message ownership.

12.3. Indexing

Indexing uses the selected Array descriptor and element contract (6.5). A
checked L3 operation validates coordinates against the dimensions available to
the operation; L2 raw-address indexing is a separate unchecked machine operation.

    value\[occurrence]field
    value\[occurrence]field[index]

The occurrence selector precedes the field name; postfix indexing follows the
selected field value. Thus `value\field[index]` first selects `[0]field`, then
indexes its value. It does not select the nth repeated field.

    beds[i] means index(beds, i)

For rank 1, require `0 <= i < length(beds)`; for rank N validate every coordinate
against its dimension. Out of range is `throw: Bounds`, not `assert` or a
sentinel. `None` may itself be an element and is not a bounds result.

A profile defines which multi-coordinate/partial-index forms it admits.
Rectangular rank-N elements occupy one row-major block; dimensions affect index
calculation, not the two-field descriptor. Partial views, negative indices if
admitted, text encoding and view-sharing/lifetime are operation-profile details.
No Array element gains an Lmx `node` merely because it is indexed.

Copying the descriptor reference shares the referent. An indexed write changes
that referent; rebinding a local reference does not rebind its caller.

Lowering may use computed addresses plus checks or a typed runtime accessor.
Explicit L2 raw-address access instead uses its machine contract, without
Array bounds metadata.


13. Typed Field Follow

13.1. Backslash Structural Traversal Form

Structural traversal / dereference-like access uses backslash syntax:

    result\harvest

The backslash form is a built-in parser form. For an ordinary Structure it
means actual traversal of the current Lmx tree/graph. It is not a lookup in a
runtime namespace and it is not equivalent to the L2 foreign/substrate
qualified-symbol form such as `c.printf`.

At L2, a distinct raw operation may also accept a typed foreign address. At L3,
the left side is an Lmx reference or an array/reference form explicitly admitted
by the selected runtime payload domain. In all cases, `\` implies an actual
traversable referent, not a dotted external name.

When the referent contains repeated exact short names, an unqualified
`value\field` selects the first matching occurrence, exactly `[0]field`. A particular
occurrence is selected with:

    value\[occurrence]field

The occurrence expression is evaluated once and must produce a non-negative
integer leaf admitted by the active numeric operation. Exact-name matches are
counted from zero in the Structure's logical current order. A missing exact name or an ordinal outside the
matching occurrence count is a field-access error. It is diagnosed during
translation when statically known and by the checked runtime access otherwise.
The bracket before `field` is part of field-follow; a bracket after `field`
remains postfix value indexing.

The same `\` character also has a prefix L2 raw-load form, described in section 11.3. These forms are distinguished by position:

    \address      raw L2 load from an address expression
    value\field   typed field follow/access

Possible normal form:

    field(result, harvest)

or an internal field-follow expression Structure equivalent to it.

Inside a generated method, reserved `node` denotes the already supplied
`callable->node`: the concrete Structure containing the selected callable.
Therefore `node\field` begins ordinary child traversal at that Structure and
performs no additional `implicit_node->node` hop. There is no implicit outward
lexical search. Access to a still higher containment ancestor must use an
explicitly resolved reference or a separately specified parent-navigation
receiver; Revision 2 does not reinterpret repeated field segments as an
automatic lexical climb. The exact source spelling for that explicit ancestor
operation remains open; only immediate `node\field` is fixed here.

13.2. Lowering

The baseline lowering of:

    result\harvest

is generic child traversal. It enumerates the branch's current ordered children,
obtains each candidate's ShortNameId from the sole reverse side table and
selects occurrence zero (or the requested explicit occurrence). A constant
source name may lower to a constant ShortNameId. The runtime tree may have been
changed or produced by `merge`, so the translator must not assume a fixed
per-name C member offset.

When the left value is already a resolved direct reference, traversal starts at
that value. When the source supplies an arbitrary textual complete path, the
operation additionally requires an explicit root and resolves every segment
against the current tree. Full paths are never cached in Lmx nodes.

A later optimizer may replace a generic traversal with a direct Lmx pointer or
validated offset only when it proves equivalence across every mutation and
runtime merge observable at that site. Failure of that proof selects generic
traversal; it is not a source error.

The language permits the occurrence selector to be an arbitrary expression and
therefore permits runtime occurrence selection. The current hosted C bootstrap
projection implements occurrence-qualified field-follow only when the selector
is a compile-time-known non-negative decimal integer. If it cannot prove such
an ordinal, it must reject only that projection with a diagnostic such as:

    trans hosted C backend limitation: occurrence-qualified field follow requires a compile-time non-negative decimal ordinal

This is a historical hosted-backend limitation, not a restriction on
Lingvamyxa source semantics. The Revision-2 runtime must implement dynamic
occurrence selection. A statically or dynamically out-of-range ordinal is a
field-access error; it must not silently select another occurrence.

!!!
Apart from the fixed missing-name and out-of-range behavior of the occurrence
selector above, some field-follow failure semantics are not fixed. A final
source-semantic rule must define behavior for dynamic Structures and
nullable/empty references, including which failures are runtime errors or
receiver-defined diagnostics.
!!!

---ANSI C---
Possible field-follow lowering variants:

variant 1:
Ordinary Structures lower to generic Lmx traversal or a proven equivalent
optimization. Generated per-name C struct-field access is prohibited.

variant 2:
Explicit raw L2 `c.struct`/typed-address layouts may lower to direct
pointer-field access in ANSI C. This is foreign substrate access, not the
representation of an ordinary Structure.

variant 3:
Arbitrary textual paths lower to root-based checked traversal using the
Lmx-pointer-to-ShortNameId index.
---ANSI C---


14. Update, Return Structures and Destructuring

14.1. Plain-Name Rebind and Addressable Update Instead of `=` Assignment

The native structural core does not use `=` for assignment.

A declaration with initialization is written through a declaration-context constructor:

    int: a 3

A later plain-name rebind or an update of an addressable field/index location is
written by placing that target in head/update position:

    a: 4

Example:

    int: a 3
    print: a
    a: 4
    print: a

The first `print` observes `3`. The second `print` observes `4`.

An occurrence-qualified field-follow is an addressable field location:

    value\[0]field: replacement

This updates only occurrence 0 of the exact name `field`. The unqualified
`value\field: replacement` updates the same `[0]` occurrence. Neither form appends
a new field occurrence.

When a plain name such as `a` appears in executable update position, it selects
the current activation value under section 21.3. An own node-backed local becomes
dirty; a declared argument or inherited/fallback dynamic copy changes only in
this activation. It does not directly mutate the caller or the `node\[0]a`
fallback. To mutate graph state immediately, or to mutate any location other
than the current method's own backing occurrence through its later dirty
checkpoint, the program writes an explicit path such as `node\a: 4` or
`reference\a: 4`. A receiver that explicitly defines structural
append/replacement may apply `implements(new old Consumer)`, but it neither
creates a runtime Namespace nor changes occurrence order. If a same-name child
is appended, the existing first child remains structural `[0]`.

When the head is an addressable field/index location, the selected receiver may
mutate that graph/storage location. A function, subroutine or receiver may
consume the same surface form as a call after compiler resolution identifies a
call-capable activation binding, or runtime structural traversal/current
activation yields an actual callable Lmx occurrence. The selected method record
must match the exact expected Consumer signature. These cases are selected by
the current activation/real graph and consuming operation, not a second
assignment grammar or reified runtime Namespace.

For example, `i: 5` is parsed as a Frame with head `i` and argument `5`. A
translator resolves its binding and expected consumption while lowering source.
An interpreter uses the equivalent private activation frame for a bare target;
it starts from a supplied containing node or explicit root only for an explicit
structural path. That private frame realizes the same dynamic-value rules as C
arguments/locals and is not a source-visible Namespace object.

A bounded Structure or compact Frame in an argument/update position remains one
Structure after parsing. A nested compact Frame is lowered as a call only if its
head binding/value or an explicit structural link yields a function, subroutine
or registered receiver with the exact expected signature. For example, in
`print(1, 2, get(1,2))`, `get(1,2)` is just the third field for P0; it is
generated as a call only after `get` resolves as callable during lowering or at
the typed runtime selection point.

This is declared head consumption, not automatic execution of every Structure in
an argument list.

At L2, an addressable mutation may lower to a direct storage write according to
the L2 memory model. At L3, addressable mutation is receiver-defined over
fields, handles and Structures. A plain-name rebind may be optimized to target
storage only when the observable ordered-occurrence semantics is preserved.

?
Initialization and addressable-mutation rules are not specified in full in this
document. Ordered child selection/update is defined by sections 7.3.1, 7.3.2
and this section; a future execution-model specification may refine storage
mutation without adding a runtime Namespace or replacing `[0]`-is-first.
?

14.2. Return Structures

A function return value is one logical source-level Lmx reference, regardless
of its classified payload domain. If the return is an `fm` Structure, updating
the fields of its existing
return Structure and returning one Structure reference admitted by
`implements(returnedValue declaredReturn returnConsumers)` are source-semantically equivalent.

A function may return a Structure with ordered and/or semantic named fields.

Example return signature:

    (f32 harvest, f32 redApples, f32 greenApples)

Canonical positional structural return into an existing `fm` result Structure:

    return: bed\area 0 0

Named return variables may also be updated before an explicit terminal value return:

    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
    return: harvest redApples greenApples

The canonical return mechanism for an `fn` is `return: value`. In a valid
terminal trailer position, `return: value` also closes the `fn` source frame.
Named return variables are child occurrences of an already existing result
Structure. The field updates occur before the return control exit; return itself
only forwards the result reference.

14.3. Destructuring

Destructuring syntax using `=` is not part of the current native structural core.

The semantic operation still exists: a produced Structure may be retained as one
Lmx value, stored in a compiler service temporary, or used to update target
occurrences from its fields by name or position.

Possible explicit receiver form:

unpack: toEat(myFilter, Monday)
    harvest
    redApples
    greenApples
end: unpack

Conceptual meaning:

    tmp receives toEat(myFilter, Monday)
    harvest receives tmp\harvest
    redApples receives tmp\redApples
    greenApples receives tmp\greenApples

If the produced Structure has no field names, a receiver-defined positional unpack form may map fields by order.

?
The canonical destructuring/update syntax is not specified. A final execution-model specification must define whether destructuring is written through an `unpack` receiver, through target-list syntax without `=`, or through another explicit structural form.
?

---ANSI C---
A translator may implement destructuring by retaining the produced Lmx reference
in a compiler-generated service temporary and then generating field updates one
by one. The temporary is not a source local, hidden namespace or closure field.

variant 1:
For a compile-time-proven invariant graph shape, generated code may replace
generic traversal with validated direct Lmx references/offsets. It must not use
a generated per-source-name C layout or attached descriptor.

variant 2:
Otherwise generated code reads fields through generic runtime Lmx traversal.
---ANSI C---

15. Structural Grammar Sketch

This grammar sketch describes the structural parser only. It does not define final receiver semantics.

15.0. P0 Source-Event Automaton

This subsection is normative for P0 structure recognition. The grammar below is
only a compact readable projection of the same machine.

P0 is one recursive source-event automaton over source positions:

    P0 = (S, E, delta, O)

where:

    S  is the parser state;
    E  is the stream of source events produced by the source scanner;
    delta consumes one event and updates the state;
    O  is the produced parser tree with source spans and node flags.

There is no separate line parser, brace parser, bracket parser or expression
parser at P0. Physical lines are input fragments, not containers in the
abstract tree. Parentheses, index brackets and Mix braces all enter nested P0
contexts; their difference is the channel where the resulting Structure is
used.

State:

    S.position
        current source position and absolute span base.

    S.lexical_mode
        SOURCE
        C_STRING
        EXACT_IDENTIFIER
        PYTHON_RAW_QUOTE
        BLOCK_STRING
        RAW_COMMENT
        LINE_COMMENT

    S.level_state
        the normalized source level state. Canonical dotted spelling maps
        leading dots to integer source levels. Relaxed indentation maps
        indentation columns to the same integer source levels through the
        indentation-stack rule.

    S.form_stack
        ROOT(level 0)
        VERTICAL(level K)
        SHORT(head at level N, implicit argument level N + 1)
        BOUNDED_PAREN(base level B)
        BOUNDED_INDEX(base level B)
        BOUNDED_MIX(local base level 0)

    S.output_cursor
        the current ordinary Structure insertion point, plus the current
        source span being attached to the next node.

Events:

    LEVEL(K)
        next source item is at normalized source level K.

    ATOM(value)
        ordinary token/value after operator splitting and payload recognition.

    HEAD(value)
        head token before `:`.

    COLON
        opens a short form for the preceding HEAD.

    NEWLINE
        physical newline separator.

    COMMA_SEPARATOR
        comma separator at the current scan depth. It separates fields like
        whitespace, but it has no indentation/source-level effect.

    SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR
        semicolon separator/trailer at the current scan depth. It may terminate
        the current open short form.

    OPEN_PAREN / CLOSE_PAREN
        `(` and matching `)` bounded Structure delimiters.

    OPEN_INDEX / CLOSE_INDEX
        `[` and matching `]` bounded index/access delimiters.

    OPEN_MIX / CLOSE_MIX
        `{` and matching `}` Mix bounded form delimiters.

    TAIL_CUTTER(candidate)
        a candidate visible close/cut form, such as `end`, `return`, `until`
        or a dash-fence delimiter. Candidate status is later validated by the
        current receiver/profile context.

    EOF
        end of source.

Lexical shielding:

    If S.lexical_mode is not SOURCE, braces, parentheses, brackets, commas,
    semicolons, colons, dots, indentation and tail-cutter words are payload
    characters for that lexical mode. They do not produce structural events
    until that lexical mode closes.

    C_STRING closes by its C string delimiter rules.
    EXACT_IDENTIFIER closes by its exact identifier delimiter rules.
    PYTHON_RAW_QUOTE closes by its matching raw quote run.
    BLOCK_STRING closes by its matching column-0 `===...` fence.
    RAW_COMMENT closes by its matching column-0 `***...` fence.
    LINE_COMMENT closes at physical newline.

    Therefore a `}` inside a string, exact identifier, raw string, block string
    or comment inside a Mix bounded form does not close that Mix bounded form.

Level transitions:

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K + 1))
        opens one Structure field under the current source parent. Portable P0
        source-level syntax opens block structure by one atomic level increase.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K))
        produces a sibling item at the current source level, unless the current
        scan depth is controlled by an open short/bounded form.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K - 1))
        closes the current visible vertical Structure by the ordinary one-step
        source-level cut.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K - M), M > 1)
        is not ordinary block syntax. It is admitted only when the current
        source event is consumed by a valid tail-cutter layer for that context.

    An empty physical line is a level-0 boundary event. It may close an open
    level-1 vertical tail by the ordinary level decrease, but it is not a
    same-level delimiter below level 1.

Short-form transitions:

    HEAD COLON at source level N creates:

        Frame(head, BodyStructure at implicit argument level N + 1)

    Inline tail fields after `:` are appended to that BodyStructure at the
    implicit argument level N + 1.

    NEWLINE inside an open short form performs the synthetic short-form level
    step from N + 1 back to N. This step is only a separator step. It is not by
    itself the trailer of the short form.

    After that newline:

        if the next source item appears at level N + 1, the atomic level
        increase N -> N + 1 opens a new Structure field in the same short-form
        argument sequence;

        if the next source item appears at level N or smaller, the short form
        is complete and the next item is handled by ordinary sibling, cut or
        receiver-trailer rules.

    COMMA at the current scan depth separates fields and does not terminate an
    open short form. A single comma does not append an empty field. Repeated
    explicit separators may materialize positional skips where the consuming
    profile admits them.

    SEMICOLON at the current scan depth terminates the current open short form.
    It does not append a field to that short form. After terminating the short
    form, it separates the next source item/argument segment at the same scan
    depth. If no short form is open at that scan depth, it is an explicit
    source-item/argument-segment separator or a cosmetic trailing marker
    according to the surrounding context. Repeated explicit separators may
    materialize positional skips where the consuming profile admits them.

Bounded-form transitions:

    OPEN_PAREN starts a nested BOUNDED_PAREN context. Its contents are parsed by
    the same P0 source/token rules, with source-level composition measured
    relative to the bounded opener. CLOSE_PAREN closes that context. If a short
    form is still open inside the bounded context, it is completed as part of
    closing the bounded context.

    OPEN_INDEX after a target starts a nested BOUNDED_INDEX context. Its
    contents are parsed by the same P0 source/token rules. CLOSE_INDEX closes
    that context and attaches the resulting Structure as an index/access suffix
    to the preceding target or to a receiver form that admits bracket syntax.

    OPEN_MIX starts a nested BOUNDED_MIX context for a Mix bounded form `{...}`.
    Its contents are parsed by
    the same P0 source/token rules in a local level-0 context. CLOSE_MIX closes
    that context. The resulting Structure is stored as a Mix-flagged parser
    node with its source position/span. Ordinary structural consumers may skip
    Mix-flagged nodes; document/Mix consumers project them into active overlay
    intervals and marks.

    Mix bounded forms are then anchored to the surrounding source stream.
    Inline/tail Mix is recorded in the current field sequence. A leading Mix
    prefix before an ordinary item is recorded at that following item's source
    level before the item itself. A column-0 Mix-only physical line is queued
    as a margin Mix event and attached to the next ordinary LMX source item;
    several such lines keep their order. A non-margin standalone Mix line uses
    the ordinary source level of that line.

    A matching closing delimiter line may return to the opener level. The
    closer token itself is the bounded-form close event, not an ordinary
    sibling source item.

Tail-cutter transitions:

    Tail-cutters are not magic words in P0 by spelling alone. A token such as
    `return` or `until` becomes a tail-cutter only when it appears at a source
    event where a valid receiver/profile context admits that close/cut role.
    Otherwise it remains an ordinary item/operator/receiver token for later
    semantic processing.

    `end: name` is a visible structural close form. Its argument is not
    evaluated as a runtime expression. It is compared with the admitted close
