    targets supplied by the receiver being closed: normally the privileged
    `name` argument as the canonical close target, and the receiver/head key
    as an admitted structural alternative.

    `return`, `return: value`, `until` and dash-fence delimiter forms are
    members of the same tail-cutter family, but each is admitted only in the
    receiver/profile positions defined for it.

EOF transition:

    EOF closes the root context after all open structures that may be closed by
    EOF have been completed. Any remaining open bounded form or lexical mode is
    a syntax error.

Output invariants:

    P0 constructs Structure, Frame, Atom/value and Mix-flagged nodes with source
    spans. It does not execute Structures, evaluate expressions, resolve
    functions, declare variables or decide type semantics.

    A named Structure is translated or interpreted as a call/function/subroutine
    only when a later semantic layer resolves its head to an explicit callable
    Lmx occurrence, provider link or structural path. An anonymous Structure
    remains Structure data until a receiver consumes it under a receiver-defined
    rule. No runtime namespace is part of P0 output or later invocation.

    Any search for a matching `)`, `]` or `}` must use the same lexical modes as
    P0 itself. There is no separate opaque boundary walker that merely counts
    characters.

    program             ::= { empty_line | physical_item }

    physical_item       ::= raw_file_comment_block
                          | block_string_literal
                          | logical_line

    raw_file_comment_block
                        ::= raw_comment_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                              raw_ignored_line*
                            raw_comment_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            where 3 <= N <= 80

    raw_comment_fence(N)
                        ::= exactly N "*" characters at column 0

    block_string_literal
                        ::= block_string_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                              block_string_content_line*
                            block_string_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            where 3 <= N <= 80

    block_string_fence(N)
                        ::= exactly N "=" characters at column 0

    trailing_horizontal_space
                        ::= { " " | tab }

    horizontal_space    ::= " " | tab

    logical_line        ::= source_level line_content newline

    indentation         ::= { "." | " " | tab }

    source_level        ::= integer source level assigned by the
                            source-level normalization prepass

    source-level normalization prepass:
                            maps the physical source spelling to source-level
                            item/level events
                            before the structural grammar consumes it;
                            in canonical dotted notation, source_level is the
                            count of leading dot characters;
                            in relaxed indentation notation, source_level is
                            computed by the indentation-stack rule.

    empty_line          ::= trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            level-0 structural boundary event;
                            closes an open level-1 vertical tail by ordinary
                            source-level decrease, but is not a same-level
                            delimiter below level 1

    line_content        ::= dot_only_marker [ comment_body ]
                          | comment_body
                          | disabled_body [ comment_body ]
                          | line_item_sequence [ comment_body ]

    dot_only_marker     ::= empty content after indentation
                            valid only if source_level > 0;
                            may delimit/open an anonymous Structure at that level

    comment_body        ::= "#" ignored_until_newline

    disabled_body       ::= "%" enabled_line_body

    enabled_line_body   ::= line_item_sequence

    compact_frame       ::= name "(" [ argument_list ] ")"

    line_frame          ::= source_level line_item_sequence newline

    line_item_sequence  ::= source_item
                            { semicolon_segment_separator source_item }
                            [ semicolon_segment_separator ]

    source_item         ::= colon_frame_body
                          | nullary_line_body
                          | structural_value

    colon_frame_body    ::= head ":" [ line_arguments ]

    semicolon_segment_separator
                         ::= ";"

    // Semicolon terminates the current open short form/source item at the
    // current scan depth and separates the next source item or argument
    // segment. Comma is an ordinary field separator and never terminates the
    // current open short form.

    head                ::= bare_identifier
                          | exact_identifier
                          | symbolic_head

    nullary_line_body   ::= name
                            valid in executable statement position only when
                            `name` resolves to a zero-argument consumable binding:
                            registered nullary operator/receiver or visible zero-argument fn/sub

    line_arguments      ::= short_tail

    short_tail          ::= short_field
                            { short_tail_field_separator short_field }
                            [ "," ]

    short_tail_field_separator
                         ::= horizontal_space+
                          | ","

    short_field         ::= colon_frame_body
                          | structural_value

    // In ordinary physical short-line form, comma separates fields in the
    // current short form. Semicolon is not a short-tail field separator: it
    // terminates the current short form and exposes the following segment at
    // the surrounding scan depth.
    // Physical newline is not a short-form trailer; after newline, the next
    // source-level event decides whether the open frame receives more fields
    // or is completed by level rules.
    // Operator-token consumption is deferred to a later consumer.

    argument_list       ::= bounded_item_sequence

    bounded_item_sequence
                         ::= bounded_item { bounded_item_separator bounded_item }
                             [ bounded_item_separator ]

    bounded_item_separator
                         ::= horizontal_space+
                          | comma_field_separator
                          | semicolon_segment_separator
                          | newline/source-level composition

    comma_field_separator
                         ::= ","

    bounded_item        ::= colon_frame_body
                          | nullary_line_body
                          | structural_value

    // Inside bounded Structures, short colon-forms may appear inline.
    // Horizontal whitespace separates simple bounded fields only when no
    // short colon-form is open. Inside an open short colon-form it remains
    // the short-tail field separator. Comma is also a short-tail field
    // separator and never closes the short form. Semicolon terminates an open
    // short form at any scan depth and then separates the following bounded
    // item/argument segment.
    // Newline/source-level composition is not a short-form
    // trailer by itself; the following source level determines continuation
    // or closure. Inside a bounded form that continues across a physical
    // newline, the next physical line must remain inside that bounded form:
    // it must be at the corresponding deeper dotted source level or, in
    // relaxed notation, at an indentation column deeper than the line that
    // opened the bounded form. A line whose next source token is the matching
    // RPAREN/RBRACKET is the closing line and may return to the opener level.
    // The matching RPAREN/RBRACKET closes the bounded Structure; if a short
    // form is still open inside it, that short form is completed as part of
    // closing the bounded context.

    bounded_structure   ::= "(" [ argument_list | vertical_argument_list ] ")"

    source_fragment     ::= P0 source parsed in a local level-0 context

    mix_structure       ::= "{" source_fragment "}"
                            parsed by the same P0 source/token rules in a
                            local level-0 context and stored as a Mix-flagged
                            Structure node

    index_access_suffix ::= "[" [ argument_list | vertical_argument_list ] "]"
                            attached to a preceding target by an expression,
                            update or receiver profile that admits index/access
                            syntax

    vertical_argument_list
                         ::= newline source_line { source_line }
                            where each contained source_line follows the same
                            source-level rules as ordinary vertical composition

    structural_value
                         ::= compact_frame
                          | bounded_structure
                          | mix_structure
                          | atom_or_value

Square brackets in the portable core are not a free-standing replacement for
parentheses. `target[...]` is bounded index/access syntax attached to a preceding
target or to a receiver form that explicitly admits bracket syntax. `[]:` in
head position is a symbolic receiver spelling, such as an Array
constructor, not the same thing as an index suffix. A standalone `[a b]` has no
portable core meaning unless a profile explicitly admits it.

The vertical Structure tree is produced by source levels and explicit visible delimiters: following source items at one-step smaller source level as level delimiters, canonical dotted delimiter lines, relaxed dash-fence lines, and admitted source-level tail-cutter forms such as `end:` or terminal return where valid. The physical newline separator is not itself the trailer; it only helps expose the next source item and its level.

For a source item at level N, vertical child arguments are written at level N + 1. A following source item at level N + 1 is one positional child item of that frame/Structure. In a short colon-form, the inline tail fields are also treated as fields at the implicit argument level N + 1. A following source item deeper than N + 1 belongs to the immediately preceding child item only if that child item opens a Structure. Scalar literals and identifier values do not become headers merely because a deeper item follows.

A canonical dotted delimiter line or relaxed dash-fence line at level K may delimit an anonymous positional Structure at level K. If it is followed by source items at level K + 1, it opens such an anonymous Structure. The next delimiter at level K closes the previous anonymous Structure and may open another one when followed by deeper items. A following source item at level K - 1 closes that anonymous Structure by the ordinary one-step cut. A larger cut is not ordinary P0 syntax and requires an admitted tail-cutter layer.

An empty colon Frame is not rejected by P0 merely because its body has zero
fields. It is still a structural item. The active receiver, validator,
translator or profile decides whether a nullary colon Frame is meaningful in
that position. For example, a profile may reject `level0:` followed by sibling
`level1:` when `level0` requires a body, or reject `level0(level1:)` when the
inner empty Frame is not an admitted argument. Those are receiver/profile
diagnostics, not a separate P0 grammar category.

This grammar is intentionally incomplete around full update semantics, full indexing, full field-follow access, type constructors, literals and relation forms because those areas require final design decisions. Native structural grammar does not include `=` assignment, `=` named-field syntax, bare `end`, `end <target>`, bare non-nullary receiver/callable calls or a built-in `case` form. The standalone dash-fence line is part of vertical structure syntax as a nameless delimiter: it may open an anonymous positional Structure when followed by a deeper item, separate sibling anonymous Structures, or close/cut an anonymous/implicit section.

Ordinary line arguments are whitespace-separated short-tail fields. A parser must not use receiver signatures to insert hidden nesting while parsing `line_arguments`.

Comma and semicolon have different P0 roles at the current scan depth. Comma is an ordinary explicit field separator, like whitespace without indentation/source-level effects; it may add another field to an open short tail. Semicolon is the explicit separator/trailer: for a short form, it terminates that short form and never adds another field to the open short tail. When no short form is open at the current scan depth, comma is optional conventional separator notation and semicolon may act as an explicit source-item separator or trailing marker. A trailing comma or semicolon with no following item is accepted as a cosmetic trailing marker at any block level, including file/root level 0. A comma or semicolon inside a deeper bounded Structure or compact Frame belongs to that deeper form.

When a semicolon terminates a short form, it terminates/materializes the current
headless argument segment at that scan depth. If the neighboring segment has no
explicit head of its own, P0 may expose it as a separate headless Structure.
This preserves the source-item boundary for higher profiles. A
declaration/profile layer may then consume those headless Structures as repeats
of a preceding repeatable head, while an expression layer may reject them or
treat them as ordinary neighboring Structures. P0 does not silently append the
headless item to the terminated short tail and does not use namespace lookup to
decide whether the item is a call or a repeated declaration.

Repeated explicit separators at the same scan depth may materialize an empty
positional field. This is not specific to semicolon: `a,,c`, `a;;c`, `a,;c`
and `a;,c` all contain an explicit positional skip between `a` and `c` when the
current bounded/profile context admits empty positional fields. A leading
separator may similarly skip the first positional field. A single trailing
separator remains a cosmetic trailing marker. The parser records such a skip as
a positional-skip field; a receiver, validator or translator may consume it
only if the corresponding positional argument has an admitted default form.
Otherwise it must report an error rather than silently compacting the argument
list.

Structural grammar has no separate anonymous-function syntax. A bounded
Structure, compact Frame or vertical anonymous Structure remains Structure data.
The parser never marks a Structure as executable merely because it is nested
inside arguments. Translation or interpretation of that Structure happens only
when the active receiver consumes it under a receiver-defined rule, or when the
translator resolves a compact Frame head to an explicit callable Lmx occurrence,
provider link or structural path. No runtime namespace participates in this
decision.

Labels are not a separate structural grammar category. A source line such as
`outerLoop:` is a colon frame with an empty inline tail and a required following
vertical block. The active receiver/profile decides whether that frame creates
a nullary callable/control Lmx occurrence in the current containing Structure,
a control-target link, both, or an error. A later bare line such as `outerLoop`
is also not special label grammar; in executable statement position the
translator may resolve it to that explicit occurrence/link. Scope is represented
by the actual Structure tree, not by a runtime binding overlay.

15.1. Structural Semantics in EBNF Form

The following EBNF describes the structural-semantic normal form produced by the
parser. It is not a separate surface syntax and it does not assign binding,
variable, type, field, atom, description, operation or execution semantics. Those meanings
are assigned later by receiver consumption, explicit description Structures and the active
level/profile.

The notation uses `Structure(...)` and `Frame(...)` as semantic constructors:

    SourceFile
        ::= RootBlock

    RootBlock
        ::= Block(0)

    Block(K)
        ::= Structure({ BlockElement(K) })

    BlockElement(K)
        ::= FrameElement(K)
         |  ValueElement(K)
         |  ExpressionElement(K)
         |  AnonymousStructure(K)

    FrameElement(K)
        ::= Frame(Head, BodyStructure, FrameTrailer)

    Head
        ::= BareIdentifier
         |  ExactIdentifier
         |  SymbolicHead
         |  ProfileHeadPath

    BodyStructure
        ::= Structure({ Field })

    Field
        ::= FrameElement(current)
         |  ValueElement(current)
         |  ExpressionElement(current)
         |  BoundedStructure
         |  CompactFrame
         |  AnonymousStructure(current)

    ValueElement(K)
        ::= NumericLiteral
         |  StringLiteral
         |  BareIdentifier
         |  ExactIdentifier

    ExpressionElement(K)
        ::= ExpressionStructure

    CompactFrame
        ::= Frame(Head, BoundedBody, RPAREN_BOUNDED)

    BoundedStructure
        ::= Structure({ BoundedElement }, RPAREN_BOUNDED | RBRACKET_BOUNDED)

    BoundedElement
        ::= FrameElement(bounded)
         |  ValueElement(bounded)
         |  ExpressionElement(bounded)
         |  CompactFrame
         |  BoundedStructure

    AnonymousStructure(K)
        ::= Structure({ BlockElement(K + 1) })

This semantic EBNF is read with these structural rules:

    ShortTail
        ::= Field { (HorizontalSpace+ | COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR) Field }
            [ COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR ]

    BoundedBody
        ::= [ BoundedElement { BoundedSeparator BoundedElement }
             [ BoundedSeparator ] ]

    BoundedSeparator
        ::= HorizontalSpace+
         |  COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR
         |  SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR
         |  NewlineSourceComposition

    COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR
        ::= COMMA_SEPARATOR

    SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR
        ::= SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR

    SEMICOLON_SHORT
        ::= SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR

    FrameTrailer
        ::= SEMICOLON_SHORT
         |  RPAREN_BOUNDED
         |  RBRACKET_BOUNDED
         |  LEVEL_DROP
         |  DELIM_NAMELESS
         |  RECEIVER_TRAILER
         |  EOF_TRAILER

    RECEIVER_TRAILER
        ::= END_NAMED
         |  UNTIL_CLOSE
         |  TERMINAL_RETURN
         |  ProfileTrailer

Comma and semicolon semantics:

    COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR appends/separates fields in the open ShortTail. It
    never terminates the current open short form.

    SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR first acts as SEMICOLON_SHORT if a short form
    is open at the current scan depth. It never appends a field to that
    ShortTail. After the short form is materialized, the same semicolon
    separates the following source item or argument segment.

    When no short form is open at the current scan depth, comma may act as
    ordinary separator notation and semicolon may act as an explicit source-item
    separator or cosmetic trailing marker.

    Therefore:

        call(cast: Type value, other)

    has one semantic argument segment for `call`, because `other` is still a
    field of the open `cast` short form. To end `cast` and supply `other` as the
    next argument segment, use semicolon:

        call(cast: Type value; other)

Physical newline semantics:

    Physical newline is not a short-form trailer. It ends the physical line.
    If the next source item is at the open short form's implicit argument
    level, that item continues the open short form. If the next source item is
    at the short form's own source level or smaller, the short form is complete
    and ordinary sibling/level-close rules apply.

Bounded delimiter semantics:

    RPAREN_BOUNDED closes the current parenthesized BoundedStructure or
    CompactFrame. RBRACKET_BOUNDED closes the current bracketed
    BoundedStructure. If a short form is open inside that bounded form, that
    short form is completed as part of closing the bounded context, and then
    the bounded form itself is closed.

Source-level semantics:

    A line at source level K + 1 is a child field of the current item at level K.
    A line at source level K - 1 closes the current open Structure by the
    ordinary one-step cut. Larger decreases require an admitted tail-cutter
    layer.
    A delimiter at level K may open an AnonymousStructure(K) when followed by
    items at K + 1, or close/separate the current anonymous or implicit section
    at level K.

Receiver semantics:

    Frame(Head, BodyStructure, FrameTrailer) is still only data after parsing.
    The receiver named by Head consumes BodyStructure and assigns semantic roles
    to positional fields, anonymous Structures, named child Frames, labels,
    operators, declarations, explicit model descriptions, message/table data and control
    trailers. The parser does not infer those meanings from spelling alone.

16. Canonical Examples

16.1. Hello World

Source:

print: "Hello World"

Full form:

L3:
    print: "Hello World"
end: L3

Normal form:

L3(
    print("Hello World")
)

Shortest form:

print: Hello World

`Hello` and `World` are accepted only if the active L3 `print` receiver and lexical rules allow bare atoms/symbols in that position.

16.2. Function with Structural Return

Source:

fm: estimateHarvest (PlantBed(bed)) (f32(harvest), f32(redApples), f32(greenApples))
    if: bed\crop = Apples
        return: bed\area * 1.7 bed\area * 1.7 bed\area * 1.1
return: bed\area 0 0

Semantic outline:

    `fn` consumes a definition Structure.
    `PlantBed: bed` constructs an Lmx subtree if `PlantBed` resolves through
        compiler work state to an admitted Structure recipe, callable/provider
        link or explicit structural operation. No runtime namespace or attached
        descriptor is involved.
    The return Structure has three named fields.
    `if` consumes condition and body Structures.
    The inner `return:` exits with the Apple-specific structural result.
    The final `return:` is terminal return. It supplies the default structural result and closes the function definition.

16.3. Explicit Return with Named Return Variables

Source:

fm: estimateHarvest (PlantBed(bed)) (f32(harvest), f32(redApples), f32(greenApples))
    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
return: harvest redApples greenApples

Semantic outline:

    Named return fields are Lmx child occurrences in the selected call/result
        Structure.
    Head/update position updates those concrete occurrences or their explicit
        graph links.
    Terminal `return:` exits, returns their current values as one return Structure and closes the function definition.

16.3.1. Recursive Function with Terminal Return

Source:

fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)
    if: n <= 1
        return: 1
return: n * factorial(n - 1)

Semantic outline:

    `return: 1` is a runtime return inside the `if` branch.
    In expression position, `factorial(n - 1)` is a recursive call.
    The final `return:` supplies the recursive result and closes the function definition.

16.4. Strings and Comments: Examples from tests

These parser fixtures show payload boundaries, not runtime method behavior.
The common four-space presentation indent is not source; remove it when copying.

`tests/python_string_in_paren.lmx`: parentheses, `#` and runs of four quotes
inside the triple-quoted payload are data.

    f("""a
    ) # not syntax
    """" stays data
    b""", '''c
    ( # not syntax
    '''' stays data
    d''', tail)

`tests/python_string_inline_multiline_tail.lmx`: fields after the closing
delimiter continue the same argument list.

    print: """hello
    """ world
    print: '''hello
    ''' world

`tests/block_string_equals_fence.lmx`: a shorter equals fence is literal content.

    doc:
    ====
    alpha
    === literal content
    beta
    ====
    end: doc

`tests/raw_comment.lmx`: raw comments may contain text that would be malformed
source outside the comment.

    root: before
    ****
    ignored " bad
    . broken:
    ****
    root: after

`tests/string_continuation_indent.lmx`: C-style line continuation removes the
line break, but preserves the four indentation spaces between `alpha` and `beta`.

    doc:
        text: "alpha\
        beta"
    end: doc

16.5. Loop and Catch Payloads: Examples from tests

Excerpt from `tests/trans_for_loop.lm2`:

    int: continued 0
    for: int(j, 0) (j < 3) j++
        if: j = 1
            continue
        continued: continued + j

The result is 2. `continue` still executes the `j++` step.

Excerpts from `tests/trans_throw_catch.lm2`:

    fn: parseOrZero (int(n)) int
        throws: NonPositive
        if: n <= 0
            throw: NonPositive(n)
        return: n
    end: parseOrZero

In the caller:

    int: payload 0
    ---
        payload: parseOrZero(-4)
        catch: NonPositive (int: bad)
            payload: bad

The failed call does not assign its result. The explicit catch parameter
`bad` receives -4, and the catch body stores it in `payload`. Physical hosted
failure carriers are not part of this example's contract.

16.6. Shared Referent: Example from tests

Excerpt from `tests/trans_structure_graph_nested.lm2`. Precondition:
`a\child` and `b\child` reference the same Structure `p`.

    a\child\x: 7
    if: p\x != 7 || a\child\x != 7 || b\child\x != 7
        return: 3

All three paths observe 7. The explicit path changes the shared referent,
not either caller's reference binding. The old fixture's descriptor/layout
prelude is not required or adopted by this excerpt.


17. Summary Table

    f(a, b)                    compact Frame named f
    f: a b                     line command Frame named f with whitespace-separated short-tail fields
    f: a, b                    f receives a and b; comma is an ordinary explicit field separator
    f: a; b                    f receives only a; semicolon terminates the short form, so b is the next source item at the current level
    f: 1 2                     ordinary short-tail fields, equivalent to f(1, 2)
    f: / child lines at . / end: f
                                canonical dotted vertical Frame named f; vertical fields are at source level N + 1
    f: / indented child lines / end: f
                                relaxed indentation vertical Frame named f
    (a b), (a, b)              bounded Structure; comma is optional conventional notation
    ()                         present empty bounded Structure; not a positional skip
    name: value                ordinary nested Structure, often consumed as a setting/semantic field by receiver
    name:                      empty colon Frame; P0 keeps it, receiver/profile decides whether it is valid
    leading dots               canonical visible source level units in the leading indentation zone
    indentation                relaxed source level units before ordinary source items
    dotted delimiter line      canonical anonymous Structure delimiter; may open, separate or close at its source level
    ---...                     dash-fence source-level marker, 3..80 `-` characters; materializes level K without an ordinary receiver item;
                               close/open/separator behavior follows from neighboring source levels
    smaller-level source line  ordinary one-step source-level cut/trailer; larger cuts require an admitted tail-cutter layer
    same-parent-level line     ordinary source-level cut for the previous child frame and start of the next sibling item
    empty line                 level-0 structural boundary; closes an open level-1 vertical tail
    #                          line comment marker; ignores text to the end of the physical line
    %                          disabled item/block marker; syntax is still parsed and checked
    {...}                      Mix bounded form; inline/tail belongs to the current item, leading prefix belongs to the following item,
                               column-0 Mix-only lines anchor to the next ordinary LMX source item
    ***...                     column-0 raw file comment fence, 3..80 `*` characters; closing fence must have the same length; syntax inside is ignored
    ===...                     column-0 block string fence, 3..80 `=` characters; closing fence must have the same length
    end: name                  explicit named tail-cutter for a visible frame/entity/label block; not the only close mechanism
    L2/L3                      second-translator level frames; L1 uses its separate translator
    sub                        receiver that constructs a callable Lmx occurrence and emits its real C body
    fn                         receiver that constructs a callable Lmx occurrence and emits its real C body
    return                     executable-body return receiver/operator; also terminal executable close at definition level
    descriptor                 ordinary explicit description-data spelling if a profile registers it; never an attached type/namespace entry
    operator                   proposed explicit operation-description form; compiler bootstrap may read explicit legacy Tables as work data
    if                         registered receiver, semantics depend on level
    match                      proposed receiver; not registered by the current bootstrap; no built-in `case` syntax
    merge                      runtime Structure operation; when reached, creates a fresh root and copies operand children/subtrees in order with node rebasing
    join                       registered Table receiver; source-marked joins are implemented for translation-time Tables; runtime Table join remains pending
    while                      registered receiver, semantics depend on level
    operator/infix tokens       structural operator fields consumed into expression Structures
    (): result                construct one ordinary Lmx Structure under the selected runtime lifetime policy
    []                         ordinary Array constructor: typed service-pool descriptor {len, data}; postfix `target[...]` is index/access (6.5)
    @                          L2 address-of storage location
    \                          L2 prefix raw load, and typed field-follow separator in `a\field`
    table                      registered receiver constructing an ordinary explicit Lmx Table value; hosted storage layout is non-normative
    relation/rule/query        ordinary explicit Structure/Table vocabulary; no current generic receiver heads
    c.union                    registered L2 physical-layout receiver; generic union is not registered
    c.array                    raw C-array storage receiver; ordinary [] instead returns the typed descriptor reference of 6.5
    method/requires.method     legacy bootstrap Tables; not normative implements input
    implements                 implements(varA varB Consumer) consumer-relative structural compatibility receiver; translated or interpreted with the same predicate
    message/operation          ordinary Lmx Structures/explicit description data in L2/L3; no current generic receiver heads


18. Remaining Open Points

This section keeps only the remaining open points that affect common syntax,
L2/L3 execution, expressions, arrays, text, field-follow, closure and ANSI C
projection variants.

18.1. Source-Semantic or Syntax Uncertainties

!!!
Field-follow traversal policy for unknown models, dynamic Structures and
nullable handles is not fixed. Missing exact names and out-of-range occurrence
ordinals already use the checked field-access rule above: they are diagnosed at
translation time when statically known and otherwise by checked runtime access.
!!!

18.2. External or Profile-Level Specification Areas

The exact operator token set and backend lowering rules may be extended by
profiles, but the ANSI C projection deliberately keeps C precedence and
associativity for primitive arithmetic, comparison and boolean expressions.

?
The L2 memory/operator specification must define exact semantics for `@`,
prefix `\`, field-follow `\field`, low-level addressable mutation, raw loads,
raw indexing and address arithmetic.
?

?
The array/text specification must still define slicing, negative indexes if
any, multidimensional arrays, text encoding and the boundary between parser
form and receiver/operator form. Bounds failure itself uses `throw`/`catch`
as specified in section 12.3.
?

?
Initialization and addressable mutation of ordinary source fields remain to be
finished without replacing the structural update semantics in sections 7.3 and
14.1. Nested `fn`/`sub` bodies are real globally reachable C methods and their
callable occurrences are Lmx children of Structures. They do not capture a
runtime Namespace object, environment record or C closure struct. Bare free
names are dynamic inputs copied from the current caller activation, with the
immediate `node\[0]name` fallback when the caller supplies none. A value that
must survive beyond that activation must instead be stored explicitly in a
Structure/Array/Message graph. `c.closure-struct` may remain only as an
explicitly requested raw-C adapter and is never the baseline source-method
representation.
?

Without `throws`, erroneous results may be represented as ordinary Lmx values
allowed by the declared result contract. With `throws`, they are `throw`
delivered to `catch` (sections 19.11 and 19.29.14). Iterator exhaustion,
message validation/execution failure and checked array bounds failure use that
same `throw`/`catch` rule. They are not encoded as `None` and are not
`assert`. The exact Revision-2 result/failure subtree and C return status are
still open and are recorded by the callable `sig`. This open item does not
permit a hidden Namespace/MessageThread/arena argument; it does not prohibit
the normative compiler-generated dynamic value arguments.
18.3. ANSI C Implementation Variants

The input ABI and observable call order are fixed in 21.8:

    typed_entry(callable->node,
                declared_actuals...,
                dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The result/status encoding and physical method-record representation remain
ABI choices. Private helpers may use private signatures. Neither choice adds a
hidden infrastructure/Namespace argument to a source method.

Inlining, cached lookup, static dirty proofs and recursion-to-loop lowering must
preserve identity, current-tree traversal, occurrence order, dirty-only/no-reload
behavior and Message isolation. Explicit L2 machine objects retain their
separately selected C layout; ordinary Structures do not acquire one.

19. L2 & L3 Operators and Receiver Families

19.0. Scope of This Section

This section defines the L2/L3 source-level receiver/operator families for
executable, explicit-description-data, data/query, message and service bodies. The first
entries are control-flow receivers; later subsections retain Registry/Table
facilities only as explicit ordinary values and operations.
`merge` is the intentionally broader structural receiver in this list. L2 and
L3 use the `merge` spelling for ordered Structure construction. Table profiles
use `join` to combine explicitly named Table values and produce an ordinary
result Table. A minimal bootstrap L2 profile may expose only the subset
listed in section 20.5, while richer L2 profiles and ordinary L3 profiles may
admit the full set or profile-specific extensions.

This shared list does not mean that L3 contains all L2 operators. L2 has an
additional low-level machine operator/parser-form surface in section 20. L3 is
the high-level subset and cannot use that machine surface. This is an
abstraction boundary, not a sandbox or authority boundary.

The control operators in this section are not raw low-level jumps. A translator
may lower them to branches, labels, Lmx control/result Structures,
cleanup-stack operations or other implementation artifacts behind the
typed method-record boundary. Structural `merge` always remains an execution-
time generic Lmx tree-copy operation; the compiler emits its recipe/call but
does not execute it, normalize a descriptor or construct its result early.

Unless stated otherwise, these operators have the same source semantics in L2
and L3 when the active profile admits them. Differences belong to profile
constraints, available value types, memory model and lowering policy, not to a
separate parser syntax.

All Revision-2 source implementations in 19.1–19.17 resolve explicit graph
occurrences from the containing Structure, direct links or an explicit root.
Bare free names additionally use the dynamic caller values and immediate-node
fallback of sections 7.3 and 21.3. This semantic dynamic visibility is lowered
to typed C parameters/automatic values, not a reified runtime Namespace. The
implementations use no attached descriptor, closure environment, hidden
MessageThread/arena argument or per-name C aggregate. Used own fields may and in
the baseline do live in dirty-tracked C working locals between checkpoints.
Where an older subsection below still illustrates one of those hosted forms,
that illustration is implementation history only; its surface receiver/control
contract survives only after being lowered through the Revision-2 architecture.

19.0.H. Current Revision-1 Bootstrap Operator Inventory (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.0.H.

19.1. `if` and `else`

`if` is a statement receiver. It consumes a condition expression and a statement
Structure for the true branch. `else` is a separate sibling statement receiver.
It may immediately follow an `if` at the same statement level and consumes the
false-branch statement Structure.

Semantic form:

    if(condition, body)
    else(body)?

Compact sibling form:

if: condition
    trueBody

else:
    falseBody

Explicit named-close form, useful for long blocks or diagnostics:

if: condition
    trueBody
end: if
else:
    falseBody
end: else

Nested form:

    if: condition
        trueBody

    else:
        falseBody


Single-line form:

    if: condition doSomething

The condition is evaluated once before branch selection.

Only the selected body is executed.

If no following sibling `else` receiver is provided and the condition is false, `if` completes normally without executing a body.

`else` is not parser syntax and not a reserved P0 word. It is an ordinary
receiver occurrence whose statement semantics are meaningful when it
appears as the following sibling of an `if` in the selected profile. If another
statement separates `if` and `else`, the pair is not adjacent; the target
projection or profile validator may reject the resulting control flow.

19.2. `match` and default-pattern receivers

`match` selects one body from a set of positional pattern/body pairs or from anonymous branch Structures. `case` is not a structural keyword.

Simple positional example:

match: value
    1
    print: one
    2
    print: two
    default
    print: other
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(value, 1, print(one), 2, print(two), default, print(other))

The `match` receiver may interpret the tail after `value` as repeating `(pattern, branch)` pairs. `default` is an ordinary atom/receiver-defined pattern value, not a parser keyword.

Block branch example:

match: value
    1
    ---
        print: one
        log: matched one
    ---
    2
    ---
        print: two
        log: matched two
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(
      value,
      1,
      (print(one), log(matched one)),
      2,
      (print(two), log(matched two))
    )

Anonymous branch Structure example:

match: value
    ---
        1
        print: one
    ---
        2
        print: two
    ---
        default
        print: other
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(
      value,
      (1, print(one)),
      (2, print(two)),
      (default, print(other))
    )

There is no automatic fallthrough from one branch to the next unless the active `match` receiver explicitly defines such behavior.

For union values, a `match` may destructure the active variant by ordinary pattern Structures.

Example:

match: shape
    ---
        Circle c
        drawCircle: c
    ---
        Square s
        drawSquare: s
    ---

If the matched value is a closed union and no default pattern is present, an implementation may require exhaustive patterns.

19.3. `while`

`while` is a pre-condition loop.

Semantic lowering shape:

    while(condition, statementStructure)

Example:

while: i < n
    print: i
    i: i + 1


The condition is evaluated before each iteration.

If the condition is false before the first iteration, the body is not executed.

A `while` body is a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

19.4. `until`

`until` is a conditional close for the current executable block. It closes the current block, executes that block as a post-condition loop body, then evaluates its condition.

Semantic form:

    until(currentStatementStructure, condition)

Labelled example:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

In this example, `until:` closes the labelled executable block `readLoop`. The
condition is the boolean-producing Structure `endOfInput(record)`. `readLoop`
also identifies the explicit control/callable Lmx occurrence for the block, as
described in section 7.6; it is not a runtime namespace binding.

The body is executed first. The condition is evaluated after the body.

The loop repeats until the condition is true. If the condition is false, control transfers to the beginning of the block closed by `until`.

`until` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

19.5. `for`

`for` is a controlled loop receiver for numeric ranges, counters, index ranges or other bounded iteration policies.

Compact counter/triplet form:

for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++
    print: i


The first three arguments are consumed as loop-control fields:

    init      -> initialization Structure
    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    step      -> iteration-step statement Structure

The vertical child Structure is consumed as the loop body.

Single-line compact body form:

    for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++ print(i)

In this form, `print(i)` is the fourth argument. The `for` receiver signature
consumes that fourth argument as its statement tail, so it lowers as the loop
body. The token form `i++` is valid only in profiles that register it as an
operator-token form.

Range-style example:

for: i 0 n
    print: i


Semantic requirement:

    `for` creates one or more loop-local Lmx iteration nodes.
    `for` advances those nodes according to the selected control policy.
    `for` executes the body once for each produced iteration state.

`for` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

?
The compact counter/triplet form is specified above. Other source forms for numeric ranges, inclusive/exclusive bounds, step values, reverse iteration, index/value pairs and destructuring of iteration bindings are not specified in this section.
?

variant 1:
Use compact positional forms such as `for(i, 0, n)`.

variant 2:
Use explicit short-tail fields such as `for: i 0 n 1`, or use an explicit
profile Structure only when the `for` receiver defines that graph shape.

variant 3:
Treat all range policies as ordinary receiver Structures consumed by `for`.

19.6. `each`

`each` iterates over a container or a Structure admitted by
`implements(source iteratorRequirement eachBody)` for the
requirement exposed by the resolved `each` receiver.

Array-like example:

each: item in items
    draw: item


Receiver-like example:

each: items item
    draw: item


Semantic requirement:

    `each` obtains a sequence of values from its source.
    For each value, `each` assigns the iteration variable in the current C
        activation. If that variable is an own graph-backed field it becomes
        dirty and is published at the next call/exit checkpoint.
    `each` executes the body once for that value.

`each` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

For arrays or array-like containers, `each` may lower to an index loop.

After that used-path check, the `each` receiver separately consumes the
iterator's callable `next` occurrence. It reads the selected
`all_methods_array.sig`, supplies `next_callable->node` plus its declared and
dynamic values, and invokes `addr` through that exact typed signature. Iterator
suspension state is the retained C activation or a private equivalent
continuation as specified in section 19.15, not an LMX runtime Namespace.
`next` produces a value by `yield` or ends
the sequence by `throw: Stop`. `each` catches `Stop` and finishes the loop.
That is the same `catch` rule as section 19.11. Exhaustion is not a `None`
element: `None` may be a valid yielded value. Any other `throw` from `next`
propagates unless the `each` body or caller has a matching `catch`. Failure
to consume `next` as a call is an ordinary receiver error, not another
compatibility relation.

In the source form:

    each: item in source

the `source` field is consumed by the `each` receiver as an iteration source. If
`source` resolves to a container, `each` iterates over that container. If its
`implements(source iteratorRequirement eachBody)` holds, `each`
consumes the Structure as iterator data. If `source` resolves to a visible zero-argument iterator
function, `each` may consume the callable Lmx occurrence by creating its iterator
state, equivalent to the explicit compact form `source()`.

Therefore both forms are valid for a zero-argument iterator source:

    each: word in words
    each: word in words()

The first form lets `each` consume the callable occurrence as an iterator
source. The second form explicitly creates the iterator state Structure before
`each` consumes it. This is receiver consumption of an already parsed field;
it is not hidden receiver-signature grouping or a hidden environment argument.

Iterator consumption by `each` is specified in section 19.15.5.

19.7. `return`

`return` exits the current `fn`, `sub` or executable-like body.

Runtime forms inside an executable body:

    return
    return: value

For an `fn`, `return: value` consumes an Lmx value admitted by the explicit
return consumer and exits. The exact C return/status/failure channel and result
subtree remain open Revision-2 ABI work (sections 8 and 19.29.5) and are recorded
by the callable `sig`. Every generated entry begins with `callable->node` and
also receives its declared and dynamically inherited values. It may not add a
descriptor, arena, MessageThread, closure environment or reified runtime
Namespace. It never copies dynamic inputs back. A bare `return` is not a valid
value-producing `fn` return in the core profile.

For a `sub`, `return` exits the `sub` body. `return: value` is invalid for `sub` unless a later profile defines status/result subroutines.

At a valid terminal trailer position for the open executable definition, an admitted return form is terminal return. It appends the corresponding runtime return as the final body item and closes the executable definition. For an `fn`, this terminal form is `return: value`; for a `sub` or no-value label/procedure executable, this terminal form may be bare `return`. This is an executable-specific close, not a general replacement for `end: name`.

For a definition opened by `fn: name` or `sub: name` at level N, the source level of the definition is N. An admitted return form at level N is the common terminal structural trailer. The same return form at level N + 1 or deeper is an ordinary runtime return inside the body. A return form at a smaller source level may still be terminal return when the normal source-level cut reaches the visible executable tail and the intervening frames admit that closure. See section 8.3.

Examples:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
return: x * x

fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)
    if: n <= 1
        return: 1
return: n * factorial(n - 1)

The exact return sequence is:

    evaluate the return expression/status once and retain/root the result;
    publish and clear only dirty own working locals;
    execute all `finally` cleanups registered for the exited scopes in reverse
        registration order;
    publish and clear own working locals dirtied by those cleanups;
    transfer the retained result/status and destroy the C activation.

Both publications are the dirty-only checkpoint of sections 7.4 and 21.6. The
first makes pre-return state visible to cleanup code. The final checkpoint is
mandatory even when cleanup performed no outbound call: assignment in the last
cleanup statement cannot disappear with the C frame. A clean local is never
republished, dynamic inputs are never copied back, and no caller reload occurs.

The current hosted bootstrap has one process-entry compatibility rule. A
`return` in the generated external `main` evaluates its status exactly once,
runs the generated cleanups for the active scopes, requests that the root
Message Thread stop with that status, and transfers to the common end-turn
boundary. It therefore ends the root loop without bypassing its final local
collection pass. This special lowering applies only to the external process
entry. A `return` in every nested or ordinary fn/sub retains the semantics
above and does not stop a Message Thread. See section 19.28.2.2.

19.8. `break`

`break` exits the nearest active loop frame.

Example:

while: running
    if: stopRequested
        break
    ---
    step


`break` is valid only inside a loop frame unless a labelled form is used as described in section 19.16.

Before control leaves the exited loop body scopes, all affected `finally` cleanups are executed in reverse registration order.

`break` is not a source-level raw jump. It is a structured loop-frame exit.

19.9. `continue`

`continue` starts the next iteration of the nearest active loop frame.

Example:

each: item in items
    if: skip item
        continue
    ---
    process: item


For `while`, `continue` transfers control to the next condition check.

For `until`, `continue` transfers control to the post-body condition check for the current iteration.

For `for`, `continue` transfers control to the next iteration-state update.

For `each`, `continue` transfers control to the next container or iterator value.

Before control leaves the current loop body scopes, all affected `finally` cleanups are executed in reverse registration order.

19.10. `finally`

`finally` registers cleanup code in the current execution context.

Block form:

finally:
    cleanupBody


Single-line form:

    finally: cleanupCall

`finally` is not part of a `try` block in this specification. It is a standalone cleanup registration operator.

A cleanup registered by `finally` is associated with the current executable/scope frame.

Cleanups are executed when their associated frame or scope is exited by:

    normal completion;
    return;
    break;
    continue;
    labelled break;
    labelled continue;
    throw;
    retry;
    redo;
    assert diagnostic exit;
    thread/task termination in the default runtime profile.

Cleanups are executed in reverse registration order.

A cleanup body is executed outside the registration operation. It is not executed at the point where `finally` is encountered, except when the frame exits immediately after registration.

`finally` cleanups are part of the Message Thread execution-context model in
section 19.28.

A control exit from inside a cleanup body (`throw`, `assert`, `return`,
`break`, `continue`, `retry`, `redo`) does not re-enter the `finally`
registration that is currently running. A nested `finally` written in that
cleanup body is a new registration for the cleanup frame, not a second run of
the outer one. After the current cleanup is left, remaining not-yet-run
cleanups of the abandoned outer frame still run in reverse registration
order. Then the exit is delivered as usual: `throw` to `catch`/`throws`,
`assert` to the diagnostic root, `return`/`break`/`continue`/`retry`/`redo`
to their ordinary targets.

19.11. `catch`, `throw`, `throws`

`catch`, `throw` and `throws` define declared recoverable failure flow.

They are distinct from `assert`. They are not the module/prelude `guard:`
receiver.

    catch / throw / throws:
        declared, expected, recoverable failure path

    assert:
        diagnostic invariant violation

The detection condition is an ordinary `if`. There is no failure-flow `guard:`.

19.11.1. `throws`

An executable definition may declare the names that may leave its body
with `throws`.

A caller of that executable must provide a `catch` for each listed name or
itself declare that name in `throws`. A call that does neither is rejected.

`throws` lists names, not types. Those names are the `catch` landing pads the
caller must supply.

19.11.2. `throw`

`throw: Name(arguments)` exits the current executable body. It does not
continue the remainder of that activation after the `throw` site and does not
produce the declared return value. A `catch` that calls the same executable
again starts a new activation from the beginning of that executable. That
re-call is not resumption of the abandoned frame.

The arguments are the throw payload. They bind to the parameters of the
selected `catch: Name (...)`.

Semantic effect:

    select throw name `Name`;
    evaluate and retain the explicit payload from `arguments`;
    publish every dirty own working local of the throwing method to its
        corresponding field in that method's Structure;
    execute affected `finally` cleanups for the current frame exit;
    publish any own working locals dirtied by those cleanups;
    leave the current executable;
    deliver the payload to the matching `catch` in the caller block, or
        propagate through that caller's `throws`.

Each publication step is the ordinary dirty-only checkpoint from section 7.4.
The first makes the pre-throw working state visible to cleanup code. The final
one occurs immediately before the C activation disappears and includes writes
performed by `finally` after the first checkpoint.
It writes only own fields actually assigned or rebound since their most recent
successful checkpoint. Dynamically inherited hidden values are activation-local
copies and are never written back automatically. Every method activation crossed
by propagation performs its own dirty-only exit checkpoint before that C
activation disappears. A clean cached value must not be published merely because
the method throws: a nested call may already have changed the corresponding graph
field explicitly.

`catch` receives the throw payload through its explicitly declared parameters.
The transfer does not install a captured namespace and does not reload the
caller's C working locals. Consequently an unqualified caller local continues to
denote that activation's current C value, while an explicit `node\field`,
`reference\field` or `array[index]` access observes the current graph/referent.
This distinction is intentional and is the same no-reload rule as after an
ordinary method call.

`throw` is part of the declared `throws` set. It is not a diagnostic abort.

`throw` does not transfer to the thread diagnostic root.

`throw` does not use the `assert` channel.

19.11.3. `catch`

`catch: Name (parameters)` is a landing pad in a caller block. It is not an
ordinary nested `sub` and is not entered on first pass through the block.

Its body runs only when `throw: Name(...)` is delivered. After the `catch` body
completes, execution continues in the same block at the statements following
that `catch`. Those following statements, up to the next `catch` in the same
block or the end of the block, are the region re-entered or skipped according
to where the `catch` was written: a `catch` before a call re-enters the
following region; a `catch` after a call continues past the `catch`.

Two `catch` receivers with the same name in one block are an error. A series
of same-named `catch` receivers is written as separate nested blocks, each
with its own `catch`. A `throw` is delivered to the `catch` of that name in the
block from which the throwing executable was called, not to a same-named
`catch` in a sibling block.

A `throw` of the same name from inside a `catch` body is not a re-entry of that
same landing pad. It is a new failure in the enclosing caller.

Example from `tests/t2.lmx`:

fn: checkedGreeting (HelloConfig(cfg)) (Text)
    throws:
        GreetingRetry
        GreetingSkip

    if: cfg\greeting\len = 0
        throw: GreetingRetry(cfg)

    if: cfg\greeting\len < 0
        throw: GreetingSkip(cfg\greeting)

    return: cfg\greeting


sub: helloMain
    HelloConfig: cfg
        greeting: "Hello World"
        maxI: 3
        maxJ: 3
    ---

    HelloConfig: emptyCfg
        greeting: ""
        maxI: 1
        maxJ: 1
    ---

    ---
        catch: GreetingRetry ()
            cfg\greeting\len++
            System\out\println: checkedGreeting(cfg)
    ---
        catch: GreetingRetry ()
            emptyCfg\greeting\len++
            System\out\println: checkedGreeting(emptyCfg)

    catch: GreetingSkip (Text: badText)
        System\out\println: ("handled EmptyGreeting: " + badText)


`checkedGreeting` may leave by `return: cfg\greeting` or by `throw`. The empty
`GreetingRetry ()` catch parameters mean this landing pad takes no payload
fields; it mutates the outer `cfg` or `emptyCfg`. `GreetingSkip` binds
`cfg\greeting` from `throw: GreetingSkip(cfg\greeting)` to `badText`.

Each anonymous `---` block owns one `GreetingRetry` catch and one call. The
two `GreetingRetry` names are therefore not duplicates. `GreetingSkip` sits
in `helloMain` after those blocks, so a skip continues at the statements
after that `catch`.

19.11.4. Throw propagation

If a throw is not caught in the current block, it must be propagated by an
enclosing or calling executable whose `throws` declaration includes that
name.

Throw propagation is structured. It is not a raw jump through arbitrary
stack frames in the source semantics. It is not a C `goto` from the callee
into the caller.

---ANSI C---
A translator may lower declared failures as a structured status return from
the callee plus a same-function branch or `goto` in the caller to the point
after the selected `catch`. Every generated exit edge must first pass through
the dirty-only checkpoint epilogue. A backend must not use a C non-local transfer
that skips these epilogues.

Root-level `longjmp` is not the default. A selected runtime profile may use a
non-local transfer only if Lingvamyxa `finally` semantics are preserved.
---ANSI C---

19.12. `retry` and `retryable`

`retryable` defines a restartable frame.

`retry` restarts the nearest or named retryable frame.

Example:

loadAttempt:
    retryable:
        loadNetworkData
    ---

Possible `catch` use:

catch: NetworkError ()
    reconnect
    retry: loadAttempt


A `retry` transfer executes the affected `finally` cleanups for the abandoned
attempt before re-entering the retryable frame.

`retry` does not roll back external side effects. It only repeats the source frame according to the retryable-frame semantics.

`retry` is a local control-flow transfer in the current C method activation; it
is not, by itself, a call, return, throw or new method activation. Therefore it
does not create an additional checkpoint merely because the `retry` edge is
taken, does not reload the method Structure and does not recreate an implicit
Lmx activation tree. The current C working locals continue into the repeated
region. If repeated execution reaches a method call, return or throw, the normal
checkpoint for that later boundary applies.

Graph mutations, already-published dirty fields, explicit `node\field` writes,
Structure/Array mutations and Message effects keep their current values. There
is no implicit transaction snapshot and no retry rollback. A stronger rollback
policy must be an explicit receiver/protocol whose state is represented by
ordinary Structure, Array or Message data.

`retry` without a label targets the nearest active retryable frame.

`retry: labelName` targets the named retryable frame.

When `retry` is reached from a `catch`, the caught payload remains governed by
the catch's explicit argument declarations. No hidden environment of the
throwing method is restored. Partially executed `finally` bodies follow section
19.10's one-cleanup execution rule; any calls or exits inside them establish
their ordinary checkpoints.

19.13. `assert`

`assert` checks a diagnostic invariant.

Example:

    assert: index >= 0
    assert: index < values\len

If the condition is true, execution continues normally.

If the condition is false, the current execution/checking phase fails with an `AssertionViolation` diagnostic.

`assert` is not recoverable `throw` control flow.

`assert` is not part of the declared `throws` set.

`assert` is not caught by `catch`.

Default L3 runtime semantics in the Message Thread profile defined in section
19.28:

    evaluate and retain/root the AssertionViolation diagnostic;
    publish every dirty own working local of the failing activation;
    execute all active `finally` cleanups in the executing Message Thread's
        ExecutionContext;
    publish every own working local dirtied by those cleanups;
    transfer control to the thread diagnostic root;
    terminate the executing Message Thread/task by default after reporting the
        diagnostic.

Both publications are ordinary dirty-only checkpoints. The first exposes the
pre-assert state to cleanup; the second occurs immediately before transfer and
cannot be omitted when cleanup's last statement dirties an own local. Thus an
assertion path which destroys or unwinds a generated method activation follows
the same pre-cleanup/check/final-checkpoint ordering as `return`/`throw`. The
same rule applies to `stop`, cancellation or another profile exit whenever that
edge removes the current C activation. It never copies a hidden dynamic input
back to its caller and never republishes a clean cached field.

Default L2 descriptor/configuration-loading use, if exposed by a receiver profile:

    an assertion violation terminates the current model-loading or translation pass with a diagnostic error.

`assert` is intended for impossible states, broken invariants, corrupted internal assumptions or translator/model errors. Expected input failure must use `if`, `throw` and `catch`.

19.13.1. `log` and `error`

`log` and `error` are observation receivers.

Canonical forms:

    log: value
    error: value

The tail may be a constant/literal value or a child/path resolved from the
containing Lmx Structure or an explicit root. The receiver consumes that value
and records it through the active observation profile.

Examples:

    log: "matched one"
    log: matchedValue
    error: "parse failed"
    error: diagnostic

`log` records an ordinary observation, trace, note, audit event, metric payload
or profile-defined informational record.

`error` records an error/diagnostic observation. It is not reserved parser
syntax and it is not automatically equivalent to `throw`, `assert`, `return` or
thread termination. If a profile wants an error observation to also fail,
abort, throw, send a failure message or terminate a task, that behavior must
be defined by that profile or by an explicit surrounding receiver.

Both receivers use ordinary structural resolution for non-literal tails. A bare
unknown name is not magically logged as text unless the active profile admits
such symbol logging explicitly. Write a string/exact identifier when the source
wants a literal textual record.

19.14. `redo`

`redo` repeats the current loop iteration without advancing the loop source.

For `each`, `redo` repeats the body with the same current element.

For iterator-based `each`, `redo` does not invoke the iterator's selected
`next` callable before repeating the body.

For `for`, `redo` repeats the body with the same loop-local Lmx nodes.

For `while`, `redo` repeats the body without re-evaluating the condition first.

For `until`, `redo` repeats the body without evaluating the post-condition first.

Before the current iteration is restarted, all `finally` cleanups registered inside the abandoned iteration attempt are executed.

`redo` is dangerous because it can create non-progressing loops. It is valid only inside a loop frame.

19.15. `yield`

Revision 2 retains `yield` and iterators as a source design family but has not
selected their result and iterator-container ABI. Suspension follows the same
activation rule as an ordinary call stack: the current method's C working locals,
declared arguments and hidden dynamic values remain part of that suspended
activation. `yield` does not, merely because it suspends, copy those values into
the method Structure, invent a call/body Structure or create a runtime Namespace
object. The method Structure remains only the published working checkpoint state.

A stackful implementation may retain the native frame. A stackless or movable
implementation may lower that frame to a private continuation record, but such a
record is a service representation of the same C activation, not an LMX
Structure, descriptor, closure environment or source-visible history. Resumption
must restore the same C working values and dirty state and must not reload them
from the method Structure.

Because `yield` hands control and a produced value outside the current
activation, it is a semantic dirty-only checkpoint boundary independent of
physical lowering. The backend publishes dirty own fields and clears their dirty
state before suspension whether it uses a C call, stack switch or generated
state-machine return. Hidden dynamic values remain private members of the
suspended C activation/continuation and are not published to the method node.

`next` is still represented by a callable Lmx occurrence. Its method record in
`all_methods_array` supplies the typed signature, including the structural
`next_callable->node` argument, its declared arguments and result/throw
convention. The producer activation's declared/dynamic working values were fixed
when that producer activation was first entered and remain in the suspended
frame; a later `next` caller does not recompute or override them. If `next` is a
separate generated wrapper with its own dynamic inputs, those values belong only
to the wrapper activation unless an explicit operation writes the continuation.
`throw: Stop` follows section 19.11. No hidden MessageThread source argument or
reified runtime Namespace is introduced.

19.15.H. Revision-1 Iterator/Closure Projection (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.15.H.

19.16. Labelled `break` and labelled `continue`

A control label may be attached to a loop frame.

Example:

outerLoop:
    for: row in rows
        for: cell in row
            if: found cell
                break: outerLoop
            ---
        ---
    ---

`labelName:` is an ordinary colon frame that the active profile materializes as a visible control target for the following block.

The label name identifies an explicit control-target Lmx occurrence/link, but it
is not callable merely by being mentioned. A standalone `labelName` item is a
compile-time error in the default profile: Lingvamyxa has no implicit `goto`.
Only control forms that define label consumption, such as `break: labelName`,
`continue: labelName`, `catch` or other profile-defined control
operators, may consume a label as a target.

`break: labelName` exits the labelled loop frame.

`continue: labelName` continues the labelled loop frame if the target label names a loop frame.

Before the transfer, all `finally` cleanups registered in scopes exited by the transfer are executed in reverse registration order.

It is a compile-time error for `break: labelName` or `continue: labelName` to target a label that is not visible in the control-label scope.

It is a compile-time error for `continue: labelName` to target a non-loop frame.

?
The exact grammar rule for attaching labels to frames is not specified beyond the form `labelName:` before a frame. A final grammar must define whether labels can attach to retryable frames, blocks, handlers, iterator frames and arbitrary Structures.
?

19.17. `merge` / Table `join`

`merge` is an L2/L3 structural receiver/operator. It is not parser syntax, not a
preprocessor include, descriptor composition or mutation of its source
operands. The parser builds an ordinary `merge:` Frame. The translator leaves a
generic merge operation at that execution site; when and only when runtime
control reaches it, the operation consumes live Lmx operands and creates a new
Lmx result tree.

The old hosted handler that flattened layout descriptions, emitted a per-name
C type, required a later constructor or materialized module values eagerly is
non-normative Revision-1 machinery. The new core must not invoke it as the
implementation of Structure merge.

For example:

    merge: (BaseFields AuditFields) CombinedFields
        u32: flags
    end: CombinedFields

generates runtime construction/merge code. It does not generate a C
`struct CombinedFields`, and no pre-execution descriptor is the result.

Semantic form:

    merge(part0, part1, ..., partN)

Each part is consumed in a Structure-producing context. It may be an already
resolved Lmx reference, a bounded or vertical Structure expression, an
explicit root-relative path result, or a receiver/call result admitted by the
consumer. Dynamic merge is therefore equally able to consume a result that was
itself produced by an earlier merge.

The default merge is ordered, copying and append-only:

    1. Evaluate the operand expressions once, from left to right.
    2. Allocate one new result root.
    3. For each operand in order, copy that operand root's direct children and
       their complete containment subtrees directly under the result root.
       Do not insert the operand root itself as an extra child.
    4. Copy the vertical body children after all operand children.
    5. Allocate every copied containment occurrence anew, preserving order and
       repeated short names.
    6. Rewrite each copied child's `node` to its corresponding parent in the
       new tree.
    7. Register every named copied occurrence in the sole
       `Lmx * -> ShortNameId` table; apply the selected ABI convention to
       positional/anonymous occurrences.
    8. Copy immutable primitive and callable payload references according to
       their address-domain policies.
    9. Do not delete, rename or in-place replace any source occurrence.

For repeated short names, occurrence numbers follow the result order from the
beginning. The unqualified path `name` is exactly `[0]name`; `[1]name` is the
next matching child, and so on. A later operand does not become the default
merely because it was appended later.

The exact policy for non-containment graph references whose target lies inside
the copied subtree remains unresolved: a conforming runtime must explicitly
choose whether to remap such internal targets to their copies or preserve the
original reference. That open question never permits sharing containment nodes
or failing to rewrite `node`.

Example:

BaseFields:
    int: id
    @: char name
end: BaseFields

AuditFields:
    u64: createdAt
    u64: updatedAt
end: AuditFields

merge: BaseFields AuditFields
    u32: flags
end: merge

Conceptual result:

    (
        int: id
        @: char name
        u64: createdAt
        u64: updatedAt
        u32: flags
    )

The vertical body of a `merge:` Frame is part of the same parsed argument
Structure as its short tail. Its children therefore become the trailing copied
children of the runtime result. If a profile uses merge to build explicit
description or import data, that data follows exactly the same Lmx tree-copy
semantics; it does not select a second descriptor-only merge algorithm.

Table profile:

In an L2 or L3 table profile, `table:` and `join:` are ordinary receiver Frames
whose bodies are valid LMX Structures. `table:` explicitly materializes one
table representation as an Lmx graph with Arrays under 6.5 where required. A named result is attached as an
ordinary child occurrence to the Structure selected by the source operation;
it is not inserted into a runtime namespace. `Table` is a profile role, not a
second value category or an attached descriptor. `join:` explicitly consumes
Lmx references and materializes one new table graph; it does not mutate its
operands.

The optional first body atom `source` is a bootstrap projection request for a
table used by the translator. It never declares the names contained in cells,
creates runtime bindings, or gives a column named `class` special language
meaning. A future replacement may compile such data into an immutable Lmx
provider, but that provider remains explicit data and is not runtime name state.

All ordinary table values are composed from Lmx Structures and the selected
Array representation. An ANSI C backend may emit constant/generated data only
when that representation preserves Lmx identity, address-domain classification,
tree links and observable lifetime. C `static` storage is not Table source
semantics.

19.17.H. Historical Hosted Table Projection

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.17.H.

19.18. Explicit Registry Data and Query Operators (Revision 2)

19.18.R2. Normative Registry Model

A Registry, Table or RegistryView is ordinary explicit program data. Its
ordinary Structure parts are Lmx nodes; any array-backed part follows the
typed-descriptor and rectangular-backing contract of 6.5.
The role words `Registry`, `Table` and `RegistryView` do not create another
Value category, attach type metadata to a node or install names into a runtime
environment.

Every registry operation receives the Registry/Table root or an explicit link
to it. Depending on its source role, a translator-resolved name becomes an own
C working slot, a hidden dynamic C argument, a direct graph link, a
root-relative structural traversal or a call-capable value/record. The first two
implement active bare-name semantics; explicit Structure paths use graph
traversal, and a callable value may be selected from either channel before exact
`sig` validation. Dynamic textual lookup receives an explicit root, enumerates that root's
ordered children and compares their ShortNameIds through the mandatory
`Lmx * -> ShortNameId` reverse index. A Registry is never searched merely
because it exists.

`table:` and `join:` construct ordinary data. Table import, overlay, priority,
row conflict and missing-cell rules are policies of the explicitly invoked
table consumer. They do not change structural lookup, method visibility or
the occurrence rule. In the resulting Structure, `name` means `[0]name`: the
first matching child in result/source order; `[1]name` is the next matching
child in the forward direction. A later table or merge input does not become
the default occurrence.

A registry cell payload is an Lmx value or an explicitly represented external
payload. It may link to ordinary data, a policy Structure, another table key,
evidence, a diagnostic, a provider or a callable occurrence. A payload called
`descriptor`, `class`, `schema` or `type` is still ordinary user data. It is
never an attached descriptor and cannot alter language meaning by insertion
into a Table. A callable payload identifies a callable Lmx node through its
registered `all_methods_array {addr,sig}` record; source execution invokes:

    typed_entry(callable->node,
                declared_actuals...,
                dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The signature fixes every declared and hidden dynamic value and its
primitive-by-value or Structure/Array-reference pass mode. It adds no Registry
lookup, descriptor or reified Namespace.

Contextual-literal maps, provider maps, conversion matrices and operation
matrices may be stored in Tables, but a consumer must reach them through an
explicit graph link or explicit root. The translator may use a compile-time
symbol table to resolve a known operation head; that compiler work table is not
materialized as language state. An arbitrary runtime query likewise cannot
publish a binding or change the meaning of another expression.

An explicit module import may compile and link the selected provider methods
and construction recipes. It neither scans a directory nor eagerly constructs
all declarations, and it creates no runtime namespace. Registry persistence,
indexing and caching are library/profile choices over explicit data.

19.18.H. Revision-1 Registry/Namespace Profile (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.18.H.


19.19. Message Pipeline Operators (Revision 2)

19.19.R2. Normative Message Pipeline

A Message is an ordinary Lmx graph used in a message role. Its envelope,
address, body, claims, evidence, policy links, callable links and Mix regions
are Structures in the same Message ontology; none supplies an ambient
namespace or an attached descriptor. External resources are represented by
explicit handles/links whose lifetime policy is ordinary explicit data.

The validation-stage operation in this section is called **policy
combination**. It must not be called `merge` in normative APIs or prose.
Policy combination selects and checks already represented data; core `merge`
is exclusively the lazy runtime containment-tree copy defined by section 19.17;
policy combination neither copies Structure trees nor manufactures types,
methods or bindings.

The normative inputs to policy combination are:

    an IncomingMessage Lmx root;
    an explicit receiving Message/endpoint/root;
    explicit route, policy, evidence and provider Lmx values;
    authenticated ingress facts supplied by the transport boundary.

Its semantic stages are:

    intake
        Decode or adopt the incoming value under an explicit codec/lifetime
        policy and produce an IncomingMessage Structure.

    route and target selection
        Follow a direct route/target link or perform explicit-root structural
        lookup. No runtime Namespace snapshot is selected.

    structural and operation resolution
        Follow already resolved Lmx links, relative `node`/child traversal or
        explicit-root textual paths. When compatibility is requested,
        `implements(varA varB Consumer)` compares the actual Structure paths
        used by `Consumer`; it does not compare attached descriptors.

    validation and evidence
        Invoke explicitly linked validation/policy callables and construct
        ordinary evidence Lmx values.

    authority and effects
        Check explicit identity, capability, effect, lifetime and delivery
        policy values.

    implementation selection
        Select an explicit callable Lmx occurrence/provider link admitted by
        the preceding checks.

    plan construction
        Construct an ExecutionPlan Lmx value holding the selected callable,
        argument/result Structures, evidence and explicit effect/delivery
        policy.

Policy combination does not execute the selected expression. Domain-specific
