dispatch, ranking, conversion, validation and provider choice are callable or
data nodes reached from these explicit operands. A Registry/Table participates
only when one of those operands links to it.

The executor consumes an ExecutionPlan and follows its selected callable link.
For a source method, it obtains the callable's `{addr,sig}` method-pool record
and invokes

    typed_entry(callable->node,
                declared_actuals...,
                dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The containing node supplies explicit structural fallback state. Declared and
dynamic primitive values are copied by value; Structure/Array reference values
are copied as references. Used own node fields may reside in dirty-tracked C
working locals between checkpoints. There is no hidden MessageThread,
ExecutionContext, reified Namespace, descriptor, closure environment or arena
argument in the generated source-method ABI. Runtime/actor/transport wrappers
may receive infrastructure arguments, but their boundary action is to locate
the callable occurrence and form this typed source call.

A logical generator is likewise an explicitly linked callable Lmx node. It may
read an explicitly linked Table of generation rules and construct zero or more
ProposedMessage graphs. Each proposed Message re-enters the same intake and
policy-combination pipeline; generation does not grant trust or authority.

The standard pipeline is therefore:

    IncomingMessage
        -> intake -> IncomingMessageStructure
        -> explicit route/target selection
        -> structural/direct-link resolution
        -> actual-Structure compatibility when requested
        -> validation/evidence
        -> authority/effect/lifetime checks
        -> explicit callable/provider selection
        -> ExecutionPlan
        -> Executor -> Result or Failure
        -> boundary result consumer / logical generator
        -> ProposedMessage(s)

19.19.H. Revision-1 Namespace/Descriptor Message Pipeline (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.19.H.


19.20. Revision-2 Runtime Structure Construction

Construction materializes Lmx nodes and containment links at runtime. A source
name does not imply a distinct C type, a shared type descriptor, a constructor
function in an object model, or static instance storage. The only universal
node representation is:

    struct Lmx {
        Lmx *node;
        size_t len;
        void *data;
    };

For each source Structure expression the translator emits a runtime
builder/evaluator using generic Lmx operations. When execution reaches that
expression, the builder:

    1. obtains the containing Lmx parent selected by the execution rule;
    2. allocates the required root/child Lmx occurrences with stable live
       addresses;
    3. sets each child's `node` to its actual containment parent;
    4. constructs `len` and `data` according to the registered payload domain;
    5. records each named occurrence in `Lmx * -> ShortNameId`;
    6. evaluates child initializers in source order;
    7. creates callable occurrences whose payloads resolve to already linked
       program-wide method entries;
    8. publishes the result only after the operation's required initialization
       succeeds.

Names never enter the Lmx header. A builder does not create any other per-node
metadata. Positional/anonymous occurrences follow the selected Revision-2
ShortNameId convention once that open ABI item is fixed.

Every executable child is discovered recursively during translation even
though its callable Lmx occurrence is materialized at runtime. The C entry is
emitted once with a deterministic program-wide unique identity, preferably a
long source-path-derived name. Its immutable `all_methods_array {addr,sig}`
record fixes the typed input list: containing `Lmx *node`, declared arguments
and ordered dynamic values. The runtime callable occurrence identifies that
record; the Structure does not contain a C method member or vtable.

Persistent body/node fields are Lmx occurrences. Declared arguments and hidden
dynamic inputs are typed C activation values, while used own fields are loaded
into dirty-tracked C working locals. Before every semantic call/exposure
boundary and exit, only own fields actually written since the last checkpoint
are published; after a nested call the caller is not reloaded. C working values
do not replace persistent node identity or explicit `node` traversal.

Named and anonymous Structure construction use the same generic operation.
Named construction additionally records the short name of the occurrence in
the reverse side table or attaches/publishes the resulting reference through an
explicit structural operation. There is no implicit runtime namespace binding.

Descriptor-like Structures remain legal user/profile data. A schema, protocol
description or backend table may be built and passed explicitly, but ordinary
Structure construction does not look for a descriptor prefix and does not need
one to determine a C layout. Explicit raw L2 `c.struct`, `c.union` and foreign
ABI operations remain separate substrate facilities and must not become the
representation of ordinary Structures.

Construction and `merge` are distinct generic runtime operations. Construction
builds the source expression being executed. `merge` additionally creates a
fresh root and copies operand children/subtrees as specified in section 19.17.
Neither operation scans the source directory, invokes the translator at runtime
or eagerly materializes every top-level declaration before execution. The
exact evaluation/reuse policy of a top-level named Structure remains an open
Revision-2 item; eager translation-time materialization is prohibited.

Direct C field access may later be emitted only as a proven optimization whose
behaviour is identical under every observable mutation and runtime merge.
Generic child traversal and root-relative textual lookup remain the baseline.

Anonymous construction examples

    (): result
        int: count 3
        @: char text "hello"
        int: count 4
    end: result

This constructs one ordinary Structure with ordered child occurrences:
`result\count` and `result\[0]count` select 3; `result\[1]count` selects 4.
There is no attached descriptor and no newest-occurrence lookup.

An existing child can be retained by reference:

    (): parent
        nested: child
    end: parent

The field retains the same `child` reference; this form does not clone its
referent or turn `child` into a constructor/type.

    (): empty

The empty Structure is present and distinct from null or a missing value.
It satisfies a Consumer with no required paths.


19.20.H1. Revision-1 Descriptor/Constructor Model (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.20.H1.


19.21. Operations, Operators and Receivers

Revision-2 operations, operators and receivers are callable occurrences in the
Lmx graph or compiler-known core forms lowered to the same runtime operations.
Explicit structural paths traverse the graph. Bare free names use caller-dynamic
values with immediate-node fallback. This dynamic visibility is carried by C
activations and does not consult a reified runtime Namespace object.

19.21.1. Direct Structural Operation Dispatch

Callable selection begins from one of three explicit values:

    an already resolved callable Lmx occurrence;
    a relative Structure path beginning at the current containing Structure;
    an arbitrary textual absolute path together with an explicit root.

The first is used directly. The second enumerates current children and resolves
ShortNameId occurrences from its supplied start node. The third walks from the
explicit root. This selects the callable occurrence; it is distinct from
selecting the dynamic values required by that callable's `sig`.

After selecting a callable occurrence, runtime classifies its `data` in the
common callable physical class, obtains `{addr,sig}` from
`all_methods_array`, validates the concrete Consumer call site, converts `addr`
to the type selected by `sig` and invokes:

    typed_entry(callable->node,
                declared_actuals...,
                dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The first argument is the Structure containing this callable occurrence. Each
bare dynamic input is copied from the caller's current activation; if unavailable
there, it is copied from the direct `callable->node\[0]name` field. Primitive
values are copied by value and Structure/Array references by reference value.
The exact result/error channel remains recorded but open. Infrastructure
MessageThread/arena/Namespace/descriptor/closure parameters are not allowed;
typed declared/dynamic parameters and dirty-tracked C working locals are
required parts of the model rather than exceptions to it.

A callable copied by `merge` retains its method payload but receives a new
containment parent. Consequently the same compiled body operates on the copied
Structure when invoked. There is no object-method binding or vtable involved.

19.21.2. Receiver and Operator Structure

A parser/compiler profile may recognize core receiver/operator spellings and
lower them to known callable references or runtime helpers. User-defined
receivers/operators are reached through ordinary Lmx paths. Multiple dispatch,
fallback, conversion or policy data may be expressed as explicit Structures,
Arrays or Tables consumed by that receiver. Such data is never an ambient
registry or runtime namespace.

19.21.3. `implements`

`implements(varA varB Consumer)` is a structural used-path predicate. It takes
the paths by which `Consumer` uses `varB`, traverses the corresponding current
Lmx structures and asks whether `varA` provides the required paths/leaf
contracts. Constant paths may be collected from P0; dynamic paths are checked
by the explicitly selected runtime operation.

If a path used by `Consumer` is invoked as a callable, its leaf contract includes
the immutable `all_methods_array.sig` record. The candidate callable must admit
the exact concrete call shape used by that Consumer: structural node, declared
arguments, ordered dynamic names/pass modes and result/throw convention.
Callable fields not invoked by Consumer are irrelevant, just as unused ordinary
fields are irrelevant.

`implements` does not require a descriptor prefix, generated C type or
Name-to-Binding map. Optional schema/description Structures participate only
when Consumer explicitly follows or receives them. The predicate neither calls
a converter nor changes the graph. Any replacement or argument-consumption
operation performs its own explicit update only after admission succeeds.

For repeated names, ordinary traversal means `[0]`; an occurrence-qualified
path checks that exact occurrence. Compatibility never changes occurrence order.

Current hosted implementation status: `lm_trans_implements` /
`lm_trans_descriptor_implements` still perform only a shallow legacy
presence-style check, and the existing callable signature checker is separate
from that walk. They do not yet implement the recursive Consumer-used tree,
forward `[0]` occurrence rule and exact used-callable `sig` comparison above.
This is replacement-core implementation debt, not a relaxation of
`implements(varA, varB, Consumer)`.

19.21.H. Revision-1 Namespace/Descriptor Dispatch (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.21.H.


19.22. Explicit Evidence and Validation Values (Revision 2)

19.22.R2. Normative Evidence Model

Description records, dispatch policies, validation rules, authority facts and
evidence are ordinary explicit Lmx data. They become relevant only when a
consumer receives their nodes through a direct link, a structural path or an
explicit-root lookup. No such record is attached to every value, visible in a
runtime namespace or consulted merely because its spelling resembles a source
name.

Evidence is an Lmx node or subtree recording why a concrete Structure path,
operation, route or authority decision was admitted. Its dependencies are
explicit graph links to the exact values, policies and authenticated ingress
facts on which it depends. Mutation, replacement, loss of authority or expiry
of a dependency invalidates evidence according to that evidence node's
explicit policy; validity is not inferred from an arena owner or descriptor
identity.

A validator is a callable Lmx occurrence or an infrastructure adapter that
ultimately invokes a source callable. Source validator methods use the
Revision-2 typed method record: reserved `callable->node`, declared actuals and
the dynamic values required by `sig`. Inputs, diagnostics, obligations and
produced evidence are ordinary values/links in the call/plan graph or typed C
activation values under sections 7.4 and 21. Tables may encode validator
relations, but only as explicitly linked operands.

Validator dependency traversal, cycle detection, memoization, conflict policy
and termination belong to the explicit policy-combination operation of section
19.19; they are not core runtime `merge`, and they cannot publish bindings or
alter structural occurrence lookup.

19.22.H. Revision-1 Descriptor Evidence Profile (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.22.H.


19.23. Explicit Description Data and Graph Composition

19.23.1. Explicit Description-Structure Composition

A description record such as `Button`, `Circle`, `ArchiveService` or `equals`
is an ordinary Lmx Structure reached through a direct link, a structural path or
an explicit root-relative lookup. Composing such records is an explicit
operation over those Structures; it is not hidden class-hierarchy copying, an
attached descriptor, runtime visibility state or a side effect of adding table
rows. If the operation used is `merge`, section 19.17 runtime tree-copy semantics
apply without a second descriptor-only algorithm.

19.23.2. Import / Provides / Satisfies

The registered `import:` receiver makes the explicitly imported unit's linked
methods and Structure providers available to the generated program. It does not
copy a runtime namespace or instantiate every imported declaration. `provides`
and `satisfies`, if a profile uses them, are ordinary data fields and do not
merge implementations merely by existing. A
program may cache compatibility results in a Table:

    provides[Array][Indexed] = true
    provides[Array][Sized] = true

External roles/contracts are explicit Structure views over operations.
Structural compatibility is computed by an explicit
`implements(varA varB Consumer)` walk over the supplied live/source Structures
and `Consumer`; a stored
`satisfies` row is only cached application data.

19.23.3. Conflicts

Compile/link conflicts, graph occurrence conflicts and ordinary Table merge
conflicts are distinct. A same-name child remains an ordered real occurrence;
unqualified lookup selects the first (`[0]`) occurrence. An explicit
`implements(new old Consumer)` check may reject a proposed graph update, but it
does not publish a runtime binding or make a later occurrence shadow the first.
Ordinary Table merge remains ordinary data processing and does not acquire a
hidden visibility rule merely because a Table permits repeated keys.


19.24. Consumption and Query Operators (Revision 2)

19.24.R2. Normative Consumption Model

Every consumption form receives or reaches actual Lmx nodes. Procedural
consumption follows the selected callable occurrence and invokes the typed source
method with `callable->node`, declared actuals and dynamic values selected by
`sig`. Object/event consumption supplies an
explicit target Message/root plus explicit policy and state Structures;
functional consumption follows explicit callable/operation links without a
reified runtime Namespace; logical consumption constructs ordinary result or
Message graphs from explicit inputs.

Rules, query variables, candidates, continuations and unification state are
Structure nodes in the rule/call graph. An implementation may use C locals as
temporary accelerators, but they are not source-semantic bindings. A
Table/Registry is queried only through an explicit operand/link, and query
results neither change method visibility nor create an ambient name meaning.

19.24.H. Revision-1 Namespace Consumption Profile (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.24.H.


19.25. Lowering, Provider and Codec Operators (Revision 2)

19.25.R2. Normative Lowering and Provider Model

The Revision-2 high-level pipeline is:

    source LMX
        -> P0 source tree
        -> Lmx construction/link recipes and globally named method entries
        -> runtime graph construction at the declared execution points
        -> explicit graph/direct-link/root-relative resolution
        -> ExecutionPlan
        -> execution or L1/L0 lowering

The translator may use compile-time symbol and dependency tables, but it does
not emit a semantic runtime Namespace. An explicit `.lm2` or `.lm3` import may
compile/link selected provider methods and construction recipes. It neither
scans an arbitrary directory nor eagerly instantiates imported Structures.
`.lm2` additionally admits the low-level operations of section 20; this does
not change the Lmx ontology of ordinary source Structures.

Every persistent source graph value is represented by an Lmx node or explicit
graph link. During an activation, declared/dynamic primitives and Structure/Array
reference values may reside in typed C parameters and automatic working locals.
A source call follows a callable Lmx occurrence and invokes its program-wide
reachable, deterministically unique C entry according to its immutable method
record:

    typed_entry(callable->node,
                declared_actuals...,
                dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The `sig` lists the declared actual ABI, ordered hidden dynamic ShortNameIds and
their primitive-value/reference pass modes, result/throw convention and physical
C pointer type. The source-method ABI has no infrastructure parameter for a
Message Thread, reified Namespace, descriptor, closure environment or arena.
The exact C result/return channel is an open ABI item; it must preserve the
declared Lmx result semantics and must not reintroduce typed per-name Structures.
Infrastructure and foreign-ABI adapters may have their required C parameters,
but an adapter calling a source method forms the typed call above.

Ordinary Structures are projected through the universal `Lmx { node, len,
data }` representation and structural traversal. A backend must not generate
an ordinary per-source-name C struct, fixed field offset, attached descriptor
or vtable merely because it knows a source spelling. Direct C struct fields,
unions, native signatures, pointers and inline operators are admitted only for
explicit raw `c.*`/L1 artifacts or as a separately proven optimization whose
observable behavior remains generic Lmx traversal. Ordinary Arrays use typed service-pool descriptors; an Lmx field may store
that reference, as defined in 6.5.

A provider, device profile, codec, validator or lowering rule is explicit Lmx
data and/or a callable Lmx occurrence linked into the program. Provider choice
follows direct links, structural paths or explicitly supplied policy Tables.
An ExecutionPlan retains the chosen callable/provider link so execution does
not repeat ambient resolution. The same source can select a different provider
only through an explicit import, graph/configuration link or policy operand,
not through a different runtime namespace.

`toLmx` and `fromLmx`, when a profile provides them, are ordinary callable Lmx
nodes with explicit codec-policy data. Portable persistence serializes admitted
Structure content and explicit links/identities according to that codec; it is
not a memory image. Raw pointers, allocator state, native handles, address
domains and ABI layout require explicit external-handle/codec policies.

19.25.H. Revision-1 Namespace/Descriptor Lowering Profile (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.25.H.


19.26. Registry and Message Operator Examples

Examples in this section are illustrative ordinary Table values plus explicit
queries/expressions, not ambient registry syntax.

Example operator cell:

    table:
        name: `equals.data`
        columns: class (u32 char) (decimal char)
        rows:
            u32     u32_eq None
            decimal None decimal_eq
    end: `equals.data`

Example proposed L3 routing Structure (the nested declarative `send` form here
is ordinary data, not the implemented positional `send:` statement from item
27):

    route:
        when:
            `equals.data`[userRole][admin]: true
        send:
            to: AdminService
            expression:
                openPanel
    end: route


19.26.1. Explicit Registry, Message and Provider Examples

These optional profile examples use explicit data, not additional grammar or
implicit lookup rules. Rows create no types, names or callables; each consumer
receives its Table or follows an explicit link to it.

Classification and exact keys

    table:
        name: classes
        columns: key kind
        rows:
            Circle layout
            int    primitive
    end: classes

Here `Circle` and `int` are row keys, not declarations. Column annotations and
exact quoted keys are also ordinary table data:

    columns: class (spelling char) (cost serial)

    u32
    result
    implementation
    sqrt_ansi_c
    `+`
    `translator type-->spelling`
    `implementation-->L2 lowering`

    table:
        name: `translator type-->spelling`
        columns: class (spelling char)
        rows:
            u8  "uint8_t"
            u16 "uint16_t"
            u32 "uint32_t"
    end: `translator type-->spelling`

A translator explicitly given this Table can read:

    `translator type-->spelling`[u8][spelling]  -> "uint8_t"
    `translator type-->spelling`[u16][spelling] -> "uint16_t"

The strings name target spellings; the Table does not alter source semantics.

Relations and explicit composition

A binary relation may be visualized as a sparse matrix:

    table `equals.relations`

              decimal          real             int
    decimal   decimal_eq       decimal_real_eq  decimal_int_eq
    real      real_decimal_eq  real_eq          real_int_eq
    int       int_decimal_eq   int_real_eq      int_eq

Another view can organize the same relation data around one operand:

    table `decimal.relations`

              decimal          real             int
    plus      decimal_plus     decimal_real_add decimal_int_add
    minus     decimal_minus    decimal_real_sub -
    equals    decimal_eq       decimal_real_eq  decimal_int_eq

A table consumer may define ordered import and priority:

    table:
        source
        name: appEquals
        import:
            numericEquals
            textEquals
        priority:
            numericEquals: 20
            textEquals: 10
        String:
            String: string_eq
    end: appEquals

This composes explicit table data. Its duplicate/conflict policy does not
replace Structure occurrence order or the `[0]name` default.

Construction and query remain explicit operations:

    table:
        name: equalsData
        columns: left right implementation
        rows:
            decimal int decimal_int_eq
            int     int int_eq
    end: equalsData

    Registry: numericRegistry equalsData

    query: numericRegistry equalsData decimal int

Cell payloads and side tables

The selected payload need not repeat the keys that located it:

    plus[decimal][int] = decimal_int_add

This is explanatory lookup notation, not source assignment syntax. A table
profile may store an implementation key directly and keep result, effect,
lowering and cost information in separate Tables:

    table:
        name: plus
        columns: class (int char) (u32 char)
        rows:
            decimal decimal_int_add None
            u32     None            plus_u32_u32_l2
    end: plus

    table:
        name: `plus cell-->result`
        columns: class (result char)
        rows:
            decimal_int_add decimal
            plus_u32_u32_l2 u32
    end: `plus cell-->result`

    table:
        name: effects
        columns: class (effect char)
        rows:
            decimal_int_add pure
            plus_u32_u32_l2 pure
    end: effects

    table:
        name: level
        columns: class (level char)
        rows:
            decimal_int_add L2
            plus_u32_u32_l2 L2
    end: level

    table:
        name: `implementation-->L2 lowering`
        columns: class (symbol char)
        rows:
            plus_u32_u32_l2 "+"
    end: `implementation-->L2 lowering`

    table:
        name: cost
        columns: class (cost serial)
        rows:
            decimal_int_add 10
            plus_u32_u32_l2 1
    end: cost

`None` in these Tables is the selected table profile's missing-cell marker;
it does not define an expression-level literal. Such profile data is separate
from runtime service records such as Array `{len, data}` and method `{addr, sig}`.

A diagnostic is ordinary data too:

    table:
        source
        name: plus
        decimal:
            String:
                diagnostic:
                    code: incompatibleOperands
                    message: "plus(decimal, String) is not defined"
                end: diagnostic
    end: plus

An explicit query may follow intermediate keys for an n-ary relation:

    multiply[Matrix][Matrix] -> MatrixMultiplySignature
    MatrixMultiplySignature[leftCols][rightRows] -> matrix_mul_impl

Message addresses and external bodies

An envelope can separate its service endpoint from its receiving route:

    endpoint = "https://archive.example.org"
    route    = "/inbox/save"
    URI      = "https://archive.example.org/inbox/save"

These equations illustrate address composition. A local address omits the
endpoint; neither an endpoint nor a route grants authority:

    to:
        route: "/inbox/save"
    end: to

A short Message can identify a large body through a validated cursor:

    message:
        id: "report-42"
        to:
            route: "/reports/process"
        end: to
        expression:
            process:
                body:
                    cursor: reportStore\flightLog\segment42
                    range: current
                    mode: readStream
                end: body
            end: process
        end: expression
    end: message

Policy combination validates the cursor, range, authority and lifetime before
execution. The body can then be read incrementally. Encoding and migration are
explicit operations, for example:

    expression:
        archive\read:
            compressedPart

    expression:
        decrypt:
            ticket
            encryptedPart

    expression:
        migrate:
            oldPayload
            targetDescriptor

Here `targetDescriptor` is explicitly supplied description data, not hidden
metadata. Each selected operation must resolve to an admitted callable.

Provider data

An explicitly linked operation can use these Tables to select a provider:

    table:
        name: `sqrt.dispatch`
        columns: class (result char) (implementation char)
        rows:
            BigFloat BigFloat sqrt_mpfr
            fixedQ16 fixedQ16 sqrt_ana
            f64      f64      sqrt_ansi_c
    end: `sqrt.dispatch`

    table:
        name: `implementation-->L2 lowering`
        columns: class (symbol char)
        rows:
            sqrt_mpfr   "mpfr_sqrt"
            sqrt_ana    "ana_sqrt"
            sqrt_ansi_c "sqrt"
    end: `implementation-->L2 lowering`

    table:
        name: providerOf
        columns: class (provider char)
        rows:
            sqrt_mpfr MPFR
            sqrt_ana ANA_SQRT
            sqrt_ansi_c CPU_C_Libm
    end: providerOf

The operation's explicit data links to `sqrt.dispatch`; policy checks admit
a concrete callable/provider, and the ExecutionPlan retains that choice.
Changing the provider changes the explicit configuration, not the expression
syntax or any ambient namespace.


19.27. Registry and Message Open Points

Open points for the optional explicit Registry/query library:

    exact optimized storage layout for CellKey -> CellPayload and optional side tables;
    whether the first registry backend is hash tables, SQLite or both;
    explicit table-query ranking/ambiguity defaults (not Structure occurrence lookup);
    how much current L2 hardcoded lowering moves into explicit backend Tables;
    exact Mix placement/value indexes over the common Message/Lmx graph;
    standard diagnostic format for failed table lookup.


19.28. Message Thread and Runtime Operators (Revision 2)

19.28.R2. Normative Message, Mix and Thread Model

19.28.R2.1. One graph ontology

A Message is a root and its reachable Lmx graph. Envelope fields, routes,
payloads, policy, evidence, source-visible scheduler state, continuations and
diagnostics are nodes/links in that graph. Containment, ordered `[0]` lookup
and the reverse-name index follow section 2.

Each Message owns one logical arena (19.29.2). Mix must be rewritten into
cooperating Messages with independent arenas and execution lanes; placement,
intervals, addresses, mirrors and values are their graph/index roles. This
applies to Mix generally. The current Java implementation has not completed
the rewrite, and a whole document is not assigned one global serial lane.
Section 19.29.2.1 defines the target and its open role-to-Message grouping.

19.28.R2.2. Message Thread

An inactive Message becomes an L3 Thread when launched by a scheduler. Its
actor state is a runtime role. Queue, worker, transport, diagnostic and cache
infrastructure may live outside the graph; source-visible Message, scheduler,
route or policy values require explicit graph links. Name resolution and
dynamic C arguments follow section 21, independently of that infrastructure.

Every L3 Thread has a FIFO inbox/outbox. It executes at most one turn at a
time, consuming at most one admitted input per turn. Serial turns make ordinary
mutation of its arena safe without a source-visible lock.

Different Messages may execute concurrently. Cooperative loops, worker counts,
worker migration between turns and fairness are runtime choices; FIFO order
and same-Message turn serialization are fixed.

19.28.R2.3. Calls, turns and handler state

Every source fn/sub/handler/validator/generator is represented by a callable
Lmx occurrence whose `data` identifies a correctly typed globally linked
method entry and canonical signature through
`all_methods_array {addr,sig}`. Calling source code means:

    typed_entry(callable->node,
                declared_actuals...,
                dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The first supplied pointer is the Structure containing that callable occurrence
and is source `node`. Declared and dynamic primitive values are copied by value;
Structure/Array references are copied reference values. Used own node fields
become dirty-tracked C working locals. A host scheduler callback may receive
infrastructure parameters and then adapt them at the boundary by finding the
callable and forming this typed source call.

This specification distinguishes two meanings historically called "hidden
argument":

    semantic dynamic argument
        a required bare-name value inferred by the translator, listed in `sig`
        and passed by every caller; this is normative;

    hidden infrastructure argument
        a MessageThread, worker, ExecutionContext, reified Namespace,
        descriptor, closure environment or Arena pointer added behind the
        source model; this is forbidden for generated source methods.

Neither term refers to machine registers/stack details of the platform C ABI.
Private runtime helpers may accept ordinary C service parameters, including an
arena derived from `node`. The exact result/return channel remains open and is
recorded by `sig`.

A turn dequeues at most one admitted input from the Message's FIFO, performs
policy combination, executes an admitted callable, stages results/outgoing
Messages in FIFO order and reaches the common cleanup/publication/scheduling
boundary. Persistent handler state, explicit histories, retry policy data,
cleanup actions and publishable results that must survive a return are Lmx
subtrees/links. Ordinary active declared/dynamic values and used own fields
reside in the C activation under sections 7.4 and 21. A suspended yield may
preserve that activation as a private continuation. The C stack is the sole
implicit activation history; it is never copied into the Message graph
automatically.

`stop:`, `exit:`, cancellation and ordinary control exits may be implemented
cooperatively at a safe turn/control boundary. Their exact scheduling status
machine is a runtime profile. Every edge that removes a generated C activation
evaluates/retains its outgoing result or diagnostic, runs a dirty-only
pre-cleanup checkpoint, executes applicable cleanups, and runs one final
dirty-only checkpoint immediately before removal. It cannot add a hidden
infrastructure argument to source methods, bypass explicit graph state or
redefine source `return`. Compiler-generated dynamic value arguments remain
required. The C return channel and result-root convention remain open where the
specification has not yet selected them.

19.28.R2.4. Validation, dispatch and queues

An incoming Message is processed by the Revision-2 pipeline of section 19.19:

    receive/decode
        -> explicit route/target link or explicit-root lookup
        -> policy combination and evidence
        -> callable/provider selection
        -> ExecutionPlan
        -> typed_entry(callable->node,
                       declared_actuals...,
                       dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)
        -> explicit result/outgoing Message handling

This policy-combination stage is not core `merge`. It cannot install a
Namespace, attach descriptors or copy method bodies. Core `merge` remains only
the lazy execution-time containment-tree copy of section 19.17; if a Message
method executes `merge`, its new occurrences receive rebased `node` links and
copied ShortNameIds under that rule.

Inbox, outbox, route and generator data may be graph nodes or infrastructure
indexes over explicit Message identities. Generated Messages receive no
implicit trust and re-enter the same receiving pipeline. Concurrent admissions
must be atomically linearized into a Message's FIFO; their order before that
linearization may be unspecified, but dequeue and successful staged publication
follow the resulting FIFO order. Capacity, backpressure, failure and retry
policies remain explicit profile decisions. Queue ordering is unrelated to
Structure name lookup or occurrence order.

19.28.R2.5. Construction and delivery

Message templates and declared handler state are construction recipes. They
are materialized at the execution point required by the source operation, not
by scanning a directory or eagerly constructing every imported declaration
before `main`. A template instance is an ordinary Message/Lmx graph and may
itself be input to later runtime `merge`.

Delivery between Message roots must select an explicit policy such as copying
an admitted graph fragment into the destination/new Message arena,
transferring/adopting compatible storage from an inactive Message, serializing
through a codec, following an explicit immutable-pool link or passing an
explicit foreign handle. The final operation set and identity/retargeting rules
remain open. Every live Message still has exactly one logical arena: an
adoption/splice operation must merge ownership and retire the conflicting
source identity, while activation preserves the activated Message's own arena.
An accidental raw mutable cross-Message pointer is never permitted merely
because both Messages are in one process.

Delivery/adoption/policy combination must not be named or specified as core
`merge`. A requested structural `merge` always makes fresh Lmx occurrences,
rewrites containment `node` links in the copy, preserves links to compiled
global method entries, and can consume a previous merge result. The exact rule
for retargeting non-containment identity-bearing graph edges is an explicit
merge-policy/open item, never descriptor composition.

19.28.R2.6. C/runtime boundary

Worker pools, OS threads, transports, codecs, collectors and foreign resources
may expose ordinary infrastructure C ABIs. Their persistent or cross-boundary
source state remains reachable from Lmx nodes, with the reverse-name index
maintained under section 2. Every call into a source method uses the exact
typed `{addr,sig}` boundary of 21.8. Active arguments, snapshots and dirty flags
remain in the C activation or its private suspension continuation (21.5–21.10).

19.28.H. Historical Hosted Runtime Profile

Historical runtime text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.28.H.
The document/addressing model below remains current; execution follows 19.29.2.1.

19.28.10. World Wide Mix

This section is a document and addressing model. It is not a replacement for
the P0 parser, Mix overlay markup in section 0.0.2, or
`implements(varA varB Consumer)` in sections 2.1 through 2.4 and 19.21.3. The
current hosted bootstrap does not implement World Wide Mix paths, prefix locks
or mirrors.

The target Mix runtime must coordinate independently scheduled Message
participants (section 19.29.2.1). A World Wide Mix placement/addressing tree
does not put an entire document into one Message or one arena. The current Java
cursor threads and page store require a rewrite into this Message model.

Mix here is not only an overlay on LMX source. It is a universal document:
a placement tree of nodes, memory cells at the leaves, and dynamic marks,
cursors and attributes over those positions. World Wide Mix is that document
when a node can be named by a hierarchical path from another carrier. The
Internet is not required for that (section 19.28.10.1).

19.28.10.1. Not the Web and Not a File

The Web combines a URL, bytes and a Content-Type. Meaning appears after
download and a separate parser (HTML, JPEG, JSON). The type lives in a header,
not in how a consumer walks the document.

A disk file is a linear byte sequence addressed by offset from the start.
Insertion in the middle shifts every later address. Editors and networks then
live in a different world from the file.

World Wide Mix is a *placement* tree: where a node sits in the world. LMX is a
*value* tree: which fields a Structure has and what a Consumer touches. They
are different trees. They meet in a cell: a Mix address holds an LMX value
(Structure, Array or Primitive). The path `3.17.4` is not `file\close`.
`implements(varA varB Consumer)` does not number Mix siblings.

Raw input before a receiver is substrate in a cell. After recognition the same
cell may hold an LMX tree with finer leaves. The document need not already be
a «file» or a «picture»; that is decided by the Consumer of the LMX value, not
by the Mix address scheme.

World Wide here is not the Web and not IP. The Internet is not required.
It is the same path of numbers (`3.17.4`) that can be named from another
carrier. The carrier may be anything: RAM, a disk sector, a wire, radio, a
flash drive, a carrier pigeon. Hop codec and occupancy change with the
carrier; the address does not.

A physical link carries a page or a chunk along an already known path. A
pigeon carries a serialized node or a short envelope (path, capability, which
Consumer). That is transport, not Mix topology.

HTTP POST (`application/lmx`) is one way to carry LMX messages. It is a layer
beside Mix (section 19.28.10.15). Without it Mix is the same: bunches,
next/prev, prefix locks, mirrors. Neighbour `3.18` is a neighbour in a bunch,
not a DNS host.

The world is a placement tree, not a socket network. Wide means the address
is not «the file on this machine at byte 12044».

19.28.10.2. Two Trees

    Mix (this section)
        topology: siblings, realm prefix, bunch, lock, mirror, cursor;
        edge: neighbour index, a natural number, written `3.17.4`;
        bunch: the occupied children of one prefix; next/prev only inside it;
        leaf: a cell (memory or a reference to a value);
        overlay of marks on positions of this tree, not a second parse tree.

    LMX (sections 2 and 19.21.3)
        value: Lmx reference; primitive/callable/branch interpretation follows
               the address domain of `data`;
        edge: field name, `file\close`, merge occurrences;
        compatibility: explicit `implements(varA varB Consumer)` over the used
               tree of *that* value;
        P0 source builds this tree; `{...}` places Mix marks *on* it.

Analogies are allowed (thin or fat Consumer, shadowing, «if you did not touch
it, do not require it»). Identity is not:

    inserting a Mix sibling changes index `3.18`, not a Structure field;
    runtime Structure merge appends copied `close` occurrences in forward
        order; `[0]close` remains the default, independently of cell address;
    a 33rd Mix child is a new slot at that level;
    a 33rd LMX field is a new name occurrence in a Structure.

The parser may still build both at once: a P0 Structure and a Mix anchor on
its position. The anchor is addressed by a Mix path; fields are read as LMX.

19.28.10.3. Address: a Path of Integers

There is no bound on the number of levels. An address is a sequence of
non-negative integers (ℕ₀) from the root to a cell. Path length is the depth
of that cell, not the height of the whole tree: branches under one parent may
differ in length (section 19.28.10.6). A level index is any such integer, with
no ceiling.

A cell may be a byte, a word, a buffer or a child Structure. The index counts
neighbours, not bytes. The written path is not LMX source grammar: numbers
separated by dots, of different lengths because the numbers differ.

    3.17.4.1023
    0.0.5
    3.100000

The address is not tied to bytes. Whoever stores it chooses a codec: varint, a
chain of widths matching live fan-out

    5 bits -> 12 bits -> 6 bits -> 10 bits

or anything else. Externally these are the same numbers. `3.17` remains
`3.17` when a slot starts being stored in 12 bits instead of 5. Width is
compression of an integer, not an address type.

A prefix of k numbers names a subtree. A shorter address is a shallower path.
Leading zeros in text do not count: `03.17` = `3.17`.

Storage widths (5 bits, 12 bits) are not different path types. Otherwise a
node that gained a 33rd child would change its address type.

19.28.10.4. How Many Bits Storage Uses

Occupancy is a property of the carrier, not of the address.

    Device RAM
        typically about 5 bits: tens of children. A compact vector of live
        children plus, if needed, a map from path index to local slot.

    Small network
        also about 5 bits, when that is the carrier: tens of machines, not
        thousands of hosts per level. A network here is an example carrier,
        not a required layer.

    Disk
        how many cells fit in a 4 KB sector or a 32 KB cluster (256 Kbit)
        depends on cell size. With a 4-byte pointer: about 10 bits per
        sector, about 12 per cluster. A buffer cell means fewer neighbours
        in the same sector.

A type chain on a path (`u5.u12.u6.u10`) is how that node was serialized, not
what it is called. Mirrors of one page (section 19.28.10.12): RAM and network
are narrow; disk is slightly wider; the number path is one.

Unallocated numbers at a Mix level are simply not allocated. That is not an
LMX unused path: Structure fields and neighbour slots are different trees.

19.28.10.5. Cell, Node, Document

A cell is a leaf: a raw memory region in the Mix store, not a slot of a
Message graph (section 19.29.2.1). Its description is an ordinary Structure
(contract plus architecture: count/integer/opaque, range, spelling); see
section 2.2.2. It is not a closed list of kinds such as «int, FILE, image».
A Message kept in a cell is stored as raw payload. Destruction of that
Message does not walk Mix; a cursor deletes the cell, or deletes itself
from Mix if it is that Message.

A node is a Structure: named or positional fields and child references. On
disk it is on the order of a 4 KB sector or a 32 KB cluster (256 Kbit). In
RAM and on a small network it is sparse, with tens of children.

A document is a root plus a tree of nodes plus a Mix overlay on tree
positions. It is not a filename extension. A file is one way to serialize
node pages.

A Mix position is not a byte offset. It is a dynamic address in the tree (a
path of integers plus a cursor inside the cell when the cell is a buffer).
Inserting or deleting a sibling changes only indices at that level, not
«every byte after the point». Cursors therefore survive edits, as in the Mix
design note.

19.28.10.6. Uneven Branches; next/prev on a Bunch

The Mix tree is not complete and not a grid of one height. Under one parent a
child may be a leaf while a neighbour is a node with a long subtree:

    3.17.4          a leaf, a member of bunch(3.17)
    3.17.5          a node, the same bunch
    3.17.5.0.2      a cell deeper in the second branch

Depth is the length of a particular path, not a world level. There is no ruler
«every cell at depth 4» and no next along such a ruler.

A bunch is the occupied child indices of one prefix:

    bunch(P) = { i ∈ ℕ₀ | P.i is allocated }

Gaps are not in the bunch: if `3.17` holds 0, 4, 5, the bunch is {0, 4, 5},
not 0..5. next/prev is the neighbour in that set, in ℕ₀ order:

    next(3.17.4) = 3.17.5
    prev(3.17.4) = 3.17.0

not `3.17.5.0.2` (that is another bunch, deeper) and not «the next document in
the world».

A cursor has two orthogonal moves:

    down / up      lengthen or shorten the path (the branch);
    next / prev    change the last index inside the same bunch.

next does not climb to the parent, does not descend into a foreign branch, and
does not jump into another prefix's bunch. The end of a bunch is no next, not
«the absolute next Mix». The world root is a bunch too: next there walks
occupied roots, not every disk on the planet.

Connectivity is therefore not absolute. Absolute connectivity would be a
doubly-linked list of all documents at the base, with overflow pouring into
the neighbour on that list. Here neighbourhood is local: if a cell fills, the
writer deepens this branch, takes a free index in *this* bunch, compacts, or
refuses. Walking next is navigation, not a spill chute.

A lock on prefix P covers bunch(P) and every deeper bunch inside P. A mirror
of P mirrors this uneven subtree, not a chain of absolute next links.

19.28.10.7. World-Wide Realm Prefixes

World hierarchy is a path prefix in the placement tree, not the Internet and
not a fixed number of slots «for the planet». How many levels belong to
realm, volume, document or chapter is a profile, not an address canon.

Example meaning (profile, not language):

    first levels     realm / administrator / planet-service
    next             document / volume
    deeper           chapter, section, block, cell

The first level has as many roots as were allocated. Names are not DNS: they
are numbers. A human name, if needed, is a Mix attribute or a Structure field
on the node, not part of the address. The address is stable; the human name
may change.

An LMX message need not carry the body. It carries a path (length plus
indices), a capability, and which Consumer will walk. The body is read by
cursor in chunks. Mix cursors for large bodies are illustrated with
messages in section 19.26.1; here the address is simply hierarchical and
world-wide, with no depth ceiling.

19.28.10.8. Mix Overlay on This Tree

Mix is still not a second parse tree.

The same addresses hold:

    active marks and intervals (colour, link, diagnostic);
    overlapping sets;
    cursors of several consumers;
    locks on path prefixes (section 19.28.10.11);
    attributes such as «still a raw buffer» or «already a File».

The LMX parser still builds P0 Structures and places Mix anchors in parallel.
World Wide Mix adds only that an anchor has a path of integers (`3.17.4`, or
however it is stored locally) that can be sent to another device without
«open my file at byte 12044».

19.28.10.9. Type Lives in the Cell's LMX Value, Not in the Mix Address

The address `3.17.4` does not type the contents. Typing happens when a
Consumer walks the LMX value stored in that cell:

    implements(varA varB Consumer)

    varA       the LMX value in the cell (or what is being substituted);
    varB       a role (File, paragraph, table, raw word);
    Consumer   who walks *value fields*, not Mix neighbours.

Strict mode: FileManager uses `file\close` and `file\read` — a fat LMX used
tree. Relaxed mode: File consumes a primitive FILE into a `handle` field — a
thin LMX leaf, converter selected by merge key.

Until someone asks for File, a Mix cell may hold substrate (JPEG, UTF-8, a
word). Meaning is attached by an LMX Consumer, not by the Mix path and not by
MIME.

19.28.10.10. Merge of Pages and Contracts

A document node may be assembled by merging Structures: a base page plus
audit plus local fields. Merge appends copied children in operand/body order.
The first same-name child remains `[0]` and unqualified lookup; later children
are `[1]`, `[2]`, and so on. Merge does not perform in-place replacement.

The same holds for cell descriptions: `u32` is merge(cell, count/integer) plus
range `0..2^32-1` plus spelling. `FILE` is merge(cell, opaque). On disk both
may occupy one slot in a sector-sized page; the contracts differ.

19.28.10.11. Message Coordination; Historical Prefix-Lock Profile

Common Mix does not define a portable mutex, reader/writer lock, byte-range
lock, prefix-lock algorithm or nested-lock acquisition order. Such algorithms
depend on shared memory, host scheduling and a concrete carrier; they cannot be
ported unchanged across RAM, HTTP, radio, disk and physical mail.

Mutable Mix state belongs to a Message arena. Its L3 Thread receives commands
through FIFO delivery and executes at most one turn at a time. Parallelism is
between distinct Messages/arenas. An operation on a path prefix is therefore a
Message to the owner of that mutable state. A capability for the prefix may be
an ordinary Lmx field of the Message; it grants authority and does not expose a
shared mutable pointer or become a mutex.

An operation spanning several Message owners requires an explicit Message
protocol with stated failure/retry/commit rules. A particular L2/storage
profile may implement native locks, atomics or prefix-lock ordering, and an
application may model a distributed lock as Messages. Neither is an implicit
common-LMX/Mix synchronization primitive.

The older Java/profile design in which writers lock prefixes, readers proceed
under a reader/writer policy, nested locks follow ancestor order and listener
callbacks report release is implementation history. During the Message-style
refactor those callbacks become delivered Messages and the lock algorithm must
not be copied one-for-one. The Structure occurrence rule remains independent:
unqualified lookup still selects the first `[0]` occurrence.

19.28.10.12. Mirrors

A mirror is not a second document and not a git clone as a kind. It is
another path (another realm prefix) whose nodes, for a given reader Consumer,
cover the same fields as the original.

Everyday mirrors exist without a «second document»:

    address             a path of integers;
    RAM / small network storage of about 5 bits per hop, tens of children
                        or machines;
    disk                storage of about 10..12 bits under a sector or
                        cluster.

RAM is a narrow projection of the same page: the Consumer walks the same
path numbers; only live slots sit in memory. A network of tens of machines
is the same kind of narrow projection.

A network mirror is that relation on another prefix:

    primary     the prefix written to;
    mirror      the prefix read from while it catches up.

Writes go to primary through its owner Message's FIFO. The mirror catches up
through change Messages over Mix (as history and undo do), not a new document
version on every edit. It may be read-only or serve the last caught-up page
under an explicit protocol. A thin mark reader may tolerate lag; a writer that
is performing close must not write to a stale mirror.

Commands admitted by one owner are FIFO-linearized. Conflicting changes owned
by different Messages require an explicit coordination protocol rather than a
hidden common lock. As with Mix marks, the occurrence chain is kept; last-wins
is only a projection for a simple renderer; a semantic Consumer may see the
whole chain and merge along its used tree.

A mirror's name is an attribute, not part of the address. A mirror's address
is its own path. Original and mirror are comparable only through
`implements` for a concrete reader, not as «forever the same bytes».

19.28.10.13. What This Model Does Not Do

It does not replace the P0 parser and does not introduce `Value = ℕ₀`.
It does not bound tree depth or the magnitude of a level index.
It does not force branches to one depth and does not make a global
next/prev of all Mix documents.
It does not fix cell size and does not tie addresses to bytes.
It does not become the Web: no mandatory URL text, DNS, or MIME as type.
It does not require the Internet, IP, or HTTP. The carrier may be anything.
It does not version the document on every edit (history is a listener over
Mix).
It does not introduce `flock` on «the whole file» and does not make a mirror
a second document kind.

19.28.10.14. Minimal Core

    address = a path of ℕ₀ integers, written with dots;
    depth is the length of that path; branches under one parent may differ;
    bunch = the occupied children of one prefix; gaps are not in the bunch;
    next/prev only inside a bunch, not an absolute chain of documents;
    hop storage is the carrier's affair (a 5->12->6->10 chain, varint, ...);
    the carrier may be anything: RAM, disk, wire, radio, flash, pigeon;
    the Internet is not required; HTTP is one message transport beside Mix;
    typical occupancy: RAM and a small network about 5 bits, disk about
    10..12;
    RAM node: sparse, about 32 children — a working mirror of the page;
    cursor = path plus offset inside a buffer cell;
    down/up walks a branch; next/prev walks a bunch;
    insert/delete at a level shifts only sibling indices in that bunch;
    coordination = explicit Messages/capabilities; a native/prefix lock is an
        optional L2/storage protocol, not common Mix semantics;
    mirror = another prefix plus a catch-up listener, not a second kind;
    Mix attributes hang on a cursor or interval, not on a byte range;
    `implements(varA varB Consumer)` types the LMX value in the cell, not
    the Mix path.

Realm profiles, page serialization and capabilities on a path prefix are
delivery and carrier around the document, not the document model itself.

19.28.10.15. Relation to REST/LMX and Message Queues

Section 19.28.7 moves *messages*: a complete LMX document as owned bytes into
a FIFO inbox, locally by route or remotely by HTTP POST (`application/lmx`).
The unit is an actor turn. REST/LMX does not place document pages in a
neighbour slot of a Mix path.

One arena per Message, FIFO order and one active turn per L3 Thread are common
semantics. The carrier and host execution mapping are not: the same Message may
travel through an in-process queue, HTTP, wire, radio, flash or physical mail,
and its turns may run cooperatively or on a worker pool without changing that
boundary.

This section places *cells*: a path of integers, cursors, coordination and
mirrors. The unit is a document node. A message may *name* a Mix path instead
of carrying the body (section 19.26.1). Transport still ships only the short
envelope; the receiver walks Mix. The two layers stack. They are not the same
tree. Transport is not Mix and need not be IP: the same envelope can go on a
wire, radio, a flash drive or a carrier pigeon. Without HTTP, Mix does not
change.

A Mix sibling (`3.18` next to `3.17`) is next in bunch(`3`), not a host and
not a spill chute. A machine appears only if a profile maps a realm prefix
onto one. Disk-full is storage-profile policy, not an implication of the
path and not «pour into the absolute next»:

    refuse the write;
    compact live slots;
    evict cold cells to a *declared* mirror prefix that published a
    capability.

It is not «when this disk fills, fetch films onto the roommate's disk».
Without a mirror capability the write fails. With one, a Consumer may *pull*
a cell by cursor when it actually uses it. Pushing an unread movie onto an
unrelated neighbour is neither Mix placement nor REST/LMX delivery.


19.29. Memory Model

19.29.0. Scope, Status and Level Independence

This section defines lifetime and allocation for the Structure/reference model
of section 2 and the Array representation of 6.5. Every Message owns one logical
arena (19.29.2); reachability and end-turn collection govern reclamation
(19.29.4). Live identity and reverse-index consistency are preserved under any
chosen physical allocator or collector. Mix's Message-based target is 19.29.2.1.

The second translator has two profiles, sharing these memory rules:

    L2 is the complete language, including explicit machine storage,
       addresses, unchecked access, raw allocation and low-level
       synchronization when the selected profile provides them;

    L3 is the high-level subset of L2 which excludes those low-level machine
       operations.

L3 retains graph mutation, Tables/Registries, messages and Message Threads.
The profile distinction concerns machine operations, not authority, purity or
sandboxing. Optimization preserves graph identity and reachability-defined
lifetime; incidental C storage duration cannot define source lifetime. The
exact physical C result/failure carrier remains open (21.13).


19.29.1. Core Lmx Memory Rules and Reference Representation

Section 2 defines the sole Structure header, containment, address-domain
classification, reverse-name index and reference identity. Section 6.5 defines
direct Array-descriptor references and their separate contiguous backing.
Section 21 defines C activation values and method publication.

Memory management must preserve these identities. Lmx nodes and live descriptor
entries stay at stable addresses unless relocation updates every affected
reference, root and side-table key atomically. A direct reference is used
directly, not resolved again by name.

Every Message owns one arena (19.29.2). Mutable graph data cannot be raw-shared
between Messages; immutable primitive atoms and method records may be shared.
Runtime helpers derive the arena from a supplied address/root or allocation-
domain metadata. Ownership is neither a fourth Lmx word nor an extra hidden
source-method parameter.

Explicit L2 machine storage remains outside ordinary Structure semantics.
Its C layout/duration cannot replace the identity or lifetime of an Lmx value.


19.29.2. One Message Arena and Allocation Domains

Every Message, inactive or running, owns one logical arena throughout its
turns and worker migration. L3 source does not select it, and Lmx has no arena
field. The arena may contain multiple non-overlapping blocks/address domains.

Its mutable storage includes Lmx nodes, child payloads, persistent own fields,
non-interned primitives, Array descriptors/backing and runtime merge results.
Immutable primitive pools, linked method code/records and explicitly foreign
L2 resources follow their own domain contracts. Runtime helpers may derive the
arena from a node address and its registered range.

A recommended implementation uses a bump pointer and backing-block list;
capacity is not a source parameter:

    if the request fits in the current block, bump;
    if the current block is full, take another host block (at least the
        request, otherwise a default or doubling size) and bump there;
    if the request is larger than the default block, give it a dedicated
        block of that size.

Filling a block preserves existing pointers; allocation fails only if the host
refuses another block. Nodes may span blocks and a physical domain may span
registered ranges. An Array's owning backing remains one contiguous rectangle
(6.5), which may occupy a dedicated block. Block membership is not a type tag.

Growth factors, block sizes, free lists and collector algorithms are physical
choices; the one logical arena and end-turn collection boundary are fixed.
Frames, handlers, attempts, transactions, branches and leaves do not introduce
separate source-semantic arenas. Parser P0 nodes and derived runtime Structures
allocate in the owning Message arena like other Structures.


19.29.2.1. Target Rewrite of Mix into Cooperating Messages

Mix must be rewritten into cooperating LMX Messages for concurrency and
scaling: each owns state in its arena and processes FIFO serial turns, while
independent participants run in parallel and coordinate by Messages. The
current procedural, multithreaded Java page/slot/cursor implementation has not
completed this rewrite; its locks and callbacks do not define the target model.

Cursor services, document coordination, placement/page services and
notifications require explicit ownership and communication routes. Their exact
grouping into Messages, physical workers and transports remains open. The target
supports many participants without requiring a separate Message per page,
cell, listener or cursor, or one serial Message for the whole document.
Participants do not implicitly share mutable storage.

Placement paths identify sparse slots; value/reference links express content
and relationships. A slot may hold serialized text/blob bytes. A receiver
materializes live content under an owning Message when needed; stored bytes
are not themselves a live subtree or execution lane. Live participants still
coordinate by Messages when storage or transport uses blobs.

Deletion/compaction, listener delivery and cursor shutdown must become FIFO
Message-style graph operations. Existing Java behavior remains implementation
status until that migration is complete. Method working state follows section
21 and graph lifetime follows reachability (19.29.4), independently of lexical
scope. L3 exposes no arena-selection argument or implicit registry selection.


19.29.3. Structure Graph and Array Rectangle

The two representations have different jobs:

    Structure occurrence: Lmx {node, len, data}
        node -> containment parent
        data -> classified primitive / children / method / reference domain

    Array reference -> entry in typed service array {len, data}
                                                  data -> contiguous elements

A Structure's `node` links form an acyclic containment tree; its other
references may form a shared or cyclic graph. Child enumeration and short-name
lookup follow section 2.

An Array descriptor is not an Lmx node. Its pool's address range identifies its
type; its own `data` points to a separate rectangular row-major block (6.5).
Primitive element cells need no Lmx headers or `node` links. An Lmx field may
store the descriptor reference, but that field is not the Array.


19.29.4. Reachability and Collection

Arena membership is not liveness. A node, descriptor or backing allocation is
live while reachable from roots through edges defined by its address domain.

Roots include the Message root, active `node` arguments, exact own-field
occurrences retained by C frames, declared/dynamic Structure or Array references,
results, continuations, scheduler handles and explicit application handles.
Tracing follows containment children and required parent links, payload
references, Array-descriptor-to-backing links, and reference-valued Array cells.
Primitive cells and immutable method records follow their domain's policy.

The reverse-name index is not a root; remove entries for dead occurrences.
Moving storage must patch every graph/root/handle reference and atomically
re-key that index. Live descriptor references need the same stability or
complete relocation. Non-moving storage is permitted.

19.29.4.1. Mandatory End-Turn Collection Boundary

Exactly one local collection pass runs at end-turn:

    determine turn outcome
        -> publish successful FIFO outbox OR discard failed staged outbox
        -> release completed inbox/turn roots
        -> collect the owning Message arena

This does not roll back earlier graph mutations. Method/handler return,
`break`, `continue`, `retry`, `redo` and a caught `throw` are not collection
points. An uncaught failure terminating a turn reaches the same boundary.
Termination ultimately releases the whole Message arena.

The pass neither scans foreign mutable arenas nor requires a process-global
heap lock. Same-Message turns do not overlap; other Messages may keep running.
Memory-pressure collection uses this end-turn boundary, not compaction inside
allocation.

Completed handler C frames are gone. A suspended native/continuation frame that
survives end-turn retains every Structure/Array reference as a root until
resume, close or cancellation. A moving collector patches or pins them.

Own fields and constructed Structures are Lmx occurrences; Arrays have separate
descriptor/backing allocations. Returning either reference is legal because its
lifetime follows reachability, not C automatic storage duration. A raw L2 address
surviving relocation requires a pin or patchable handle. Sending/transfer must
establish the receiving ownership under 19.29.7, never leave an implicit pointer
into another Message's reclaimable mutable arena.


19.29.5. Return

Revision 2 fixes the source-method input boundary through the selected callable's
`all_methods_array {addr,sig}` record. The typed entry receives
`callable->node`, declared actual values and the ordered hidden dynamic values
listed by `sig`. The exact C return type, success/failure convention and result
graph location remain unresolved and are listed in
`struct_refactoring_version_2.txt`. No implementation may use that open item to
add a hidden infrastructure MessageThread/arena/Namespace/descriptor/closure
parameter or a per-source C result layout. Every return path follows the exact
section 8.8 sequence: evaluate/root the result once, dirty-checkpoint pre-cleanup
state, execute applicable `finally` cleanups, dirty-checkpoint cleanup writes,
then transfer the result and destroy the activation. Declared/dynamic input
values are never copied back automatically and the caller is not reloaded.

19.29.5.H. Historical Exact-Reference Return Profile

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.29.5.H.


19.29.6. Running Message (L3 Thread)

A running Message is a Message/Lmx execution graph admitted as an L3 Thread.
It owns exactly one logical arena, a FIFO inbox/outbox and one serial execution
lane. No more than one turn of that L3 Thread executes at a time. Whether turns
run on a dedicated OS thread, an actor in a worker pool, a cooperative host loop
or another host scheduling mechanism is an implementation choice (section
19.28), and the same L3 Thread may migrate between host workers without moving
or sharing its arena. Conceptual components include:

    the Message's Lmx graph and its one arena;
    FIFO incoming and staged outgoing message queues;
    selected handler callable occurrences and their containing published-state
        Structures; active invocation history remains only in C frames or
        private yield continuations;
    optional immutable-payload pool;
    optional application Registry/cache references;
    scheduler state.

There is no Message-Thread Namespace object. A handler wrapper supplied by a
scheduler may own infrastructure state, but entry into source code resolves one
callable occurrence and its exact `{addr,sig}` record, materializes declared
actuals and the ordered dynamic values required by that signature, and invokes
the typed entry beginning with `callable->node`. When the wrapper has no source
caller value for a required dynamic name, it copies the immediate
`callable->node\[0]name` fallback; if that field is absent, admission fails. The
wrapper does not add an infrastructure MessageThread, arena, descriptor,
environment or reified Namespace parameter to the source method ABI.

This is a runtime Structure family, not a source-level profile. `thread:` and
`send:` are ordinary L2/L3 receivers. `reply`, `await` and `schedule` remain
proposed until those receivers are declared and lowered.

Any Registry, cache or Table of a Message is ordinary Lmx data reachable from
explicit Message roots in that Message's one arena. It is
optional application state, not an allocator.

Raw mutable Lmx references inside one Message may point only into storage whose
lifetime is valid for that Message or into explicitly shared immutable/address-
domain storage. A raw reference into another Message's reclaimable graph is
invalid until an explicit boundary operation has established its ownership and
lifetime.

Releasing one Message does not require stopping or scanning every Message in
the process. Different Messages may execute concurrently on different workers
because their mutable arenas are isolated. The scheduler must never overlap two
turns of the same Message; ordinary L3 code therefore requires no portable
shared-memory lock for its own graph. Concurrent senders are linearized at FIFO
admission. L1 may synchronize that runtime substrate internally, while explicit
L2 shared-memory/thread profiles define their own low-level contracts.

The current hosted standalone bootstrap begins as one root Message and runs its
external process entry in that Message's turn loop. This includes `parser`,
`trans`, `make`, `finalize` and `buildCore`. Its concrete actor/pool ABI is
implementation status; its one arena, FIFO order and non-overlapping turns
implement the fixed Revision-2 semantics.


19.29.7. Message Boundary

Revision 2 requires every cross-Message graph transfer, sharing or serialization
to be an explicit operation whose lifetime policy covers all Lmx pointers and
address-domain payloads involved. A raw mutable pointer into reclaimable storage
must not silently cross the boundary. Copy/deserialize allocates fresh mutable
state in the destination or new Message arena; activation preserves the
activated Message's existing arena; adoption is legal only when it leaves one
logical owning arena and no concurrently active source owner. The exact
copy/join/activate/serialization operation set and graph-reference retargeting
remain unresolved.

19.29.7.H. Historical Hosted Join/Activate/Serialize Profile

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.29.7.H.


19.29.8. Compaction and Message Destruction

The physical collector algorithm, region/block layout and Message-destruction
mechanics are open Revision-2 runtime choices. The local collection schedule is
not open: it runs at the common end-turn boundary fixed by section 19.29.4. A
profile may compact only if it rewrites
every affected Lmx pointer and atomically re-keys the
`Lmx * -> ShortNameId` index. This includes `node`, ordered child storage,
graph-reference payloads, call/result roots, scheduler handles and every other
reference declared by an address-domain scanner. A stable-address non-moving
collector is the simpler conforming first profile.

Objects with a live L2 raw address or foreign view must be pinned, represented
by a stable handle, or relocated only by a profile that can patch every such
reference. Destroying a Message releases/reclaims its one arena after preserving
any explicitly transferred result and completing required resource cleanup;
external/raw resources follow their own contracts. The operation must also
remove or re-key all affected reverse-name entries.

Compaction and destruction cannot manufacture an alternative Structure value
merely to preserve an old generated C layout. Return identity, result roots and
transfer behavior are governed by the still-open generic Lmx call/result and
lifetime ABI, never by descriptor or per-name-struct rules.


19.29.9. Immutable Values and Interning

High-level payloads may be immutable by explicit operation/domain policy. A
Structure leaf may point into an immutable address domain instead of owning
separately mutable storage.

An implementation may maintain an immutable value store or intern table:

    hash(value) -> canonical immutable object

Equal immutable values may share storage. Since no alias can mutate the shared
object, such sharing does not create mutable-aliasing behavior.

Values which may be suitable for immutable storage include:

    atoms;
    symbols;
    text and strings;
    numeric constants;
    normalized decimal constants;
    small immutable arrays;
    explicit description Structures;
    normalized Table rows;
    expression and parser trees;
    explicit codec description Structures.

Interning is an optimization and representation strategy, not a source-level
identity collapse for all Structures or Arrays. The selected address-domain
operation must define the relevant immutability and equality contract. Mutable
ordinary aggregates retain identity even when their current contents compare
equal. Merely having equal children or the same ShortNameId is never permission
to merge two Lmx node identities.

The high-level mutation rule for an immutable value is that an update produces
a new value or new branch; it does not mutate canonical storage through an
alias. A copy operation may therefore return shared immutable storage when its
contract defines value independence rather than distinct object identity.

Immutable sharing across Message Threads is permitted only when publication,
lifetime and thread safety are guaranteed. Intern tables reside in storage
selected by the explicit runtime/address-domain profile and must be rooted for
their declared lifetime. The chosen profile must state which roots keep the
canonical Lmx nodes or payload cells alive.

Interning does not decide the still-open C result ABI. A result operation that
already selects an interned Lmx node must preserve its canonical identity unless
the explicit operation requests a copy; it must not silently run a second
intern lookup merely because control is leaving a method.


19.29.10. L2 Low-Level Interaction and the L3 Boundary

L2 may expose explicit raw buffers and allocations, typed addresses,
unchecked indexing, raw foreign calls, raw allocation, explicit release,
atomics, locks and other machine operations when the selected L2 profile
defines them.

An L2 thread/shared-memory profile is deliberately a custom low-level contract,
not a general concurrency solution. It must define its own memory ordering,
aliasing, synchronization, cancellation and lifetime rules. Native mutexes,
prefix locks and independently running callbacks from such a profile cannot be
promoted into common L3 or Mix semantics; portable/common concurrency is the
one-arena/FIFO Message model.

A source-visible typed address, foreign handle or raw-allocation handle is an
Lmx leaf whose `data` belongs to an explicit machine/foreign-handle domain. The
raw storage or external allocation denoted by that payload is not itself an Lmx
containment subtree. Such a handle may need explicit release or generated
cleanup; its lifetime and aliasing follow the low-level receiver/ABI contract.
A raw buffer is not automatically an ordinary Array: it needs the typed
service-pool descriptor and lifetime contract of 6.5.

When a low-level resource is represented inside a high-level Structure, the
L2 handle contract must say at least whether it is:

    released with this Message;
    not released by this Message (pin / foreign);
    immutable/shared;
    protected by synchronization.

That is an L2 resource contract, not an L3 owner-edge kind or implicit field in
Lmx. A handle released with this Message is released when the selected Message
lifetime ends. A foreign handle is not.

L3 excludes source operations which expose raw machine storage or addresses.
It may use a checked Lmx wrapper, an explicit handle-domain leaf, a message or a
receiver which hides the raw representation and
provides automatic bounds/lifetime validation. This is a level-surface
distinction, not a claim that all L3 code is safe, pure, authorized or free of
effects.

For example, a checked high-level array operation may be available in both L2
and L3, while unchecked address arithmetic over its backing buffer is L2-only.
A Message Thread or service operation may be available at both levels, while a
raw OS-thread handle, atomic operation or allocator call is L2-only.

Raw low-level references must still obey their declared L2 contract and must
not cross Message boundaries contrary to sections 19.28.R2.5 and 19.29.7. Dropping
to L2 does not silently weaken Message isolation; it gives explicit machine
mechanisms whose use must be described by the L2 profile.


19.29.11. No L3 Arena Argument

The memory model defines no ordinary `stack:`, `heap:` or `static:` allocation
receiver, and no Registry/Table selects object lifetime.

Ordinary Structure constructors materialize Lmx nodes in the current Message's
one arena. Module/top-level Structures and persistent source-local/own-field
graph occurrences belong to the relevant Message arena; non-interned primitive
cells, merge results and ordinary Array headers/backing belong to the arena of
the Message executing their construction. Declared/dynamic C activation values
and used-own-field C snapshots are not separate arena allocations.
Array representation is fixed in 6.5; its machine encoding and physical pool/
arena allocator remain ABI items. L3 has no constructor argument that selects
an arena, hidden descriptor, named C layout or owner object. Runtime helpers
derive the arena from the supplied containing Lmx pointer/root or allocation
range, so generated source methods receive no hidden arena parameter.

L3 receivers:

    own: / borrow: / move:     not L3; do not register;
    copy:                      optional ordinary deep copy into storage selected
                               for the current Message graph (`copy: expr`).

`libown` helpers are not those receivers. The absence of a lifetime annotation
does not authorize a hidden descriptor conversion or per-name aggregate copy.
Exact method result/failure ABI remains open (section 19.29.5). Cross-Message
movement is an explicit Message operation, not an implicit consequence of
`return`.


19.29.12. ANSI C Lowering Strategy

Revision-2 C lowering has the following mandatory shape:

    typedef struct Lmx Lmx;

    struct Lmx {
        Lmx *node;
        size_t len;
        void *data;
    };

Structures, field/method occurrences and persistent own fields use the common
Lmx graph representation (2). C activations hold typed arguments, copied
references and dirty-tracked used-own-field locals under section 21.

Recursively discover every `fn:`/`sub:` body in P0, including definitions inside
named/anonymous Structures and executable bodies. Emit real program-wide
addressable C methods with collision-free identities. Deterministic source-path
names, separators encoded as underscores and collision suffixes are the preferred bootstrap
spelling; linkage/mangling may vary while callable-domain reachability remains.
