Conceptual generated method boundary:

    /* exact C return/failure ABI is still open */
    lm_method_result
    lm_method_<source_path>(
        Lmx *node,
        declared_arguments...,
        dynamic_values_in_sig_order...)
    {
        /* load only used own node fields into C working locals */
        /* imported declared/dynamic inputs have no automatic write-back */
        /* at every 7.4/21.6 semantic call/exposure boundary and exit:
           publish dirty own locals, then clear dirty */
        /* after a call: do not reload the suspended caller */
    }

A callable field is itself an Lmx occurrence. Invocation classifies its `data`
as callable payload, obtains its immutable `{addr,sig}` record from
`all_methods_array`, validates exact signature equality for the Consumer call
site, converts `addr` to the typed pointer selected by `sig`, and calls

    typed_entry(callable->node,
                declared_actuals...,
                dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The registered method domain and permitted function-pointer representations
follow 21.8, including its exact signature and evaluation/checkpoint order.

For every executable construction site, generated C evaluates the actual site
when control reaches it and calls generic Lmx builder operations. A `merge:`
site evaluates its live operands left to right, allocates a fresh result root,
copies each operand root's direct children and containment subtrees under that
result, appends body children, rewrites all copied `node` pointers and registers
ShortNameId for copied occurrences. It reuses immutable primitive/callable
payload references according to their address-domain policy. It never invokes
the translator/compiler at runtime and never pre-flattens another Structure
into a per-name C layout.

Imports make code and explicit providers linkable; they do not execute
construction/merge, instantiate all declarations or scan unrelated directories.
Cross-unit Structures are resolved Lmx links, explicit-root lookups or providers
returning such links (21.12). C symbols provide code reachability, not bindings.

Explicit machine-layout constructs and foreign adapters may use concrete C
layouts; wrappers enter source methods through the typed boundary of 21.8.
Physical allocator/collector, branch encoding, result/failure and module-loading
choices must preserve section 2's representation and this Message memory model.

19.29.12.H. Revision-1 Hosted C Lowering (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.29.12.H.


19.29.13. Shared Low-Level Resources and Large Read-Only Data

The Message Thread memory model is message- and branch-oriented. It is the
ordinary model for active runtime state, handler data, high-level events,
configuration, explicit Registry queries and service messages. It should not pretend
that a very large shared raw buffer is an ordinary copied message payload.

If a program needs a large resource that many Message Threads read directly,
such as:

    a numeric table;
    a shared search index;
    a memory-mapped dataset;
    a model-weight array;
    an external database page cache;
    a read-only media buffer;

the raw resource belongs to an explicit low-level L2 or external-provider
profile.

No generic source storage receiver is defined here. An explicit L2 provider
binding or typed primitive handle may expose such a resource. The resource is not an
ordinary mutable Message Thread branch: it is explicitly immutable/shared or
externally managed, with a declared lifetime and publication contract.

An application may pass a Table of provider configuration such as:

    storage.kind[BigArray] = external
    storage.element[BigArray] = f64
    storage.access[BigArray] = shared_readonly
    storage.lifetime[BigArray] = provider
    storage.mapping[BigArray] = memory_mapped
    ownership.edge[BigArray][data] = borrowed
    operation.method[BigArray][read] = BigArrayRead

These rows do not register or activate a provider by existing. Provider and
access operations must be explicit callable Lmx links, explicit root-relative
lookups or linked provider entry points. A checked Structure wrapper, when used,
is an ordinary Lmx graph; only the operation that exposes raw storage or
unchecked machine access is L2-only.

Shared immutable data may be read from several Message Threads when the active
provider guarantees:

    immutable contents after publication;
    stable lifetime while handles exist;
    thread-safe publication;
    suitable alignment and representation for the selected backend;
    checked bounds in high-level L3-facing accessors.

Because no thread can mutate the shared object through an alias, this does not
violate the no-shared-mutable-reference rule.

Shared mutable data is different. If multiple host or low-level threads must
write or coordinate over one resource, it must use an explicit L2 profile with
defined:

    atomics;
    locks or read/write locks;
    barriers;
    memory ordering;
    ownership publication;
    cancellation behavior;
    external-resource lifetime;
    failure and recovery.

Such a raw resource remains L2 even if a checked higher-level L3 receiver wraps
it. L3's inability to access the raw buffer directly is not a general claim
that the wrapped service has no effects or concurrency.

The default distinction is:

    Message Thread message boundary
        copy, serialization or whole-branch ownership transfer;

    shared read
        immutable/read-only or externally published resource with typed
        handle;

    explicit shared mutation
        L2 raw/shared buffer plus synchronization and lifetime contract;

    L3-facing use
        checked Structure/handle/message receiver which hides the machine
        operations.

Language-visible storage, ownership, synchronization and lifetime rules are
implemented by explicitly reached L2/L3 receiver/operation nodes. Explicit
Tables may configure those operations; L1 remains the external substrate used
to lower them to C99 and platform APIs. No Table or provider creates a runtime
namespace.

Neither shared immutable storage nor an external provider changes ordinary
aggregate semantics. A source-visible handle is an Lmx leaf in an explicit
machine-handle domain or an Lmx wrapper Structure. A
provider-specific retain operation, if required, must be explicit in the
handle/operation contract and must occur before return publication; it is not a
hidden copy performed by `return`.


19.29.14. Control Exit, Diagnostics and Message-Thread-Local Collection

Revision 2 has not selected the final call/result and exception encoding, nor
the physical block allocator or tracing/compaction algorithm. It has selected
the ownership and scheduling boundary: exactly one logical arena belongs to
each Message, and one local collection pass runs at the common end-turn point
after FIFO outbox publication or failed-turn rollback. Persistent handler state,
results, failure data, own fields and explicit histories remain Lmx nodes/links.
A source method receives `callable->node` plus the declared/dynamic values in
its exact `sig` and uses dirty-tracked C working locals between checkpoints.
Live graph traversal follows address-domain policies; no cleanup path may depend
on a reified runtime Namespace, attached descriptor, closure environment or
hidden infrastructure argument.

The method-exit boundary and the end-turn boundary are distinct:

1. A method evaluates and retains its result or explicit throw arguments,
   checkpoints dirty own fields, runs applicable `finally` cleanup, checkpoints
   cleanup writes, then returns/unwinds (8.8 and 19.11.2). Each nested call
   follows the ordinary checkpoint rule. The caller is not reloaded.
2. A caught throw continues through the explicit catch argument contract; it
   does not by itself fail the Message turn. Handler return transfers control
   to the runtime wrapper and is not itself collection.
3. Once the turn outcome is established, the wrapper publishes the successful
   FIFO outbox or discards the failed turn's staged outbox. It releases input/
   transient turn roots while retaining surviving result/continuation roots,
   then performs the one local collection pass (19.29.4.1).

Outbox rollback does not roll back previously published own-field or explicit
graph mutations. No implicit graph transaction is introduced. The still-open C
result/exception carrier cannot reorder these semantic boundaries.

19.29.14.H. Historical Hosted Control and Ownership Vocabulary

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.29.14.H.


19.29.15. Future L2 Low-Level Threads, Shared Blocks and Explicit Release

This structural memory model does not yet define the complete source API for:

    standalone low-level execution threads;
    raw shared memory blocks;
    direct allocator calls;
    explicit `freemem`-style release;
    low-level cancellation and join;
    complete atomic/memory-order operations.

Portable host-thread, mutex and condition operations now exist as an
implementation/runtime ABI. This subsection remains future work for a generic
source-facing L2 machine-thread and shared-memory API. The host ABI is not
currently a `machine.thread:` or another public source receiver.

These are future L2 operations because their source semantics expose machine
storage, addresses, synchronization or allocator lifetime. L1 remains the
external implementation/lowering substrate and is not a Lingvamyxa source
level. L3 may use checked wrappers but does not directly expose these machine
operations.

The current hosted Message implementation already realizes a canonical
actor-local ownership domain and the non-overlapping-turn boundary. Its
additional sibling `LmOwnArena` shells are migration-era physical allocation
regions and must converge on, or be observed as, blocks/ranges of one logical
Message arena. The physical end-turn collector and the
join/activate/serialize algorithms remain implementation/current-profile
details. Revision 2 requires that every source-visible value remain an Lmx
node/link in its Message arena, that cross-Message transfer be explicit, and
that no raw mutable pointer silently cross into storage owned by another
concurrently executing Message. The common collection point is fixed at
end-turn; result-transfer and delivery algorithms remain open inside that
boundary.

Mix must be rewritten into cooperating Messages for independent execution and
scaling. Its current Java cursor/thread architecture is transitional; neither
its locks/callbacks nor a single global document actor define the target.

A future L2 profile may define operations for:

    creating a standalone low-level execution thread;
    joining, cancelling or terminating that thread;
    allocating a raw memory block;
    allocating a shared memory block;
    mapping a file or external buffer;
    publishing shared storage;
    synchronizing with atomics, locks and barriers;
    defining memory-order rules;
    releasing raw/shared storage through a matching explicit operation;
    wrapping the resource in a checked Lmx handle node or subtree.

Conceptual future L2 sketch:

    machine.thread: workerThread workerEntry workerArgs
    mem: sharedBlock bytes shared(mutable)
    freemem: sharedBlock

This sketch is not normative current syntax. The corresponding operators are
NOT IMPLEMENTED unless an active L2 profile explicitly supplies their callable
occurrences/providers.

A low-level execution thread is not automatically a Message Thread. It may
expose raw addresses, shared mutable buffers and platform synchronization under
its explicit L2 profile. A running Message is the higher structural runtime
graph with explicit roots, exactly one arena, a FIFO inbox/outbox and one
non-overlapping execution lane. Only its mapping to host workers, fairness,
capacity/backpressure and physical arena/collector machinery remain profile
choices.

L3 code may see:

    a checked typed handle;
    an explicit description Structure;
    a capability;
    a service/message adapter;
    an automatically bounds-checked collection;

rather than the raw address, allocator or synchronization operation.

An explicit low-level `freemem` operation, when implemented, is not:

    a local Message Thread collection pass;
    an end-turn collection pass;
    an ordinary Structure/Array destructor;
    a message-boundary rule;
    a high-level `return` operation.

It is a low-level release primitive for memory allocated by the matching L2
allocation profile. Its validity requires a contract which prevents double
release, mismatched allocators, use after release and release while another raw
user remains active.

Future profile work must define at least:

    exact L2 source spellings;
    allocation ownership and matching release rules;
    shared-block publication rules;
    memory ordering and synchronization;
    thread start/join/cancel semantics;
    interaction with Message Thread wrapper handles;
    interaction with service and device adapters;
    diagnostics for double release and use after release;
    cross-level escape diagnostics;
    which operations are always L2-only;
    external-provider failure and cleanup behavior.

Until such a profile is specified and implemented, raw shared blocks and
standalone low-level threads remain L2/L1 implementation mechanisms, while
ordinary L2/L3 Message code communicates through Messages and Lmx nodes owned
by their one Message arena or shared only through explicitly immutable/foreign
domains under the selected explicit runtime policy.


19.29.16. Contract and Implementation Status

The memory contract is specified in 19.29.1–19.29.15, the Array contract in 6.5,
and activation/checkpoint semantics in 21. The implementation checklist and
remaining physical ABI decisions are in `struct_refactoring_version_2.txt`.
Hosted support inventories are historical evidence, not conformance criteria.

19.29.16.H. Historical Implementation Inventory

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 19.29.16.H.


19.30. Explicit Reactive Registry and Query Receivers

19.30.1. Reactive Layer

An explicitly held Registry value may be updated by an operation that also
produces events. Events are messages. Query receivers run inside Message Threads
and receive the Registry/Table to query as an explicit operand.

19.30.2. Agent Candidates

An agent receiver may propose candidate rows or messages. A validator must
accept, reject or rank the candidate before an explicit operation updates the
    target Registry/Table. No runtime namespace exists or is created by this
    operation.

19.30.3. Boundaries and Bootstrap

Reactive Registry processing obeys Message Thread memory/message boundaries. A
minimal bootstrap may implement only:

    row inserted;
    query receiver;
    candidate row;
    validation;
    accepted row.


19.31. HTTP, Service and Transport Operators

19.31.1. Status

HTTP Lingvamyxa REST is a built-in service transport profile. It is not the
core execution model.

19.31.2. Service Instance

A service instance is a bundle of Message Threads connected by standard messages.
The bundle is not a shared mutable heap.

19.31.3. HTTP to Message

HTTP request handling is:

    HTTP request
        -> route table lookup
        -> IncomingMessage
        -> receiver validation and explicit policy combination
        -> executor
        -> result/failure message
        -> HTTP response mapping

The route map is an ordinary explicitly configured value, commonly a Table.
External HTTP data becomes an LMX message before service dispatch.

The standard request target is the normalized `MessageAddress.endpoint +
MessageAddress.route`. A remote sender posts the complete LMX Message document
as the request body. The receiving application resolves only the route part in
its local route table. An HTTP route never names an OS worker or exposes a raw
MessageThread handle.

The core runtime does not own an HTTP client singleton. Its per-runtime
REST/LMX provider receives the already normalized request URI and an exact
borrowed byte span, performs one POST with media type `application/lmx`, and
returns either an HTTP admission status or a native transport failure. This
keeps the message address, outbox and shutdown semantics identical for a test
provider, libcurl, WinHTTP or a later transport implementation.

19.31.4. Serialization, Client/Server and Service Boundaries

Serialization adapters are callable Lmx occurrences reached through an explicit
link or structural path and may be configured by explicitly supplied Tables.
HTTP client/server adapters are services or hosted native adapters that inject
messages into Message Threads. No codec is selected from an ambient namespace.
Service boundaries obey the same memory and authority rules as Message Thread
boundaries, with stronger default isolation for remote input.

The initial REST/LMX media type is `application/lmx`. HTTP status and response
mapping describe transport admission and, in a request/response profile, may
carry the resulting LMX reply. An asynchronous profile may acknowledge inbox
admission first and later deliver the semantic reply to `replyTo`; in both cases
the semantic result/failure is an ordinary LMX Message with `correlation` equal
to the request `id`.


19.32. Authentication and Authorization Operators

19.32.1. Status

Authentication and authorization are not a separate role system. They are
explicit verifier/policy values, validators and evidence attached to a message
flow. Tables are one optional representation.

19.32.2. Primary Credential and Verifier

A verifier is a protected Structure/value that can validate a primary
credential without storing the credential itself.

The default password verifier may use libsodium password hashing in the C99
runtime profile.

19.32.3. Default Installation Credential

A fresh installation may have a system user name and an empty primary password.
This is not global security policy. It is explicit initial verifier state. A
user or administrator may replace it with a stronger verifier.

19.32.4. Verifier Realms and Protected Objects

Protected objects may have their own verifier realm:

    protected module;
    protected service;
    protected archive;
    protected directory;
    protected administration operation.

Password reset may reset an access verifier to an empty/default state. It does
not recover separately encrypted content whose key depended on the old verifier.

A protected object may require:

    one verifier;
    multiple verifiers for one user;
    a quorum of users/sessions;
    creator-parent tracking.

Parent/creator rows do not grant content rights by themselves.

19.32.5. Delegated Ticket

After primary credential validation, the runtime may issue a delegated ticket
for a module or service. A profile may represent ticket/evidence data in a Table:

    ticket[user][module] = ticket_id
    ticketAuthority[ticket_id][operation] = allowed
    ticketExpiry[ticket_id] = time

Changing or resetting the primary verifier invalidates dependent tickets by its
explicit policy Structure.

19.32.6. External Authentication Modules

OAuth or OS-login modules are ordinary L2/L3 modules. How they obtain authority
is defined by the selected authentication/authorization profile, not by their
source level. They do not replace the explicitly configured verifier. They may request or
receive delegated tickets after the primary policy allows it.

19.32.7. Authentication Evidence Rows

Authentication validation produces an explicit `AuthEvidence` Lmx node or
subtree. Its fields/rows may include:

    authUser[flow] = user
    authVerifier[flow] = verifierRealm
    authTicket[flow] = ticketId
    authority[flow][operation] = allowed
    guarantee[flow] = sessionQuorumSatisfied

Message validators receive that evidence reference explicitly and may require
these fields/rows before producing a ConcreteExpression or ExecutionPlan. No
ambient evidence table is implied.

19.32.8. ANSI C / libsodium Runtime Sketch

A C99 runtime profile may use libsodium for:

    password hashing/verifier creation;
    random ticket material;
    constant-time comparisons;
    secure memory helpers where the runtime owns the memory region.

UI password entry is outside libsodium's control unless the UI layer is also
implemented with memory handling that the runtime owns.

19.32.9. Standard Statement

Authentication is a validation/evidence layer over the message pipeline.

    credential/verifier -> validator -> authority evidence -> message validation

Authorization is an explicit operation over that evidence and policy. The
operation may query a supplied Table; it is not a global role hierarchy.


19.33. Operator and Runtime Map

| Contract | Section |
| --- | --- |
| Control, exception payloads, retry and yield | 19.1–19.16 |
| Merge, explicit registries, validation and providers | 2.3, 19.17–19.27 |
| Message construction, delivery and FIFO turns | 19.28.R2 |
| Message arena, roots, end-turn outbox and collection | 19.29 |
| Mix document addressing / cooperating-Message runtime | 19.28.10 / 19.29.2.1 |
| Reactive queries, HTTP, authentication | 19.30–19.32 |
| L2-only machine forms | 20 |
| Dynamic visibility and exact typed invocation | 21 |

A backend implementation or optional receiver profile does not extend the core
ontology. Its operations consume explicit values under the relevant contract.

20. L2 Primitive, Address and Parser Operators

20.0. Scope of This Section

This section defines the minimal L2 operator/parser-form set.

L2 has the common Structure/primitive/Array and address-domain rules plus
explicit low-level machine payload domains. Explicit machine storage/allocation forms produce Lmx
leaves whose `data` points into a typed-address or foreign-handle domain.
Receiver operations and parser profiles assign final semantics to Structures
built from source syntax.

The minimal L2 profile therefore has no ordinary Structure syntax for C-style
direct aggregate value access. Source primitive/scalar values are still Lmx
leaves; after address-domain classification a backend may operate directly on
their payload only as a semantics-preserving implementation step. Explicit
machine aggregate storage is reached by an explicit handle-domain Lmx leaf,
while an ordinary Structure expression yields an Lmx reference and an Array
expression yields the descriptor reference of 6.5. This keeps
`u32: x` simple and prevents an embedded record from
silently becoming a copyable whole-Structure expression.

This section specifies the L2-specific low-level machine operator/parser-form
surface. Its machine forms are not part of L3; ordinary shared forms mentioned
for contrast retain their separately specified L2/L3 availability. In this
sense L3 is smaller than L2: L3 keeps the
high-level algorithmic/control, data/query and runtime surface and excludes raw
storage, address, physical layout-storage and unchecked access. A parser form
from this section is L2-only only when its source semantics expose such machine
state; parser involvement by itself is not a level boundary.

20.1. Pointer and Field Syntax Exclusions

L2 does not use `*`, `&`, `^`, `.`, or `->` as pointer/field syntax.

This rule should be stated once as an L2 invariant instead of repeated at every address example.

Consequences:

    `*`  remains available as a multiplication operator token.
    `&`  is not address-of.
    `^`  is not pointer dereference and is not a field-follow marker.
    `.`  is source indentation only when it appears in the leading dot prefix; it is not field access.
    `->` is not a Lingvamyxa field-follow operator.

L2 address and field forms are:

    @ⁿ: T x     reserved address-depth declaration head, for example `@@@: int ppp`
    @x          address of storage location `x`
    \x          raw load from address expression `x`
    x\field     typed field-follow through address/reference `x`
    x\[n]field  zero-based exact-name occurrence field-follow through `x`
    x[i]        indexed access through address/reference/container `x`

Field-follow and index chains bind tighter than prefix `@` and prefix `\`.

The C-profile mnemonic table in section 11.3 shows how the L2 forms cover the C declarator/expression roles of `*`, `&` and `->` without using those tokens as Lingvamyxa pointer/field syntax.

20.2. Primitive, Array, Address and Indexed Forms

The ordinary `[]:` family constructs an array value. It is not an ANSI C array
declaration and does not select stack, heap or static storage. Its surface forms
and rectangular/index semantics below remain language requirements. The
service-pool descriptor and backing model are fixed in 6.5.

    Primitive: name [initialValue]
    []: ElementType name size [initialValue...]
    []...[]: ElementType name size1 ... sizeN [initialValue...]
    [size1]...[sizeN]: ElementType: name [initialValue...]
    @ⁿ: BaseType name

Examples:

    int: count 5
    []: int leafs count
    @: int p
    @@: int pp
    @@@: int ppp

The `leafs` constructor materializes a typed Array descriptor and its backing,
initializing the requested elements according to the selected Array domain. Repeated bracket
groups specify rank and dimensions. Both empty and payload-bearing bracket
groups are accepted as described by the parser profile; missing dimensions are
a diagnostic. An Lmx field stores the descriptor reference; the descriptor itself has no
`node`. Indexing follows 6.5, reference copying 21.4, and tracing 19.29.4.

The leading `@` fields before an Array element descriptor specify an admitted
low-level address-depth element form. `@ⁿ:` itself produces the explicit L2
typed-address primitive described by the selected low-level profile; it is not
an ArrayReference.

Indexing an ordinary array uses Array semantics and explicit runtime bounds
metadata. Indexing a
raw typed address is a separate L2 low-level operation. If a backend needs raw
fixed C-array storage, it must expose a distinct explicit L2 machine-storage
operation and must not assign that meaning to ordinary `[]:`.

The distinct hosted C99 machine-storage receiver is `c.array`. It has exactly
one logical child, in one of these two forms:

    c.array: ArrayDeclaratorFrame
    c.array: const: ArrayDeclaratorFrame

`ArrayDeclaratorFrame` must have a head made only of one or more bracket groups.
The optional `const` Frame must itself contain exactly that one bracket Frame.
No other child, sibling declaration or qualifier is admitted by the minimal
receiver. Examples:

    c.array: [3]: int: top_values 2 4 6
    c.array:
        [][]: int matrix 2 2
            []: 1 2
            []: 3 4
    ---
    c.array: const: [2]: int: fixed_values 9 11
    c.array: [rows][columns]: int: matrix

The array rank is the number of bracket groups and must be at least one. A
non-empty group such as `[rows]` supplies its own extent expression. Each empty
group consumes exactly one following declarator field after the element type
and binding name, in bracket order. After all empty groups have consumed their
extents, the remaining fields are initializer clauses. A missing extent is a
`c.array` receiver diagnostic; an initializer is never used to infer a missing
extent. An extent supplied after an empty group must be a single atom in the
minimal hosted profile; a compound extent expression is written inside a
non-empty bracket group. Empty and non-empty groups may be mixed.

The element type uses the existing L2 C-declarator type forms, including an
admitted address-depth type for pointer elements. The optional `const` wrapper
has the same C-profile meaning as other declaration composition: it qualifies
the base element type. It does not turn the declaration into an ordinary
immutable ArrayReference.

`c.array` declares target-machine C array storage. It allocates no Array
object/header, arena backing, owner edge, length/shape/stride record or bounds
metadata. The storage is not a Lingvamyxa Value and the declared name is a
machine-storage designator, not an ArrayReference. Where the hosted C expression
profile admits array-to-pointer conversion, consuming the designator produces
the corresponding typed-address Primitive. Its address depth is determined by
the element's admitted address depth plus the one remaining array dimension:
indexing may expose a rank-one subarray for decay, while indexing every array
dimension exposes the element itself. A designator with more than one remaining
dimension is not silently flattened to an unrelated pointer type. Conversion to
or from an ordinary ArrayReference requires an explicit operation which states
the raw backing address, shape and lifetime contract.

At block scope the declaration has C automatic storage duration. Runtime extent
expressions select the admitted C99 variable-length-array rules; a VLA cannot
have an initializer. At module/top level the hosted lowering emits internal-linkage
`static` storage, requires constant extents and applies the C99 requirements for
static initializers. With no initializer, automatic elements are indeterminate
and static elements are zero-initialized. A present initializer follows C99
aggregate initialization. In the conservative hosted bootstrap, a
multidimensional initializer must spell each nested dimension with an explicit
`[]:` or Structure brace group; flat brace-elided scalar clauses are rejected.
Omitted elements are zero-initialized and excess or incompatible initializers
are diagnostics. The conservative hosted bootstrap additionally
requires a top-level address initializer to name storage whose declaration has
already been emitted; a source-forward machine-storage initializer is rejected
rather than reordered behind its dependency.

A block-scope `c.array` has the ordinary lifetime of its C automatic storage.
Storing its address in an Lmx graph does not extend that raw C lifetime. Until
an explicit adapter or raw-lifetime operation exists, the hosted backend
rejects escape of this raw storage instead of emitting a dangling pointer.
Ordinary anonymous Structures and ordinary Array descriptors/backing follow
the owning Message's lifetime policy. A containing Lmx field is distinct from
its Array referent; neither lifetime is inferred from `c.array` automatic
storage.

Postfix indexing dispatches from the binding category. For `c.array`, `a[i]` is
unchecked raw C-array indexing and does not use the checked ArrayReference
operation. One bracket suffix consumes one dimension, so multidimensional raw
storage uses `a[i][j]`, not an ArrayReference multi-coordinate shorthand. A
partial index denotes the remaining raw subarray; a full-rank index denotes an
element lvalue and may be read, addressed or used as an update head. No view is
created and no bounds check is synthesized. Statically known dangerous accesses
remain subject to the selected C99 undefined-behavior policy.

Taking the address of a whole or partially indexed C array produces a C
pointer-to-array type rather than another ordinary typed-address depth. The
minimal hosted profile does not yet expose pointer-to-array declarators as a
Lingvamyxa type and rejects that expression instead of misclassifying it as
`T **`; address-of remains admitted after all array dimensions have been
indexed to an element.

The `const` wrapper qualifies the base element, following the declarator rule
above. For an array of pointers, the pointer slot itself remains assignable while
an update that traverses the declared pointer depth and reaches the const base
element is rejected.

The hosted bootstrap provides this exact `c.array` receiver. Intentional raw
arrays in production bootstrap code and low-level fixtures use `c.array`,
including buffers that require C array-to-pointer decay, `sizeof(array)` or a
raw-pointer ABI. `c.array` remains C storage and is not affected by ordinary
Array semantics.

Names that contain spaces use exact quoted identifiers as ordinary fields:

@: int `second slot`

This declares an address-depth-1 primitive slot with base type `int` and
introduces the exact quoted name `second slot`.

The same short-tail whitespace rule applies to address-depth declarations:

@@@: int ppp

This declares `ppp` as an address-depth-3 slot with base type `int`. The spelling `@@@: int, ppp` is the same declaration with an explicit comma field separator. The spelling `@@@: int; ppp` is different: the semicolon terminates the `@@@:` short form after `int`, so `ppp` is no longer the binding-name field of that declaration. The following `ppp` is parsed as the next source item at the current level.

20.2.H. Historical Hosted `LmSlice` Array Projection

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 20.2.H.

20.2.1. Revision-2 C Lowering of Structures and Methods

Ordinary Lingvamyxa Structures never acquire a source-name-specific ANSI C
layout. Every runtime occurrence uses the exact common prefix/header:

    typedef struct Lmx Lmx;

    struct Lmx {
        Lmx *node;
        size_t len;
        void *data;
    };

The translator must not emit `typedef struct SourceName`, calculate
`offsetof(SourceName, field)`, represent a method as a C member, or use a
descriptor pointer to select a source layout. `data` is classified by registered
address domains; generic operations enumerate branch children, access primitive
pools, resolve callable payloads and handle explicit low-level storage.

Generated source methods are ordinary C functions emitted at translation-unit
level even when the source definition is nested. Every entry has a deterministic
program-wide unique identity; a long C name derived from the normalized source
path with path separators replaced by underscores is preferred. The build/link
profile must make every such entry addressable by any callable Lmx occurrence
in the linked/loaded program and prevent required entries from dead stripping.

The generated-method input contract is fixed by the selected callable record:

    typed_method(callable->node,
                 declared_actuals...,
                 dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The first parameter is the Structure containing the selected callable occurrence
and is the reserved source `node`. `all_methods_array.sig` supplies the declared
arguments, ordered hidden dynamic ShortNameIds/pass modes and exact physical C
pointer type. Primitive values are passed by semantic value; Structure/Array
references are copied reference values. Used own node fields are dirty-tracked C
working locals. The C return/error convention is not yet fixed. Hidden
infrastructure `LmMessageThread *`, arena, descriptor, reified Namespace or
closure-environment parameters are non-conforming. Infrastructure wrappers may
receive platform contexts and then form the typed source call above.

Translation of a source Structure emits runtime construction/evaluation code,
not an already instantiated C aggregate. Translation of `merge` emits a generic
runtime merge call at the original execution site. A module may compile/link an
explicit dependency, but it does not inspect a whole directory or flatten the
dependency's Structure shape into the current C translation unit.

Ordinary field traversal lowers to generic Lmx operations:

    direct pointer                 -> use directly
    relative path                  -> start at supplied Structure
    node\field                     -> first child lookup on the already supplied
                                      `callable->node`; no extra parent hop
    arbitrary textual absolute path -> require explicit root and traverse

Short-name comparison uses the sole `Lmx * -> ShortNameId` reverse index.
Full paths are reconstructed, not stored. A later optimizer may replace these
operations only after proving equivalent behaviour across mutation, runtime
merge, repeated occurrences, recursion and concurrency.

Raw L2 C constructs remain distinct. An explicit `c.struct`, `c.union`, foreign
function declaration or ABI record may and usually must emit the requested C
type/layout. Such a raw substrate artifact is not an ordinary Lingvamyxa
Structure and must not leak its representation into the universal Lmx model.

20.2.1.H. Revision-1 Namespace Descriptor/C Layout Projection (Historical)

Historical text and examples: `Lingvamyxa_spec_history.txt`, section 20.2.1.H.

20.2.2. L1/L2/L3 Profiles and Explicit C Interoperability

`L1:` selects the direct C99-oriented language/first translator (1.2).
`L2:` and `L3:` select profiles of the second translator. Each receiver
consumes a Structure; it does not reinterpret arbitrary string fields as code.
Unhandled strings at the translated body's top level are a consumer diagnostic.
Nested receivers define their own payload meaning.

`C:` is the isolated last-resort text escape. The old hosted translator's
`L1:` raw-dump behavior is historical, not the new L1 contract.

Include and platform selection

    include: "<stdio.h>" "<windows.h>"

emits:

    #include <stdio.h>
    #include <windows.h>

A decoded include payload already enclosed in `<...>` or `"..."` retains
that delimiter; otherwise it becomes a local quoted target.

    os:
        win:
            include: "<direct.h>" "<windows.h>"
        unix:
            include: "<unistd.h>"
        default:
            C: "/* fallback L1 text */"
    end: os

The last branch only illustrates the escape boundary, not the recommended
way to implement a fallback. Ordinary bootstrap code uses structural L1.
The profile maps branch heads to target conditions such as `_WIN32`,
`__APPLE__` or `__unix__`.

In L2, selected branches also supply the compiled declarations/methods.
Alternative implementations must satisfy the Consumer's contract; selection
does not change runtime occurrence order or the `[0]` default.

    os:
        win:
            fn: platformValue () int
                return: 1
            end: platformValue
        default:
            fn: platformValue () int
                return: 2
            end: platformValue
    end: os

    external:
        fn: main () int
            return: platformValue() = 0
        end: main
    end: external

The selected L2 callable needs no `c.platformValue()` spelling. A genuinely
foreign C symbol uses the explicit `c.name` adapter (6.6.6).

Raw-C boundary example

The historical raw-dump example is preserved here with the explicit escape
receiver. It is an interoperability illustration, not an ordinary L1 recipe:

    L2:
        C:'''typedef enum LmTransSymbolKind {
            LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_VARIABLE = 1,
            LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_FUNCTION = 2,
            LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_PROCEDURE = 3,
            LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_LABEL = 4,
            LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_STRUCTURE = 5
        } LmTransSymbolKind;'''

        fn: main () int
            return: c.LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_VARIABLE
        end: main
    end: L2

These constants remain C symbols; the block creates no Lmx field or structural
binding.

Explicit configuration

Ordinary keys may be bare; operator/punctuated or exact spellings use backticks:

    u32
    prefix
    sqrt_ansi_c

    `return`
    `+`
    `!=`
    `c.*`
    `operator.infix`
    `translator class-->spelling`

The delimiters are not part of the key. Bare and exact spellings with identical
valid identifier content denote the same name (3.4).

The hosted bootstrap loads selected configuration rows from `lm2/core.lm2`
and `lm2/trans_registry.lm2` into compiler-private tables. Its early
`table:`/`join:` loader and accepted file extensions are implementation
details, not permission to scan arbitrary application Tables or create runtime
namespaces.

In the Table profile, the first column is the row key. A bare `columns:` atom
is a column name; `(l2 binding)` names `l2` and carries metadata `binding`.
Names such as `class` have no privileged structural effect. Rows are dense:
cell count must be divisible by column count, with missing cells explicit.
Alignment and physical source lines do not add fields. The optional `source`
projection and any flat internal `row: table key payload` encoding remain
compiler-profile choices, not another Table ontology.

    join: (BaseTable OverlayTable) TargetTable
        columns: class  (value char)
        rows:
                 local  value
    end: TargetTable

This invokes the explicit Table operation (19.17), not an import directive.

Foreign entry and machine forms

All nested and top-level source methods must remain program-wide reachable
through their method records (21.8). `external` wraps exactly one `fn` or
`sub` and additionally requests an exported foreign C entry:

    L2:
        external:
            fn: main () int
                return: 0
            end: main
        end: external
    end: L2

The entry adapter obeys the source-method boundary. C declarator qualifiers
remain explicit receiver composition; for example `const: @: LmP0Node p`
projects to `const LmP0Node *p` in a machine declaration.

    @count              address of the primitive storage location count
    p: @count           store it in an address-depth-1 slot
    pp: @p              store p's address in an address-depth-2 slot
    \p                  raw load through typed address p
    leafs[3]            checked ordinary Array access
    \(rawBase + 3)      raw load after explicit typed-address arithmetic

The reference, address and bounds distinctions are defined in 11–13 and 20.1–20.2.


20.3. Primitive Expression Operators

The minimal L2 primitive operator profile assigns explicit operator roles:

    grouping        (expr)
    prefix          @expr, \expr, ++expr, --expr, +expr, -expr, !expr, ~expr
    postfix         expr++, expr--
    infix           +, -, *, /, %, =, !=, <, <=, >, >=, &&, ||, &, |, ^

Source style: prefix operator spellings such as `@`, `\`, `++`, `--`, `!` and
`~` should be written adjacent to their operand when the operand is on the same
physical line, for example `@slot`, `\item`, `++index` and `!ready`. This is a
style convention, not a P0 tokenization requirement; P0 still tokenizes
operator spellings as separate operator-token fields where appropriate.

Indexing is a postfix expression operator with the bracket form `target[index]`,
specified with storage and address forms above. For an ArrayReference it is
checked Array indexing; for an explicit typed-address Primitive it is the
separate L2 raw-address operation. The `index` part is consumed as its own
expression range by the active L2 expression profile. Nested index operators,
calls, bounded Structures, strings and ordinary operator tokens inside the
brackets are therefore lowered by the same expression machinery as ordinary
argument expressions. Field-follow remains the L2 `\field` form, not ANSI C
`.` or `->` source syntax.

The bracket in `target\[occurrence]field` is not this postfix operator: its
position immediately after the field-follow backslash makes it the
occurrence qualifier for the following exact field name. Therefore
`target\field[index]` and `target\[index]field` are distinct forms.

This bracket form is separate from the `[]:` receiver spelling. `[]:` is a head
symbol used by Array constructors. `target[...]` is a
postfix index/access suffix. A bare `[a b]` is not a portable L2 grouping form;
use `(a b)` for a bounded Structure unless a profile explicitly admits standalone
square-bracket values.

The token `=` is comparison/equality for expression/operator consumers. It is not native assignment.

The C-profile lowering maps the listed primitive operators to ANSI C operator
spellings, except that Lingvamyxa `=` lowers to ANSI C `==`, `@expr` lowers to
address-of and `\expr` lowers to raw load/dereference when it is not a
field-follow token. Shifts, overflow rules, integer division rules,
floating-point rules and conversion rules remain L2 profile details unless a
later specification selects a mandatory set.

20.4. Update and Callable Consumption

L2 uses the same head/update surface form as the rest of the structural core:

    target: value

Examples:

    count: 6
    tree\branch: branch
    leafs[3]: 10

After resolution, the target is an actual Lmx occurrence, an explicitly selected
child position, or an Array/index location in the selected Array profile. The
operation immediately updates or appends addressed nodes in that graph only
when an explicit path or structural receiver selects graph mutation; it does
not append a binding to a runtime Namespace. Deferred publication of a dirty
own backing occurrence is the separate checkpoint rule below. A plain
executable short-name update first selects the
current method's own declaration (a graph-backed body local or activation-only
formal/result), otherwise its inherited dynamic value, otherwise a local copy
initialized from immediate `node\[0]name` fallback. Assignment to a graph-backed
own slot marks dirty; formal/result/dynamic/fallback assignment stays private.
If an explicit structural receiver appends a same-name child, structural lookup
still selects `[0]`, the first matching child.
An addressable field/index form updates the selected graph/storage location. A
function/subroutine/receiver form resolves a callable Lmx node, reads its exact
`{addr,sig}` method record, forms declared/dynamic arguments from the current C
activation and invokes the typed entry beginning with `callable->node`.

Any `implements(new old Consumer)` validation is an explicit compile-time or
runtime graph operation over supplied Lmx values. It is not a descriptor gate
that creates visibility. The reverse name index is updated for every created,
moved or removed occurrence. A backend may reuse physical storage only when it
proves that node identity, graph references, occurrence order and arbitrary
textual lookup in a mutated tree remain observationally identical.

The update operator tokens `++` and `--` are part of the minimal L2 C-profile as
both prefix and postfix expression operators. They are expression operators, not
a second assignment syntax. Other profiles may lower them differently, but they
still compose with the same operator-token consumption model.

20.5. Minimal L2 Control Receivers

The minimal L2 executable-body control set is:

    if / else
    while
    synchronized
    until
    for(init, condition, step)
    return
    break
    continue

These are source-level control receivers. They are not raw jumps in the source
semantics, even if a translator lowers them to labels and branches. In
particular, `else` is resolved to an explicit receiver/callable occurrence by
the compiler and runtime Structure rules; it is not parser syntax and does not
require a runtime namespace.

The first ANSI C bootstrap translator needs only:

    if / else
    while
    synchronized
    return
    break
    continue

`until` and `for` remain part of the minimal L2 semantic set, but may be added
after the first procedural bootstrap subset.

20.5.1. `if` and `else`

L2 `if` consumes:

    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `if` receiver

L2 `else` is a separate statement receiver. It consumes:

    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `else` receiver

`else` is valid as the false branch only when it immediately follows an `if`
statement at the same statement level.

Example:

if: count > 0
    print: count


With a following sibling `else`:

if: count > 0
    print: count

else:
    print: 0


20.5.2. `while`

L2 `while` is a pre-condition loop.

Semantic lowering shape:

    while(condition, statementStructure)

Example:

while: i < n
    print: i
    i: (i + 1)


20.5.3. `until`

L2 `until` is a conditional close for the current executable block.

Example:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

In this example, `record` is an explicit compact-call argument of `endOfInput`. The `until:` line consumes the whole tail `endOfInput(record)` as a boolean-producing condition Structure. The condition producer is the compact call `endOfInput(record)`, not the invalid space-separated spelling `endOfInput record`.

More explicit condition form:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

Conceptual lowering shape:

    until(currentStatementStructure, condition)

The body executes first. The loop repeats until the condition is true.

If the condition is false, control transfers to the beginning of the executable block closed by `until`. If the condition is true, the block completes and execution continues after the `until` line.

20.5.4. `for(init, condition, step)`

L2 `for` consumes:

    init      -> initialization Structure
    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    step      -> iteration-step statement Structure
    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `for` receiver

Vertical body form:

for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++
    print: i


Compact body form:

    for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++ print(i)

20.5.5. `return`

L2 `return` exits the current executable body.

Bare runtime form inside a body:

    return

Value runtime form inside a body:

    return: value

For an `fn`, `return: value` consumes an Lmx value admitted by the explicitly
requested `implements(value declaredReturn returnConsumers)` operation and
exits. The exact C return/status channel and graph location of the returned Lmx
value remain unresolved under sections 8.8 and 19.29.5; no hidden output
parameter or alternative Value category is implied. A bare `return` is not a
valid `fn` return in the core profile.

For a `sub`, `return` exits without producing a normal return value. `return: value` is invalid for `sub` unless a later profile defines status/result subroutines.

At a valid terminal trailer position for an open executable definition, an admitted return form may be terminal return. Terminal return appends the corresponding runtime return as the final body item and closes the executable definition, in the same checked close position where the executable's admitted `end:` target would otherwise be expected. For an `fn`, that target is the function name exposed by the `fn` receiver, such as `end: square`, and the terminal return form is `return: value`. The common compact position is the executable definition's own source level; a smaller source level may also be valid when the ordinary source-level cut reaches the visible executable tail and intervening frames admit that closure.

Example:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
return: x * x

20.5.6. `break` and `continue`

`break` exits the nearest active L2 loop frame.

`continue` starts the next iteration of the nearest active L2 loop frame.

For `while`, `continue` transfers control to the next condition check.

For `until`, `continue` transfers control to the post-condition check for the current iteration.

For `for`, `continue` transfers control to the iteration-step Structure, then to the next condition check.

20.5.7. `synchronized`

`synchronized` consumes an object/address expression and a statement Structure
owned by the `synchronized` receiver. It is a structured synchronization
receiver, not a raw lock/unlock pair in source syntax.

In the minimal L2 profile, `synchronized` has an object-expression plus
statement-tail receiver signature. The source after `synchronized:` is still the
ordinary P0 argument Structure.
Inline fields remain ordinary fields of that Structure. If an inline object
expression is followed by a child source level, that child part is materialized
as a nested Structure field; therefore:

    synchronized: @lock
        break

is structurally equivalent to:

    synchronized: @lock (break)

while:

    synchronized: @lock break

keeps `break` in the inline object-expression field sequence. A receiver may
then reject that expression semantically, but P0 must not silently reinterpret
the same-line word as a body. If the consumed body tail is a single Structure,
that Structure is consumed as its fields, as in the general
single-Structure-as-N-arguments rule.

Example:

synchronized: @object
    updateObject: object

The first ANSI C bootstrap projection may lower this to runtime hooks:

---ANSI C---
/* enter/leave are external synchronization boundaries in this profile.
   lm_resolve_* may remain a private traversal helper and is not, by itself,
   a semantic checkpoint. Exact outbound-call ordering follows section 21.8. */
Lmx *lock_handle = lm_resolve_lock_handle(containing_structure, /* object */);
lm_synchronized_enter(lock_handle);
{
    Lmx *callable = lm_resolve_callable(containing_structure, /* updateObject */);
    const LmMethodRecord *method = lm_method_record(callable);
    LmUpdateObjectEntry entry =
        (LmUpdateObjectEntry) method->addr; /* exact type selected by sig */
    entry(callable->node,
          object,
          /* dynamic values in method->sig order */);
}
lm_synchronized_leave(lock_handle);
---ANSI C---

The names `LmMethodRecord` and `LmUpdateObjectEntry` are illustrative backend
spellings. This is not a generic untyped `lm_invoke` ABI: validation has already
established the exact canonical signature for this Consumer call site. Before
the `entry` transfer, section 21.8 requires the callable, declared actual and all
dynamic/fallback values to be materialized, followed by the caller's dirty-only
checkpoint; the abbreviated C block does not redefine that order.

The runtime hook must implement the selected monitor policy, including
Java-style reentrant entry for the same thread when that profile is selected.

The first ANSI C bootstrap translator registers each active `synchronized`
monitor as a cleanup entry. Ordinary `return`, `break` and `continue` transfers
emit the required `lm_synchronized_leave(...)` calls before leaving the protected
region. A value-return inside `synchronized` may use implementation-only Lmx
reference/status temporaries selected by the still-open generic result ABI;
after the value/result roots are made safe for that ABI, active monitors are
released and control follows the selected return channel. This rule neither
defines a native per-source return type nor creates separate Primitive,
Structure and Array return categories.

Diagnostic failure across a synchronized boundary belongs to the wider
runtime/translator cleanup rules.

20.6. Outside the Minimal L2 Set

The following section 19 operators are not part of the minimal L2 control set
unless an L2 profile explicitly enables or redefines them:

    match / default-pattern receivers
    each
    finally
    catch / throw / throws
    retry / retryable
    assert
    redo
    yield
    labelled break / labelled continue

They may still exist in L3 or a richer L2 profile.

This subsection describes the first/minimal L2 bootstrap control subset, not a
level-order rule where L3 is wider than L2. L2 remains the wider low-level level
because it also owns the machine forms in sections 20.1 through 20.4; L3 never
exposes those forms.

21. Runtime Structure Resolution, Dynamic Visibility and C Activations

Revision 2 distinguishes source-semantic dynamic visibility from a reified
runtime Namespace object.

Dynamic visibility exists. A method may use a bare name that it does not declare,
and the current caller may supply the visible value for that name. The translator
computes this relation and lowers the selected values into ordinary typed C
arguments and automatic C working locals. The active C call chain is therefore
the physical runtime realization of dynamic visibility.

No additional Name-to-Binding dictionary, overlay stack, captured lexical
environment, descriptor map or namespace graph is created. Saying `no runtime
Namespace object` does not mean `no dynamic visibility`. It means that the
program's already-required C activations carry the values directly instead of
consulting another runtime name container.

21.1. The Only Runtime Node-to-Name Index

The only mandatory per-node auxiliary name mapping is:

    Lmx * -> ShortNameId

It is a reverse index from a concrete Lmx occurrence address to one short path
component. It does not answer a name with a value, store full paths, select a
type, describe a method signature, record lexical depth or track active calls.
Creating, copying, moving and destroying a named occurrence must update the
index consistently with node lifetime.

A full current path for a known occurrence is reconstructed by reading its short
name, enumerating the current parent's ordered children to compute its
zero-based ordinal among equal-name siblings, and following its `node` links
toward the root. If equal-name siblings exist, the textual path emits `[k]name`
for that occurrence; `[0]name` may be emitted explicitly although plain `name`
selects the same first occurrence. An
arbitrary textual absolute path is resolved only from an explicitly supplied
root: enumerate the current children at each segment, compare their ShortNameId
values and select the requested occurrence. A resolved Lmx pointer is followed
directly and never needs this textual lookup again.

Method ABI metadata is not part of this name index. It resides in the immutable
program-wide `all_methods_array` described in 21.8.

21.2. Reserved `node` and Explicit Structural Access

`node` is a reserved service word under every source spelling, including quoted
identifier spelling. It cannot be declared, shadowed, dynamically supplied,
rebound or used as an ordinary ShortNameId.

For a selected callable occurrence, the structural argument is fixed by that
occurrence:

    implicit_node = callable->node

The caller chooses the callable occurrence but does not replace this pointer
with a caller-scope value. The generated entry receives `implicit_node` as its
reserved `node` argument.

Consequently source `node\field` means exactly:

    result = child_named(implicit_node, ShortNameId(field), occurrence 0)

There is no additional `implicit_node->node` hop. It selects a directly
enumerated field of the Structure containing the callable occurrence.
Traversal farther through containment or another graph link must be written
explicitly. `node\field` never consults dynamic visibility and cannot be
overridden by a same-named caller value.

Likewise `reference\field` and `array[index]` operate on the explicitly selected
referent. A graph reference does not create a second containment parent; the
target's own `node` remains the reverse containment-tree link.

21.3. Bare Names and the Dynamic Caller Chain

For every method M, the translator computes `DynRequired(M)`: bare free names
used by M, plus transitive names required by statically known callees or the
expected signature at a runtime-selected callable site, excluding names
satisfied by M's own binding. Explicit structural paths are not dynamic needs.
The method signature records `DynRequired(M)` inputs in deterministic order.

For a required bare name `x`, a call establishes its value in this order:

    1. the caller's nearest current activation-local own binding for x, if
       present: a graph-backed OwnUsed field, declared formal/result, or another
       receiver-defined local;
    2. otherwise the x value dynamically inherited by that caller;
    3. otherwise the direct field node\[0]x of the selected callee node;
    4. otherwise the callable is incompatible with that call and validation
       must reject it (or the runtime admission boundary must fail if the
       target could not be known before execution).

Thus the nearest dynamic caller value overrides the callee's structural
fallback. The fallback is only the immediate supplied `node`; it is not a
lexical walk through `node->node` ancestors. A method that requires the
structural value regardless of its caller writes `node\x` explicitly. A caller
that requires a value to be supplied independently of dynamic visibility uses
an explicitly declared argument.

Dynamic needs propagate through direct and mutually recursive call graphs to a
fixed point. A method that does not read `x` itself but calls another method
requiring `x` must still retain and forward its current `x`: if M owns x, that
field enters `OwnUsed(M)`; otherwise x enters `DynRequired(M)`. At a callable
boundary the selected `sig` determines the exact required names and their
deterministic physical argument order. This is compiler/signature work, not a
runtime Name-to-Binding lookup.

An imported dynamic value is private to the receiving C activation. Rebinding or
assigning bare `x` changes only that activation's C working value and the value
it may pass farther down. It never writes back to the caller and never
automatically writes `node\x`. To change external graph state the method must use
an explicit path or explicit Message operation.

21.4. Value and Reference Passing

Primitive dynamic and declared arguments are passed by semantic value. The
callee receives an independent C value even when the source Lmx primitive is
physically represented by a pointer into a registered address range such as
`all_chars_array`. Sharing an immutable primitive atom is an implementation
detail; assignment in the callee never changes the caller's variable.

Structure and Array arguments are Java-style reference values. The reference is
copied into the callee's C activation; the referenced graph/storage is not
cloned. Rebinding the callee's local reference changes only that local reference.
An explicit `reference\field` write or `array[index]` write mutates the shared
referent and is visible through every reference/path to that same node or Array
storage inside the Message. Resizing or
other Array operations follow the same distinction: mutation through the
reference affects the referent; assigning the local reference to another Array
does not rebind the caller.

No raw Structure/Array pointer is passed `as is` between Messages. Message
delivery must use the specified clone, serialization or ownership-transfer
boundary so that distinct Messages do not share mutable arenas.

21.5. Own Fields as C Working Locals

A source method has one generated C entry and immutable method record. A
selected callable is an Lmx leaf occurrence whose `data` identifies that record;
its persistent containing Structure is `callable->node`. The graph may contain
several callable occurrences for the same method record, especially after
runtime merge, and their containing Structures may differ. No one of these
three entities is cloned into a new semantic activation object for each call.
At entry the generated C body loads only those own fields of the selected
containing Structure that this body actually uses through unqualified own
bindings into typed automatic C working locals. Explicit `node\field`,
`reference\field` and `array[index]` operations continue to read or mutate their
selected graph/referent location on every execution and are not converted into
the no-reload own-field cache. It also receives or constructs the dynamic values
required by 21.3.
These C values are the active method state between observable boundaries.

An own working local has graph backing in a directly selected field of
`callable->node`. Declared arguments and dynamic imported values are
activation-local inputs and have no automatic write-back target. Their
assignment/rebinding never mutates the caller. To publish one as method state the
program must assign an own field or use an explicit graph path. Generated C may
use further service temporaries for address
classification, child lookup, dirty state, bounds checks and outcome
propagation; those temporaries are not additional source entities.

At entry, each own working local records and roots the exact Lmx occurrence from
which it was loaded. Normal payload assignment uses that identity. If nested
code structurally moves, detaches or replaces it, the active reference still
keeps it alive, but the later publication destination follows the explicitly
selected active-backing conflict policy in 21.13; Revision 2 has not yet
chosen update-detached, checked re-resolution or rejection. No implementation
may turn that ambiguity into a silent `[0]` lookup or reinsertion.

`OwnUsed(M)` includes an own graph-backed binding read/written unqualified by M
or used to supply an outgoing callee's dynamic value. Only `OwnUsed(M)` fields
become backed/dirty C working locals. `DynRequired(M)` is the separate hidden-
parameter set from 21.3; a forwarded dynamic name never becomes an own graph
field merely because it is forwarded. Mentioning a field through an explicit
path also does not make it a cached own local. Other node fields are neither
loaded nor stored. This rule is both the first implementation and an intentional
optimization; it does not erase the corresponding fields from the graph.

Every binding classified as an own graph-backed field must resolve to its
declared direct occurrence before entry. A normal builder materializes that
field in the containing Structure before its callable can be invoked, and
runtime merge copies it with that Structure. If later structural mutation has
removed a required own field, invocation/admission fails; the translator or
runtime must not silently reclassify the missing own declaration as a dynamic
input or `node` fallback.

21.6. Dirty-Only Checkpoints

Every own graph-backed C working local has conceptual dirty state. It becomes
dirty only when the current activation actually executes an assignment or
rebind to that C local after its last successful checkpoint. Dirty is not
inferred by comparing the cached value with the graph, and it is not set merely
because some control-flow branch contains a possible assignment. A backend may
prove the state statically for a path or emit a runtime dirty flag. `++`, `--`,
compound updates and an operation that actually writes through granted access
count as writes. Merely making writable access available does not. An outlined
helper must report the executed write, or the generated lowering must otherwise
instrument it; a clean snapshot cannot become dirty from capability alone.

Immediately before every outbound Lingvamyxa call, and before every external,
re-entrant or publishing runtime/foreign boundary capable of exposing or
controlling graph state outside the current activation, invoking user code or
calling back into LMX, the generated code writes each dirty own local to its
corresponding node field. It does the same before every method exit,
including fallthrough, `return`, `throw`, failing `assert`, `stop` and
cancellation, and before `yield` suspension. After a successful write that local
becomes clean. Declared and dynamic input values are never included in this
automatic publication.

Dirty fields are considered in the forward source/child-occurrence order of
their backing fields in the selected containing Structure. The intended
outbound call or normal exit cannot proceed until all required dirty values have
been published successfully, and a successful store clears only its own dirty
state. Whether stores are guaranteed infallible, all-or-nothing after
prevalidation, or prefix-committing on failure remains a selected ABI/error
policy. That policy must define visible stores and dirty flags, report incomplete
publication without recursively retrying the same failing epilogue, and cannot
silently add a Namespace or rollback log.

Clean values must not be re-published. The distinction is observable after a
nested call:

    outer entry: C x = 1, clean
    outer calls inner
    inner explicitly writes outer's graph field x = 2
    outer returns without assigning its local x

A flush-all implementation would wrongly overwrite 2 with its stale cached 1.
The conforming dirty-only implementation writes nothing on outer return. If
outer later explicitly executes `x = 3`, x becomes dirty and the next checkpoint
publishes 3. Without an intervening call or explicit graph mutation, an
accidental store of an unchanged value may be unobservable, but this does not
permit flush-all at a general boundary.

There is no automatic reload after a call. The caller's unqualified local
continues with its C snapshot; explicit graph paths observe the current graph.
This is intentional. Checkpoint publication is not a continuously coherent
mirror between a suspended C frame and the graph.

A private, non-escaping helper used only for the current activation's own
traversal, address classification or explicit graph operation is not a semantic
boundary, even if that helper reads or writes the graph and regardless of
whether the backend inlines or outlines it. A call that may publish/escape
state, invoke user code, re-enter the Message or transfer control outside the
activation is a boundary; every uncertain external call is treated
conservatively as one. Direct tail recursion may be lowered to a loop and
eliminate redundant checkpoints only after proving identical visible effects.

21.7. C Stack, Recursion and Explicit History

The C call stack is the sole implicit history of method activations. Each direct,
mutual or callback recursion creates an ordinary distinct C activation with its
own argument values, dynamic values, working locals and dirty state. No
per-invocation Lmx call/body Structure, hidden activation node, closure
environment or global active-argument record is created.

The selected callable occurrence's containing Structure (`callable->node`) holds
the method's currently published working state; it is not a call journal.
Recursive calls through that occurrence may overwrite this published state. A
merged callable occurrence which identifies the same C method record but has a
different `node` publishes into its own containing Structure instead. The
suspended outer activation is not reloaded and retains its own C values. It can
later publish a value only if it actually modifies that local after its previous
checkpoint. This intentionally makes attempts to use the containing Structure
as an ever-growing recursive biography unsafe and visibly wrong.

Worked recursive example (state trace, not additional source syntax): M has
an own field `x` in the containing Structure S. Both calls select the same
callable occurrence and therefore the same S; `n` is a private declared
argument.

| Step | Outer C local | Inner C local | Published S.x |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Outer entry loads S.x = 1 | 1, clean | — | 1 |
| Outer assigns its own x = 2 | 2, dirty | — | 1 |
| Pre-call checkpoint, then M(0) enters | 2, clean | 2, clean | 2 |
| Inner assigns own x = 9 | 2, clean | 9, dirty | 2 |
| Inner returns through checkpoint | 2, clean | frame ends | 9 |
| Outer resumes without reload | 2, clean | — | 9 |
| Outer returns without assigning x | frame ends | — | 9 |

On the resumed outer frame, bare `x` reads 2 and explicit `node\x` reads 9.
If the outer instead executes `x = x + 1`, its own snapshot becomes 3 and
dirty; the next checkpoint publishes 3 into S. That later overwrite is an
actual outer assignment, not restoration of an activation snapshot. If x
were only a dynamically imported value, its assignment would remain private
and none of these own-field stores would occur. The published Structure holds
working state; the C stack is the sole implicit activation history.

A synchronous callback which re-enters LMX in the same Message is another nested
C activation in the same serial turn. Before control crosses that callback
boundary the caller materializes the callback's exact typed values and performs
the ordinary dirty-only checkpoint; return uses the ordinary no-reload rule. If
a foreign C API cannot carry those typed values directly, its L1 adapter may
retain a private call-adapter record for the duration of the synchronous call.
That record is backend state, not a source Namespace, and every retained Lmx
reference is an arena/collector root while it is live.

A callback which may run after the source activation has returned cannot retain
an implicit caller biography. Same-Message work is enqueued as a later FIFO turn
with explicit Structure/Message state; work for another Message crosses the
normal clone/serialization/transfer boundary. A foreign thread may not
concurrently re-enter one Message's mutable graph under the common L3 contract.
Such raw shared-memory callbacks require an explicit L2 synchronization profile.
Different Messages may concurrently execute callable occurrences that share the
same immutable method record because their containing Structures and arenas are
distinct.

Normative ontology statement:

    Стек вызовов является единственной неявной историей активаций. Structure
    метода содержит рабочее опубликованное состояние метода и не является
    журналом вызовов. Любая история, предназначенная для переживания возврата
    или разделения между ветвями, должна быть представлена явной Structure,
    Array или Message. Побочный эффект загрязнения биографии вызывающего, при
    неграмотно составленных рекурсивных вызовах, это съедающая память
    архитектурная ошибка, использование которой Lingvamyxa намеренно делает
    опасной и логически невозможной.

An English restatement is normative as well: history intended to survive a
return, to be inspected later or to diverge between recursive branches must be
explicit Structure, Array or Message data. The language does not silently turn
stack ancestry into persistent graph biography. A recursive dispatcher or
finite mutually recursive state machine is valid. Unbounded physical recursion
still requires a resource limit, an explicit failure, or semantics-preserving
loop/trampoline/Message lowering; no finite implementation promises an infinite
native stack.

21.8. Callable Records and Typed Invocation

Every callable Lmx occurrence has `data` in the callable address domain and
identifies one immutable record:

    all_methods_array[MethodId] = {
        addr,
        sig
    }

`addr` identifies the concrete globally reachable generated C entry. `sig`
identifies the complete source and physical invocation contract:

* ABI version and target calling convention;
* the reserved structural `node` parameter;
* each declared formal's canonical source name/position, required, optional or
  default mode, semantic value/reference contract, exact C ABI type and pass
  mode;
* each dynamic input's canonical ShortNameId/name identity, semantic copy mode,
  exact C ABI type and pass mode, in physical parameter order;
* the source result contract plus exact return/out-result convention;
* each declared throw identity/name, payload contract and propagation ABI; and
* either a varargs prohibition or the complete canonical varargs shape.

This information also determines the exact physical C function-pointer type
required by the selected target ABI. Two methods with the same C arity but
different named formals, dynamic names, source contracts, result or throw
contracts therefore do not have the same `sig`.

Dynamic entries use a deterministic canonical order selected by the signature
ABI version. A profile may use canonical ShortNameId/name order or another
link-stable algorithm; the choice is not source-visible, but all translation
units and adapters using that ABI must agree. The signature has a versioned
canonical linked identity. Every dynamic
ShortNameId is program-wide after relocation, or is accompanied by canonical
name bytes/a collision-checked stable hash. Two translation-unit-local numeric
intern IDs are never compared directly. The exact record layout remains a
backend ABI choice, but exact signature equality must be deterministic across
all linked modules.

The translator knows all native method records admitted to the current
translated/compiled program. At a call site it validates exact canonical
signature equality with the one known Consumer call type, converts `addr` to
that typed function pointer under the selected ABI and emits the complete
argument list:

    typed_entry(callable->node,
                declared_actuals...,
                dynamic_values_selected_by_sig...)

The normative evaluation/checkpoint order is:

    1. evaluate the callable occurrence exactly once and retain/root it;
    2. obtain its method record and validate exact expected `sig` equality;
    3. evaluate declared actuals exactly once in their specified source order
       into typed C temporaries;
    4. select/copy hidden dynamic actuals in `sig` order into typed C
       temporaries: current caller value first, otherwise the selected
       callable's immediate `node\[0]name` fallback;
    5. perform the caller's dirty-only checkpoint;
    6. call the exact typed `addr` with `callable->node` and the retained
       declared/dynamic values;
    7. continue without reloading the caller.

Any nested call reached while evaluating steps 1, 3 or 4 performs its own normal
checkpoint protocol. Generated C must materialize this order explicitly rather
than rely on C's unspecified argument-evaluation order. In particular, the
checkpoint may update graph state, but it cannot retroactively change the
already selected callable or fallback copy for this call.

Lingvamyxa intentionally selects this ABI contract; it does not require the
implementation to pretend that conversion between `void *` storage and a C
function pointer is portable ISO C. A backend may instead store a properly typed
pointer inside the immutable method record and use a generated typed adapter.
Both representations preserve the same Lmx semantics.

Code received through a future Message/module mechanism is not invoked as an
unknown C signature. It must first pass validation and then be translated and
compiled, or interpreted by a future compatible interpreter. Only then may its
callable record enter the admitted method domain.

After runtime merge, a copied callable occurrence has a rebased `node` and the
same immutable method record. Its machine body is reused, while its structural
fallback fields come from the newly containing Structure and its dynamic values
come from the current caller.

21.9. `implements(varA, varB, Consumer)` at Callable Leaves

The three-argument predicate remains directional and Consumer-relative: `varA`
is the candidate, `varB` is the required value/role and `Consumer` determines
the paths actually used. It never becomes a two-argument nominal type test.

For ordinary used paths it compares the actual current Lmx tree and primitive
address-domain contracts. When Consumer actually invokes a callable leaf at
used path `p`, that leaf's `all_methods_array.sig` is part of the used-tree
comparison. Validation must establish the exact equation

    sig(varA.p) == sig(varB.p) == ExpectedSig(Consumer, p)

at every concrete Consumer call site. Candidate and required addresses may
differ; signature positions,
pass modes, dynamic ShortNameIds and result/throw convention may not. An unused
callable field does not reject substitution. Once Consumer invokes that field,
however, its complete signature participates; there are no individually
ignorable signature positions.

This signature comparison is not a per-Structure descriptor and does not publish
visibility. It is a check over an explicitly used callable leaf and an immutable
method record. A successful check permits the call site to use one known typed
function-pointer conversion; it does not authorize calling an arbitrary
untyped address.

Rejected substitutions

    int: x 1
    x: "hello"

Reject this substitution when Consumer's remaining uses of `x` require a
primitive contract that the text value does not satisfy. Likewise:

    factorial: "hello"

Reject it when the remaining Consumer calls `factorial`: the replacement lacks
the expected callable signature. Unused fields and methods remain irrelevant.


21.10. `throw`, `retry` and `yield`

`return` and `throw` are method exits and therefore perform the ordinary
dirty-only checkpoint. Throw payloads and catch parameters remain explicit.
